# stackshot

stackshot is a coordination board for one person and the Claude sessions that work for them. Every session, whether a
Claude Code pane on one of your machines or a claude.ai or Cowork session in the cloud, reads and writes the same board
through one MCP connector. Each item belongs to a project page, and the board keeps a ledger of which session still owes
its page an update, so the pages stay the place a fresh session can pick up from. Optionally, anchor-hub watches external
things (Slack channels, Notion pages, Drive files, Gmail threads, GitHub issues) on behalf of a project and drops a
one-line notice into the sessions that care. It runs on one Linux box with systemd; other machines run a small client.

## The pieces

**The board and the publisher.** `server.py` holds the store (one JSON file per item, `<store>/<letter>/<n>.json`), is the
only writer and the only id allocator, and serves the rendered board at `/`. `publish.py` renders the board
(`render.py`: markdown and the one-line brief), commits the store to a git repository and pushes it, after every
write (debounced), on a timer and when the machine or session state changes. A publish that changes nothing commits
nothing.

**The connector.** The same server serves MCP at `/mcp`, behind Google OAuth (plus one optional machine token for a
Cloudflare MCP portal). Its tools:

| Tool | What it does |
|---|---|
| `brief` | The board at a glance: counts, the Machines panel, the receivers, the project index, then every open item in full. |
| `get` | Read one item by id; it shows `needs:` (whose move it is), `after:` (the items that must close first, each with its state), `observers:`; it leads with `mover:` and `then:`, shows `contact:` (the front agent the person talks to about the item; the item page's side column leads with it as "Owner"), and History says who created the item. |
| `create` | Create an item; `contact` names the front agent the person talks to about it (see **An item's contact**); the gates refuse a bad subject, description, project, enum or date. `request_id` makes a retry safe. A thing the owner does himself is not `needs=decision` (his choice between options) but a your-step: a needs=decision ask that only confirms his own action is refused (see `update`). `needs` is a list: `decision`, `external` and session names (a background agent of any project, or the mover itself), several at once; the sessions named hear the item's wake, recycle and after notices (the page's keeper when none is named); `observers` hear a close or a reopen; `after` names the items that must close first (`agent=` is the older way to name one session). The mover, when it is a user-facing agent (a background agent is refused), is not messaged about its item's moves (a front agent's pane wakes only for what he has to do; a move to needs=decision included, since the board's Waiting on you (now Need your call (agents)) view is where the owner reads it): a close, a reopen or a naming by another session goes to the project's keeper (its builder, else its `-main` agent), which passes on what needs the owner; the owner's own answer or comment from the board or GitHub still reaches it. A close with the owner's own words on the item is the other exception: a `said` entry, or his name before a quote or a weekday in the description or the close's reason (a board answer alone does not count, answering a decision picks an option, it does not ask to hear of the close); it is then a row on his For you list (see **The For you list**), not a message to the mover, whoever closed it (the router's `close-words` rule only rules a notice recorded before that as merged). A mover that is not a front agent hears a close, a reopen and its naming as before. `check` names the done check that closes the item once work handed to another session is done (below); a create is never refused for a hand-off. A sender off the project's agents map gets a note at the end of the reply naming its -main agent (**A session off its project's map**). |
| `update` | Change the fields passed; `contact` changes the item's contact; `sender` charges the page debt to the editor rather than the item's mover. A needs=decision item carries an ask line (`ask`: `Decide: … · Recommend: … · Stakes: … · By: …`, one line, at most 240 characters). One that asks for a pick between alternatives lists them (`options`: 2–6 entries `{label, line, recommended}`, exactly one recommended), shown on the item page, in the queue's tooltip and in board.md; an ask or description that names alternatives while none are listed is refused (keywords, a list "x, y or z" or "x vs y", and labeled choices in the Decide part such as "A or B" or "A (…) or B (…)"). A yes/no decision lists two options, `Approve` and `Decline` (the recommended one marked), so the owner gets one-click buttons: the page draws Approve and Decline only when they are listed. An open question (a Decide part that opens with what, which, how, who, whom, whose, when, where or why) lists its answers as options too, or is reworded as a yes/no (`Decide: whether to …`): with no options it is refused, since the page's Yes / No fallback cannot answer it. The ask's Recommend part names the recommended option's label; when it does not, the reply carries a note and the write goes through. A needs=decision item whose description or `next` links a mockup (a `https://claude.ai/artifact/…` page) while its ask does not is refused: put `[mockup](<that link>)` in the ask's Decide part, and the home row and the item page draw `Mockup ↗` beside the question (not twice when the ask's own link is already drawn there). A mockup link goes in the ask only: an option is a clickable card that selects or unselects, so an option whose label or line holds a markdown link or an `http(s)` URL is refused, on `create` and on `update`. A needs=decision item whose ask only confirms the owner's own action is refused, since his Yes would be read as consent and not as the step done: an ask that asks whether he has done it ("have you done X?", a bare "is it done?") with no alternative or judgement in it, or Yes/No, Approve/Decline or no options over a Decide part that names the action of one or more `<owner>:` lines of `next`. Use `step=<his action>` instead (on a create, create without `needs=decision` and set it by `update`), which the board ticks when he says it is done. A choice between options, an open question ("which", "should we", "or") and a judgement ("have you decided …") stay decisions; in doubt the write is accepted. `work` adds or moves one status line of the item's subtasks per call (`{text, state, agent}`: a few words, at most 40 characters, at most 8 open lines; `defined` → `assigned` → `in progress` → `done`, `remove: true` drops one), by the mover or a session named in `needs`: the board row shows the lines in progress as "engineer: migrating", the item page the full list, and each change is one line in History; it owes no page and changes no needs. `next` replaces only the sender's own lines (every line of `next` remembers the session whose update wrote it, kept in `metadata.next_by`; `nextlines.py`; a line stored before an earlier change is the creator's): `next="none"` removes only the sender's lines, `clear_next=true` wipes all. `done` and `dropped` mark steps of the next checklist (a 1-based position or the exact first line of an open step, any step whoever wrote it); a `next` or `next="none"` that drops one of the sender's own open steps without marking it is refused, and `clear_next=true` marks every line it wipes as dropped. `check` sets or clears (`"none"`) the item's done check; an update that hands the work to another session (a line of its `next` in the form `<session>: <action>`, naming a session other than the mover and the writer that is not already in `needs`) is refused unless the item carries a check (or, when none fits, a wake, a reminder; never a hold). `wake` (a date, or a date with a time) alone is a reminder, never a hold: the item stays in its lane, and at that moment the sessions its notices go to (the sessions in its `needs`, else its mover) are told once (one message per session per pass) and the wake is dropped; status, `needs`, `next` and the state clock do not change. Only `hold=true` parks an item, and with a `wake` it is released at that moment, as before; a hold and a wake stored before this change release the same way. The server records who set a hold and when (`metadata.hold_set`). `client.py holds` (read only) lists the open items on hold that nothing holds now (no open `after` item, pane recycle, `needs=external` or item-scoped anchor; a stored reason is never trusted), with mover, wake, setter and time, for the item's mover to release one by one (`update clear_hold=true`); the board.md head, the `brief` tool's head and the session-start brief list the holds set in the last 24 hours and what holds each. No session text suggests parking an item on a date the session picked. `wait_reason` names what a step the mover waits on (its own wake, or a dated line of its own `next`) waits for: required with such a write, judged once by a model call that refuses when the step could be done now (`[wait_guard]`, below). An item whose `needs` include `decision` is never shown on hold (the board, board.md, the item page, the brief, the staleness lines and the GitHub mirror ignore a stored flag on it); its next update drops the flag, keeps the wake as a reminder, and says so in the reply. A sender off the project's agents map gets a note at the end of the reply naming its -main agent (**A session off its project's map**). |
| `confirm` | Mark an item still true: it reads fresh again. Sets its `confirmed` clock only: no page owed, nobody told. |
| `complete` | Mark an item completed (items are never deleted) and list anchors left with no open item. A close takes `outcome` (done, dropped or superseded), a one-line `reason`, and `outcome_text` (how it ended, as `- ` bullets, shown as the Outcome block above Background); the description stays as it was. |
| `said` (on `create`, `update` and `complete`) | The owner's own words on the item, under the Linux kernel's tags: `[{tag: Acked-by (his decision) \| Suggested-by (his idea or request) \| Reported-by (a problem he reported), quote: his sentences cut verbatim, msg: the uuid of his message, session: where he said it (default: the sender)}]`. The quote is checked against that message in the session's transcript on the server box (`[said] transcripts`); a readable transcript that fails (no such message, not his own, text a tool typed in, the quote not there) refuses the write, one the box cannot read stores the entry unchecked. Stored as `metadata.said`, appended; the issue comment ends with `Acked-by: <owner> <link>` (` (unchecked)` when so), never the quote, and is never bundled; the item page shows a tag on the History line with the words behind it. His questions are not posted: only conclusions go to the board. A message he sends while a session is working (a reply he got elsewhere, pasted in) is recorded in the transcript as an attachment entry of type `queued_command` with `attachment.origin.kind` human, inside the session's turn, not as a typed message, and is refused as his words; `{kind: acted, quote: a piece of its text verbatim, msg: the uuid of the attachment entry, session}` (no tag) records it instead: checked to be a `queued_command` attachment of human origin with the quote in its `attachment.prompt`, wherever it sits in the transcript (every other attachment type, a `queued_command` from a peer, a task notification or with no origin, and one whose text a tool typed in (the same marker, prefix and phrase test as his typed words), is refused), stored with the tag `Done-by` and `kind: acted`, and read only by the audit's N4, where a checked acted entry proves the owner's ticked steps on the item within 24 hours after its date, never where his words or his decision are needed. |
| `anchors` | List, add or remove a project's anchors, project-wide or scoped to some of its items. The page that holds an anchor keeps it (its keeper, its routing log and its receivers: its keeper, or on a page with no keeper its session and the movers of every open item it is scoped to, of any project), but an item reads the anchors scoped to it from every project's page, not only its own project's, each once (`anchor_scope.py`, the one reader the server, the board pages, the briefings and the model guards share); a project-wide anchor counts for no item. |
| `route` | A ruling on a notice delivered for a project: passed, merged, held or dropped, with a reason. The router rules the notices of every project itself; this overrules a ruling that was wrong. |
| `message` | Send a message to another session (a pane on any machine, or a cloud session), labelled as a teammate's request. A message to a front agent (a session the owner talks to) must carry `[for <owner>]` on its first line, and the mark is for what the owner has to do: a build hand-back, a result, a screenshot, "it's live" or an FYI goes to the project (`to=projects/<slug>`), where the router rules it and passes on what needs the agent. A marked message that says nothing is needed from him is refused too, by a short fixed list of phrases (`_NOT_FOR_OWNER` in `server.py`: "nothing needed from <owner>", "nothing for <owner>", "no action needed", "no action for <owner>", "no decision needed", "nothing to decide", "FYI", "for your information", "for the record", "no reply needed"; whole words, any case, in the text after the mark); the refusal names the phrase and where the message goes instead. **A background agent's plain status report is written onto the item, not sent** (a front agent is woken by a message only when it has something to do or to tell him; `reports.py`, `server._builder_report`). A builder may message a front agent only to ask it something, tell it something it must know or give it something to do; a plain result goes on the item. The rule is turned around: a list of ask words always misses some, so only what clearly reads as a report is held back and everything else is delivered. A marked message from a background agent to a front agent (or a project message that opens `For <front agent>`) that names one to three board items as plain-word ids is saved as the server's note on each item (a History line from the sender, no field and no clock moves) and delivered to nobody only when `reports.report_doubt` finds nothing against it: every sentence, once the mark, the `For <agent>,` address and the ids (items, PRs, shas, numbers) are taken out, says done, live, merged, released, deployed, closed, opened, fixed, passes, green, recorded, written, pushed, landed, unchanged, "is up" or "no change"; it has no `?` anywhere (a URL's included), no fenced block and no prose in a code span (an id, sha, path or short command is fine); none of fail, broke, error, rolled back, cost, `$`, blocked, wait, await, pending, need, must, should, require, review, ready, approve, decide, confirm, please, ask, option, recommend or a word of `[keywords.reports] ask_words` (and a few more, `TROUBLE` in `reports.py`); it names no person or role to act (the owner or one of his aliases, you, your, owner, keeper, a front agent's name); and it states no plan (will, once, when, if, then, until, after …). The reply begins "Not delivered: written onto <item> as a History line from <sender>, because …". Anything not matched is delivered as before, and so is a message whose item has `decision` in its needs or names the target front agent in its needs, one that names no item or more than three, one from a session that is not a background agent, one to a background agent and any failure in the check. From a message to a project page, a sender off the project's agents map gets a note at the end of the reply naming its -main agent (**A session off its project's map**). |
| `sessions` | The live sessions and machines, with each session's link, for a session that cannot see them itself. |
| `rules` | Serve the rulebook (`paths.rules`) every session is told to read at the start of a task. |
| `bump_versions` | Refresh the latest-version lookups and republish now. |

**A new tool or parameter** (`work` on `update`, say) reaches a running session's cached tool list only after about a day:
claude.ai keeps its own copy of a connector's tools, and a pane recycle does not refresh it. Until then a session calls the
new form through the MCP Portal's Code Mode, which reads the live schema: `portal_codemode_execute` with
`async () => codemode.stackshot_update({ item_id: "board#N", sender: "<your session>", work: { text: "migrating the ids", state: "in progress", agent: "my-engineer" } })`.

**The page rule and the client hooks.** Every item names a project page (`projects/<slug>` or `infra/<slug>`) in a
directory of markdown files with frontmatter. A state-changing write (a create, a complete, or an update of
description, next, needs, status or due) records a debt against the writing session. The debt settles by itself once
the page is newer than the write. On each machine, `pages.py` runs as four Claude Code hooks: `touch` (PostToolUse on
the connector's write tools) remembers the write, `nag` (UserPromptSubmit) reminds the session at every prompt while the
page is owed, `check` (Stop) runs the board check below, and `keeper` (SessionStart) shows a page's keeper the current
state of its pages. The Stop hook asks nothing about pages: the end-of-turn page question (`Page: no change, <reason>`)
was retired, and with it `[page_gate] mode` and `block_after`. The hook scripts live
in this repository under `client/hooks/`: `project-page.sh <verb>` forwards to `pages.py`, and `session-start.sh` prints
the board's brief and then the keeper's pages. `./stackshot client install` wires the four of them into
`~/.claude/settings.json`. A machine with no puller uses three more hook verbs of `client.py`: `register`, `seen` and
`drain`.

**The board check at the end of a turn.** With `[board_check] mode = "block"`, `check` (Stop) also asks about the board.
A turn that did real work (a board or other connector write, a message to another session, or a shell command that
commits, pushes, opens or merges a PR, or runs `deploy.sh` or `release.sh`) while its session owns open items it neither
updated nor confirmed is asked once to update them, `confirm` them, or say there is no change. The answer can be in the
turn's own final message: `Board: no change, <reason>` covers every such item, `Board <id>: no change, <reason>` one
item, a bare `Board:` line followed by one bullet per item (`- <id>: no change, <reason>`) several, and the reason is at
least three words (the same line in the instance's own language, set under `[keywords.pages]` `board_label` and `no_change`, reads the same). Items on hold are never asked
about. The hook gets the session's open items with one read of the client API (`items?key=<session>`), only on a turn
that wrote something; when the server does not answer, nothing is asked. With `mode = "note"` the same question does not
hold the turn: `check` keeps it in the session's state and `nag` says it at the next prompt, which saves the extra model
request a block costs. The page debt is said at every prompt while it is owed, whatever the board check's mode.

**The daily board audit.** With `[audit] enabled = true` the board is read once a day and what was found is reported
(`audit.py`, `audit_run.py`). `[audit] mode` says who reads it. **`"script"`** (the default) runs a script: the
field checks run in code with no model call (an ask missing, the next-line rules and the unmoved-flag escalation). Five checks that read item fields only are not made here but by the ten-minute passes, described below: stuck-elsewhere, the board checks' arbitrary-hold and mislabeled-state, an item whose own wake has passed, and an item on an agent that has had no activity for 45 minutes (the silence pass, which replaces the two silence rules this run had); a cheap heuristic then picks suspects for the judgment checks (a step already
done, wrong needs, an ask that no longer fits, a needless escalation, a project page newer than the item), and each suspect
gets exactly ONE `claude -p` call (the `[router]` command, run on the `[audit] lane` when that names one) with the fixed rubric in `audit_rubric.txt` as its system prompt,
answering `{finding, check, reason, fix}`. At most `[audit] max_calls` calls a run (default 40), one after the other, never
parallel; three failed calls in a row end the calls; an item judged is not judged again until it is written. Findings go to
the item's mover, one message per session, only when the mover is a background agent; a finding on an item a front agent, a retired session or an outside party moves goes to the project's page key
(`projects/<slug>`, unmarked), where the router hands it on, and never straight to a front agent. The marked paths are two: a finding on an item that is the owner's move goes to the project's -main agent
with `[for <owner>]` first, and a finding that went to a builder and stayed unanswered through its reminders and the builder-level send goes once more to -main, marked for its owner (the second level of the ten-minute passes' chain, below). A summary goes to `[audit] summary_to` (default `projects/example`, the stand-in page). A finding already sent for an item, with the item not written since, is not sent again (a write after the run read the item counts, one made while the run judged included, except those its own deliveries make: the record keeps `sending_from`, the time just before its first send; the record keeps it with its `sent` time, and the
summary counts it as not sent again); an item written since, or a finding that went away and came back, is sent anew. The unmoved digest (a finding of the earlier run whose item has had no write
since, chased to the project's builder) names an item once, in the first run that finds it unmoved; the record's `stuck` list and the summary keep naming it. A stuck-elsewhere finding (the ten-minute filing pass's; the first open step opens with someone other than the mover) reads a waiting
item against the session that takes it when the wait ends, so a parked, held or outside item whose step is that session's,
or the owner's while the wait is on him, is no finding, and neither is a session's step when the owner is the post-wake mover
(a session chases, then the owner acts). While a wait is on (a hold with its wake ahead or none, or any waiting item whose wait has not ended) nothing is stuck, whoever the first step names, and the owner's step dated for
after the item's wake is not the step in hand. On an alive item the owner moves, a session's first step is the one for after his answer or step, so it is no finding when that session is the item's `then` or the writer named no `then`; it still is beside a `then` that is another session. A message the tool refuses because its recipient is a front
agent goes to the project's page key unmarked, where the router hands it on; any other failure goes to the project's -main
agent. When a run finds `[audit] threshold` findings (default 5) of one check, or flags more than a fifth of
the open items, ONE message goes to `[audit] builder` naming the check and the item ids (the tightening review). The
shared "who moves this item, and is that session idle, for how long" lives in `mover_state.py` (the mover) and `liveness.py` (the pane), used by the stall pass, the audit and the
silence pass alike. `python audit.py run --dry-run` lists the suspects and makes no call, no
message and no record. **`"agent"`** is the earlier way and the rollback: an agent session (`[audit] agent`) reads the board and reports; the
server only cues it and reads what it wrote. A cue is one line pushed to the agent through `push.py`, as a wake notice goes.
Either way the run starts from `stackshot-audit.timer` every day at each time of `[audit] at` (one "HH:MM" or a list, such as `["05:00", "20:00"]`) in the instance timezone
(`audit.py cue scheduled`), or from the board's "Audit now" link, `POST /audit/now` (script mode starts the run detached and answers at once). The answer is
202 (started or cued), 409 (a run is in progress), 404 (the audit is off) or 503 (the run could not start, or in agent mode the agent is not live, with `push.py`'s answer);
every cue is logged to `<paths.state>/audit/cues.jsonl`. The board's side panel draws the audit in its Audit section as a "Daily audit" card (last audit, findings, next) with the Audit now text link beside the card's name (a real button drawn as a link: accent text, underline on hover and keyboard focus, grey while a run is on, a 44 px tap area on a touch screen; the name row stays one line, as high as the other cards' name rows), and after it a "GitHub calls" card with the hour's writes and reads against their caps and the 60-minute window they were counted over. The Runners section above it is one line a machine, in the Machines panel's order, `busy/online` summed over the repos (a machine whose CI switch is off and has no job left reads `disabled`), linking to `/runners`. The run writes one record,
`<paths.state>/audit/run-<YYYY-MM-DD>-<HHMMSS>.json`, the start time in the instance timezone, so a second run the same day keeps the first one's record (readers take the newest; the plain `run-<YYYY-MM-DD>.json` the audit agent writes is read as before; the script adds `judged`, `calls`, `messages`, `idle_pass`, `mode` and, on a crash, `error`): `started`, `finished` (null while it runs; each an epoch number or an ISO-8601 string such as `2099-03-28T05:10+00:00`, and `GET /audit/status` gives them as epoch seconds), `items_read`, `findings`
(`{id, project, owner, category}` each), `by_session` and `by_category` (counts). An unfinished record two hours old or
more is a crashed run and holds no cue back. `GET /audit/status` gives the board its Audit card (last audit, findings, next): enabled, the
agent, whether a run is in progress and since when, the last run in brief and the next scheduled one. Both routes sit
behind the board's own wall, like the board itself. A script run that dies leaves a finished record with `error` set and a line in `cues.jsonl`, never an open record that holds the next cue back; the detached run of the button
writes its output to `<paths.state>/audit/run.log`. A run is claimed under an exclusive lock (`cue.lock` in the same folder), so two cues at once get one 202 and one 409, and `audit.py run` (without `--dry-run`) is refused with a line and exit 1 while a run is in progress, when `[audit] enabled` is false or when the mode is
`"agent"`. `[audit] max_minutes` (default 100) stops the run from beginning new model calls late. **The orphan hand-over** (script mode, the run's first stage, before the board is read; `audit_run.orphan_stage` asks the server, `POST /client/orphan-handover`, which holds the item lock and the fleet's heartbeat; the pure half is `successor.py`) finds every open item whose mover (an alive item) or post-wake mover (a waiting one) is a session that is gone, the last agent of a retired project included, and hands it to the closest live agent. An item with a mover goes first to a live background agent of its project (the builder, then the others), because the minute pass sends a front agent no notice and never chases one; a front agent (the project's own, then the parent pages', the hub page's, last the scope's general `-main` agent, `[router] general_<scope>`) takes it only when no background agent is live, and then the History line says it is a front agent. A waiting item's post-wake mover keeps the closeness order (the project's `-main` first). A held item (`hold` set) waits on its wake and is not moved; a parked item whose post-wake mover is gone is. A name that is no session (the server's label, a script's) is never read as gone. The write is the minute hand-off pass's own (`server._handoff_write_move`: the new mover heads `needs`, an alive item gets the hand-off record, the new mover is told), and the item's History carries a line with the reason (`handed to X: Y is gone (...); closest live agent: ...`). An item the owner moves or waits on is never touched; an item with no live successor stays and is listed in the summary and on the record (`orphans.left`); a second call finds nothing left to do. `[audit] orphan_handover`: `"report"` (the default: the run lists what it would move and moves nothing), `"apply"` or `"off"`. To switch on the hand-over, set `orphan_handover = "apply"` under `[audit]` in the instance config and reload; `audit.py orphans --apply` moves only in that mode. At most 40 moves a run. The check stands still on a partial view of the fleet: an empty heartbeat; a mover on a machine whose `machine.json` cannot be read, or one with no row while a machine's session list is empty or unreadable, is not read as gone; and more orphans than 10, or than a quarter of the open items, is reported with the note that a partial view is likelier than that many gone sessions, and nothing is moved (`orphans.ceiling`). `--dry-run` lists what would move and writes nothing; `audit.py orphans [--apply]` makes the same call by hand. **The proof-waits stage** (script mode, `audit_run.proof_stage` over `audit_checks.proof_wait_findings`; code only, no model call, no write) runs after the judgment calls and before the pages stage. An item held open for a real event closes if the fix's tests passed and it is live; otherwise its mover is told to stage the event now (a downgrade, a probe, a dry run) and to close on the result. A candidate is an open waiting item (held, parked, or waiting on a time or a fleet event) that is an event wait for something the fleet can run on its own (`recycle`, `fleet_op`, `job_run`) or whose own words (the wait basis, the expected and watch reasons, next and the open steps) say it is kept open to see the fix happen for real; a wait on a pull request's merge or release is the release path's and no candidate, and neither is a line that names a pull request still to merge or be released. The fix counts as tested and live when the item carries a release notice (`metadata.released_told` holds a bare `owner/repo#n`: main's full run passed on that merge and the merge is in the live checkout; a merge-only or dead stamp is not one): the finding then says to close the item with that evidence, else to stage the event now (a job run, a downgrade, a probe, a dry run) and, if it cannot be staged, to write why as the wait's basis; a recycle or a fleet op needs the owner's go, and that is the basis when staging it needs his go. A wait that really cannot be staged keeps its basis named and is left alone, listed on the record's `proof_waits.left` with the basis: an outside party (needs external), news only the owner relays, an anchor event, another item's close, a real-world date (the wait basis) or the owner's recorded words for a date, a release still to come (an open step with its proof), a post-wake mover who is the owner or an outside party. The finding (category `proof-wait`) goes to the item's mover through the same `deliver_findings` with the same send stamps as every other finding (once per unchanged item; when no mover can be reached it falls back like the other findings), and the audit closes nothing. An item whose move is the owner's or an outside party's is left alone. At most `audit_checks.PROOF_MAX` (10) fresh findings are sent per run; the rest are counted in `proof_waits.capped`, named in the summary line and go out on the next run (findings sent before take no place). One item whose metadata cannot be read is skipped and counted (`proof_waits.skipped`). A stage that fails is a stage not made: the record's `proof_waits.failed` and the summary line say so and the run goes on. `audit.py run --dry-run` lists the findings and the items left alone. Suite: `tests/proof_waits_scenario.py`. **The pages stage** (script mode, `audit_run.pages_stage`) makes the page-debt call (see **The page-debt call**) once per run after the judgment calls, under the same limit; its counts are on the record's `pages` and in the summary line, and a failed page or a failed stage does not stop the run. **The pattern review** (script mode, `audit_run.pattern_stage` over `audit_checks.group_patterns`, `pick_patterns` and `parse_drafts`; `[audit] pattern_review`, `"shadow"` by default, `"off"` for nothing) is the last stage of each model run, after the pages stage and before the run's messages. Findings that repeat are a sign the flag is not the fix, so it reads every audit's findings since the review before it, groups the repeats by kind (the category; for `next rules` and `board checks` the rule or kind inside it), agent (the mover) and project, and makes ONE model call that drafts one guardrail for each pattern: a hook, a mod, a tool's text, a rulebook line, or a new audit or an edit to one (`audit_checks.PATTERN_BRIEF` is the system prompt; the call runs on the `[audit] lane` and `[audit] model` as the judgment calls do). **What it reads:** the findings log, `<paths.state>/audit/findings-log.jsonl`, one row a line (`at`, `source`, `id`, `kind`, `agent`, `project`, `detail`). Each audit pass (`write_pass_record`, shared by the filing, dates and silence passes) appends a finding the first time its record shows it, and again if it ended and came back; the run appends its own fresh findings after the review has read them. The window opens where the review before it finished reading (the previous record's `pattern_review.at`); when that review failed, was cut short or left patterns to the cap (it has no `at`), the window opens at the last review that did finish reading, found by walking the run records back, so nothing is lost however many such reviews follow one another (the dedupe keeps a pattern from being drafted twice); with no record it is the last 24 hours, with records but no finished review 48, and never more than 48. A log line that is no finding's appearance (wrong shape or type) is skipped. Appending to the log is one write on an append descriptor, so a batch of any size goes in whole beside another writer; the run's upkeep of the log (its own findings, the 14-day prune) does not depend on the review succeeding. Rows older than 14 days are dropped by the run. **A pattern** is at least 3 appearances on at least 2 different items (`PATTERN_MIN`, `PATTERN_MIN_ITEMS`); one item flagged over and over is its mover's business, not a pattern. **Dedupe:** a pattern (kind, agent and project) drafted in the last 7 days (`PATTERN_DEDUPE_DAYS`; the drafts are on the earlier records) is not drafted again; **cap:** at most `PATTERN_MAX` (5) patterns are asked about per run, the largest first, the rest wait for the next run. **Budget:** the call begins only inside `[audit] max_minutes` and takes one of the `[audit] max_calls` (the judgment calls keep `max_calls` minus one; a cap of 1 gives it to the judgment calls, a cap of 0 leaves none, and the record says so). **Shadow** (the default, and the only mode built): the drafts are only logged. The record's `pattern_review` holds `mode`, `at`, `since`, `read`, `groups`, `patterns`, `deduped`, `capped`, `called`, `failed`, `timed_out`, `note` and `drafts` (`key`, `kind`, `agent`, `project`, `count`, `items`, `guardrail`, `route`, `draft`, `why`, `at`); the summary line says how many patterns were found and drafted (`pattern review (shadow): 2 pattern(s) in 14 finding(s), 2 drafted (hook, rulebook)`), with one line under it per draft naming its guardrail, where it would go and its key (the text of a draft stays on the record); nothing is sent to any agent, no board item is opened, and nothing is edited. `route` says where a draft would go once the review goes live: `code` (a hook, a mod, a tool's text or an audit: a board item for `[audit] builder`), `rulebook` (a message to the session that holds the rulebook's rule command, never an edit), or `owner` (it changes what the owner sees or receives, or what a rule means: a board decision item with options and a recommendation). Going live needs a way to open board items from the audit script that does not exist (opening one is a server route), so it is a later change and a decision for the owner; until then the key has two values. The review never edits code, config, the rulebook or an audit, and writes no memory file. A stage that fails (a call that fails or whose answer cannot be read, an error in the stage) is recorded as failed (`failed`, `note`) and the run goes on; the audit's own writes (the log is no item write) are not activity. `audit.py run --dry-run` groups and picks from the log and says how many patterns it would ask about, with no call. Suite: `tests/pattern_review_scenario.py`. A finding whose message to its mover fails goes to the project's `-main` agent, noted on the record's `messages`. `install.sh units` installs and starts the timer only while the
audit is enabled, and disables and removes a timer it installed earlier once it is not.

**Two runs a day.** With two times in `[audit] at` each day has two records (`run-<date>-<HHMMSS>.json`; neither overwrites the other and no cue is refused for having run today; only a run in progress refuses one). `GET /audit/status` reports the newest record as `last` and the soonest time ahead as `next`.
A finding already sent is not sent again until its item is written, across runs as within one, so the second run sends what the first did not. The unmoved digest compares a run with the one before it (the last one that started at least six hours earlier, which the example's two scheduled runs, 06:00 and 18:00, 12 hours apart, always are), so an item flagged in the morning and still unwritten in the evening is chased then, once. The tightening history counts days, taking the newest record of each earlier day, so "3 days running" still means three days. A run that is not the first of its date names itself with its start time in its messages and summary (`Board audit 2030-01-15 18:00`; the first run of a date says only the date, so a once-a-day schedule shows only the date unless another run, a button press say, happened earlier that day). The threshold message and the page agent lines message are not deduplicated across runs: each goes out on every run that finds something, so twice a day, under headings that differ by the run's time (the threshold message is retired by the audit redesign plan). The rulebook scan (`tools/rulebook_scan.py`, no model) is the unit's `ExecStartPost`, so it runs after each run and its page checks and not before, and the run never reads its result; a note already sent and still true is not sent again.

**The page agent lines check** (script mode only; `tools/page_agent_report.py`, `audit.py page-agents [--line|--json]`, run by `audit_run.py` after the item findings). Sessions get renamed and retired and a project page does not follow on its own, so a page can go on naming a session sala no longer knows. Each daily run lists those lines and sends them, one message per agent, to the agent that keeps the page: the page's own -main agent, else the top-level page's, the hub's and the scope's general one, the first of them that sala knows, else straight to the audit's -main agent (never through the router's fallback, `anchors.fallback_main`, which walks the same candidates without asking sala and can answer the very name that is gone; a message to a gone name queues silently). A front agent's message starts with `[for <owner>]`, the audit's -main agent's too. The message is not an fyi: it asks the agent to fix the line or retire the name. A name is a leftover when it is on an `agents:` map (user-facing or `background:`) or a sub-page's `agent:` line and is neither a live sala session nor an alias of one, or when a page's `display-name:` is no live session and no live session's own name starts with `<display-name>-` (an alias does not excuse a display-name: the old name of a session outliving its project's name is the case this catches). A page that is retired, done or moved is skipped, and so is a line that holds an id and no name. The live sessions are read by `anchors.sala_sessions` (the code behind `anchors.sala_live`); when sala's list cannot be read (no fleet store, no sala, a timeout, a failing call) or is empty, nothing is flagged and the run's summary says "page agent lines not checked (the reason)", never every name as stale. A list cut short but not empty is refused the same way: when 10 or more of the distinct names read are unknown and they are more than a quarter of all the distinct names read (`UNKNOWN_MIN`, `UNKNOWN_SHARE` in `tools/page_agent_report.py`), nothing is flagged and the note says the list looks partial (a real run flags a few names of a few hundred). An agent written on a page before its session is spawned is flagged until the spawn: the page is the one to hold back, or the line waits for the spawn. The lines are not item findings: they are kept on the run record under `page_agents` (`checked`, `note`, `sessions`, `pages`, `findings` of `{page, line, name, to}`), logged in `messages` with `kind: "page-agents"`, counted in the summary ("page agent lines N") and left out of `by_category` and the tightening review. They repeat each day until the line is fixed; a failed delivery is not retried before the next run. `python audit.py run --dry-run` prints them as `[page-agents]` lines and sends nothing. Suite: `tests/page_agent_report_scenario.py` (the check, its CLI and routing) and section 26 of `tests/audit_run_scenario.py` (the run).

**A project page with items and no agents** is the page agent lines check's third kind of line, and `rename_page.py` has the same check at a move. A page that is `type: project`, no sub-page (a key under another page of the registry, or a `hub:` line; a `parent:` line makes none) and not retired, done or moved, with open items (or items touched in the last 30 days) and no front agent on its `agents:` map, has items with no contact and no one to take its notices: the daily run sends `<page> · no agents map · N open items · <the fix>` (`no front agent` when the map lists background agents only) the way it sends the other lines, to the page's own -main agent, else the fallbacks, else the audit's -main agent, and repeats it each day until the map is added. A one-session page (a `display-name:` line and no agents map) is a page by design and is not flagged while that name line names a live sala session of its own prefix (a live session named so, or one that starts with `<name>-` and does not belong, by the longest project name it starts with, to another top-level page); a name that is, or starts with, the page's own slug is the page's own next to a shorter project (`foo-bar` beside `foo`); it is flagged when no live session answers to the name line, and also when its only session is named for another project (the name starts with that project's name, not the page's own: `<page> · other project's session · <session>`), however live that session is. The rule is one function, `projects.agent_gap`, used by both. `rename_page.py` makes its check only where the page registry is readable: with an empty or missing registry it prints one note and skips it (a would-be sub-page cannot be told from a project), and a page nested only by `parent:` still needs an agents map or `--no-agents-ok`. If the board items cannot be read the check says so on stderr and makes no such line. `rename_page.py plan|copy` print a WARN when the page at the new slug is such a project page, and `stub` refuses (FAIL, exit 1, nothing changed) until the destination page lists a front agent, its -main agent, or `--no-agents-ok` says the project has none on purpose; a move to a sub-page, a note, a retired project, a one-session page with a live session of its own or a page that lists a front agent is not touched. Suites: `tests/rename_scenario.py`, `tests/page_agent_report_scenario.py` and `tests/audit_run_scenario.py`.

**A session off its project's map** (`anchors.off_map`, one rule for a write's note and the audit's line). A session is off the map of a project when no page of the project's hub lists it (`anchors.session_hubs`: the page, its parent page and the `hub:` page; `agents:` entries of both kinds, a `display-name:` or `agent:` line, under the name or an old name) and it is either listed on no page at all or named for the project (`<project>-<role>`: it belongs there and the map forgot it). A session listed on another project's map is a visitor and is left alone, and so are the owner, a `stackshot…` label (the server and the audit), a cloud session, a label no fleet view or session registry knows (a script) and, for the audit, a session sala no longer lists. *The note:* the reply of `create`, `update` and `message` to a project page (`to=projects/<slug>`) from such a session ends with a line `(note: <project> has no agents-map line for <session>. Its -main agent is <agent>: ask it to list you or to take this work.)`, the -main agent being the one `anchors.fallback_main` names (a page with no agents map falls to its hub's or the scope's general one), or `The project names no -main agent.` when none; a note, never a refusal, and a failure to make it leaves the reply as it was (`server._off_map_note`). *The finding:* the page agent lines check, which runs each day, carries a fourth kind of line, `<page> · session off the map · <session> (<n> items), … · <fix>`: for each active `type: project` page, the live sessions that created (`metadata.owner`), move (`metadata.mover`) or are the contact of an open item and are off its map, up to four named with their item counts and the rest counted (`page_agent_report.offmap`); the page is the one that holds the map (the hub's, for a page with a `hub:` line). It goes the way the other lines do, to the page's own -main agent, else the fallbacks, else the audit's -main agent, marked `[for <owner>]` on line 1 (`audit_run.page_agent_groups`, `par_target`, `compose`), and repeats each day until the page lists the session; it needs sala's session list as the other lines do (not read: no such line). Refusing a session for being off the map is not done here. Suites: `tests/off_map_scenario.py` (the rule, the notes, the moved page left with no agents map replayed from created items, the delivery) and `tests/audit_run_scenario.py`.

**The puller and the inbox.** A notice or message for a session on another machine waits in the server's inbox
(`inbox.py`). Each machine runs one `puller.py`, which holds a long poll open to the server with its machine token,
reports the sessions live on that machine (from the CLI's own registry, `~/.claude/sessions`; the router's own `claude -p` call, which runs in a `stackshot-router-*` temporary directory, is not a session and is skipped), writes each waiting line
into the session's inbox (a unix socket on Linux, a named pipe on Windows) and confirms it. The poll is also the
machine's sign of life. `client.py` mints and revokes machine tokens on the server box and reads the board from any box.
`push.py` puts the lines into the inbox and delivers on the server box itself what the inbox does not serve: a pane
here over its local socket, a cloud session with `claude --cloud`. The server box reaches into no other machine.
A line is for a session by its permanent id (code finds a session by its permanent
id, names are for people): `inbox.put` resolves whatever the sender wrote (a permanent id, a name, an alias, a transcript id) to the
permanent id of a live registry session and stores it on the line (`pid`; `name` stays for display), and a poll offers the line to
the live transcript that carries that id (`inbox._carriers`: the transcript the registry lists under it, else the one whose
server-kept name resolves to it; the newest registration wins when two carry it). A relaunch with a new transcript, a rename or the
CLI's placeholder name therefore strands nothing. A line with no permanent id (a name the registry does not know, a registry that is
down, a line stored before an earlier change) is matched as before: by the id it was addressed to, else by the name the server kept for the
session (never the name its puller reports: a relaunched pane reports the CLI's placeholder), the newest registration winning when two live
sessions carry one name. A line that still waits `[client] inbox_flag_minutes` (default 5) for a session that is running, on a
machine whose puller checks in, is reported once, with the likeliest reason, to the running pane that sent it and to the fleet's
keeper (`inbox.new_overdue`, the clients' tick). Not reported: a line held on purpose (a relaunch hold in
`held-msg.txt`, a paused source), the flag's own lines, and anything in the first minute after the server starts.

**The puller keeps a client box current** (puller 9; the PC's helper is its one service, and it
keeps itself current). Every poll's answer carries what is current (`clients.current`: the server's puller `VERSION` and
checkout commit, and whether that box may update itself). When the box is behind (its checkout is not at the server's
commit and is its ancestor) and idle (no live session in the CLI's registry, no `claude` process: the hooks run from the
checkout under a session, so it is never moved under one), the puller fast-forwards its whole checkout (`git fetch
origin`, `git merge --ff-only` to the server's commit: `pages.py`, the hooks and everything else the box runs from it move
with the puller; a dirty or diverged checkout is refused and the refusal reported).

**What the server advertises, and a box stranded off main.** The commit the server advertises is its checkout's HEAD only while `origin/main` (as last fetched) contains it: a checkout switched to an unmerged branch keeps advertising the last commit it advertised (kept in `<state>/clients/advertised-commit`; origin/main's own commit when it has none) and logs one warning line, so no box follows a branch that main will not contain. A box that did follow one anyway (HEAD is a commit the puller itself moved to, kept in `puller-updates.json` as `moved`, but is not in `origin/main`, tested first against the local ref with no network call, and fetched only when that says off main) and is clean is put back on the advertised commit with `git reset --keep` when that commit is in `origin/main`, the recovery recorded as an attempt (`recovered: left <commit>, which is not in origin/main`) and under `recovered` in the same file; a dirty checkout, or a HEAD the puller did not move to, is still refused. A target commit is tried once, then
again after 1 h, 4 h and daily; after every checkout update the puller reloads itself (the reload described above, on Linux
and Windows alike, whether or not the `VERSION` changed: the new file runs in the same process, so a user unit or the scheduled
task's instance never ends). Every attempt is reported with the
next poll as `facts.updates`, `[{what: "stackshot checkout", from, to, ok, at, reason}]` (the last 10, oldest first; `from`
and `to` the commits' first 12 characters; `at` in UTC; `reason` empty on success), beside `facts.stackshot` (the box's
checkout commit in full, kept on the machine's record like its CLI version); the Machines panel keeps the attempts and
draws a failed one on the machine's column. The target is compiled from git before the tree is touched (the puller's files and the modules they import; a commit that does not
compile is refused), idle is asked again right before the merge, a failed merge is rolled back to the old commit, and a reload
that does not load is recorded as failed and the running puller carries on. A checkout that reaches the server's commit some other way (swapped for a fresh clone, moved there by hand) is settled on the next poll with one ok attempt (`already at the server's commit`), so an older failure stops showing; `puller-updates.json` is never edited for that. On Windows only the CLI's own `claude.exe` counts as a
claude process, not Claude Desktop's; a process check that cannot answer counts as busy. `[client] self_update = false` switches it off: in the server's config for
every box (the server never says yes to its own box: `deploy.sh` keeps that checkout), in a box's own config for that box.
`puller.py --dry-run` prints what the box would update now, or why not. Until this, sala kept a Windows box's `~\stackshot`
current: its heartbeat fast-forwarded the checkout to `origin/main` every round, and its watchdog provisioned a lagging box
over ssh (`client.py puller-lag`, still served for the panel); both retire on sala's side, replaced by this. The helper has no
part in the Claude CLI (it only reports the installed version): the machine's own provisioning step updates it.

**Session links.** Both come from the fleet tool (see [The fleet tool](#the-fleet-tool)), and both are off until configured.
On the web pages (the board, the item pages, the project pages and inline mentions) a session's name links to its view in
the fleet tool's web UI, `client.session_view` with the name url-quoted for `{name}` (on an instance run with sala, sala's
`https://sala.example.com/#s={name}`), in a new tab, while the session holding that name has a live pane by the one
presence rule (`fleet.present`: running, or relaunching within the grace); a name with no live pane, a cloud or Cowork
session's included, is plain text (link the board and sala both ways). board.md, the brief
and the `sessions` tool give the `url` the fleet tool writes for that transcript id in its own live store
(`client.session_links`, `<dir>/<letter>/sessions.json`): on such an instance a web-terminal link for a pane on the server
box. A session the store has no row for, or a row with an empty url, is named there without a link, whatever its Remote
Control bridge does; a missing or unreadable store is logged once and leaves every pane without a link. A cloud or Cowork
session (`cse_…`) keeps its claude.ai link from `cloud.py` there. This is the one read of the fleet tool's files the server
makes since an earlier change; names, machines and presence still come from the
server's own clients.

**anchor-hub and its receivers.** `anchor-hub.py` (a user unit) materialises each project's anchors into the sessions
bound to them (the project's keeper, plus the movers of its open items in scope), debounces bursts into one notice and
a follow-up, and delivers through `push.py`. Each receiver is a module in `anchorhub/` with a `run(hub)` function:
`slack.py` (Socket Mode, a user token), `gworkspace.py` (Drive and Gmail, push channels with polling behind them),
`notion.py` (webhooks where the integration can see the page, polling where it cannot) and `github.py` (through the
`gh` CLI: issues, pull requests, CI, releases, notifications). A receiver with no credentials stays off. `routing.py`
logs every notice delivered for a project and its ruling (the router's).

**The router** (`router.py`, the judging; `router_live.py` below acts on it). It judges every notice held for any project page: first by a rule in code (a notice already ruled by `stackshot (auto)`; a notice about a page from a job named in `[router] page_senders`, held for the page pass; a wake notice for the mover of the item it is about, dropped; an anchor notice on the WATCH anchor `example-upstream/tool#123`, passed to `other-main` for the owner; an anchor notice on a GitHub pull request whose head branch starts with a background agent's name and a slash (`my-engineer/topic`; anchor-hub reads the head from the PR and the routing record keeps it as `head`, with the author as `author`) and that is not itself for the owner, passed to that builder (rule `anchor-builder`: a CI or PR notice on a builder's pull request is the builder's to act on, not a turn of the item's front keeper; an identical repeat within an hour is dropped); an anchor notice that reports a CI or check-run result (anchor-hub's event type `ci_failed`, `ci_passed` or `ci_activity`, which the routing record keeps as `etype`; a record without one is read by its sender `CI`) on any pull request, upstream ones included, and is not itself for the owner, passed to the project's builder (a sub-page's: its hub's) (rule `anchor-ci`: a PR's CI run only goes to the engineer and never produces a front turn; it follows `anchor-builder`, so a builder's own branch still goes by that rule, and it carries the item id when the anchor is scoped to exactly one open item; it applies also when the item waits on the owner's decision or he moves it, since a CI result is never his; an identical CI notice is dropped as a repeat only when the latest earlier CI notice on the anchor is the same within the hour (a failure, a pass and the same failure again are three notices); a project with no builder routes the notice as before); an anchor notice from an anchor that carries a `#mention=<text>` filter (rule `anchor-work`), passed to the agent the page's lead names for the work (the one agent of the project's agents map, front or background, other than its -main agent, that the page's lead paragraph, the text before its first `## ` heading, names; `pages.py` records those names in the registry as `lead_agents`, so no page is read per notice), without the thin call's judgment, also when the notice names the owner, an identical repeat within an hour dropped; it runs ahead of the `quiet` rule, and fires once the page registry is rebuilt with `lead_agents` (publish.py's pass; before that the notice is ruled as before); it is held as before when it is only an image, and goes on to the rules below when the anchor is also listed without a filter, is scoped to items (anchor-item), when the lead names no agent or several, or when the work agent sent it; an anchor notice on an anchor scoped to one open item, passed to that item's mover (rule `anchor-item`), except, since an earlier change (a front mover is woken only for what may need him), build-side news on a GitHub anchor when the mover is a front agent, by a whitelist of the event's type (the routing record's `etype`: every type of the notice is `comment`, `review`, `review_comment`, `pr_*` or `issue_*`, or the anchor is a repository's `@releases`; a notice with no type on another anchor, which is what the `@notifications` reasons review requested, mentioned you, assigned to you, a security alert, an invitation and on your thread are, and an unclassified or mixed burst, stays with the keeper): it goes to the item's mover when that is a background agent, else to a background agent its needs or first next line name, else to the project's builder, and the item id rides along; a front agent mover keeps the notice when the owner wrote it (his login as the sender, any of a burst's senders), when its text names him (`[for <owner>]`, `needs: decision`, a decision word, the owner or one of his aliases, or `@` his login), when the item waits on his decision or he is its mover (the model's derived mover), in a project with no builder, and for an anchor that is not GitHub's (an identical repeat within an hour is dropped for such a pass; anchor-hub's follow-up for a burst of events keeps the burst's event types joined with `+`, its senders joined with `, ` and, for any earlier text that names the owner, that text first in the gist and `other` added to the types, so a comment folded behind a CI result is not a CI notice); a message naming one open item, passed to whoever does the item's work: a background agent the item's needs name, else its mover when that is a background agent, else the project's builder (the background agent whose mandate says build, maintain, merge or engineer), else, when the message itself is for the owner (`[for <owner>]` or `needs: decision`), the front agent mover for the owner, else held for the digest when it only names the owner or a decision, else merged because the item carries it, and merged when the target is the sender itself (a project with no background agent passes to the mover as before); an exact repeat of an anchor notice, dropped; a message of the same sender whose text, with hashes, quoted commit subjects, digits, weekdays and months masked (at least 80 characters), equals an earlier message already ruled by the router within `max_age_hours`, dropped (a sync-check script); on a project page whose config marks Drive anchors as quiet (`[[router.quiet]]`, e.g. `projects/example-site`) a non-urgent Drive anchor notice, held for one digest after the quiet hour (also when it only names the owner or a decision; only `[for <owner>]` or `needs: decision` goes on to the call); a notice whose text is only a file marker such as `[files: image.png]`, held with the reason "image not read", never passed; a green `[release]` message from the project's own maintainer, a sender named `*-maintainer` that the page lists as an agent, merged as "log only: the maintainer's own release"), the rest of a project's unjudged notices together in one fresh `claude -p` call (Sonnet, no tools, a fixed rubric, the project's agents, its open items (at most ten, most recently changed first, a count of the rest: id, mover, the background agent that does the work when it is not the mover, the party it waits on, the title cut to 80 characters; a mover or worker that is no agent of the project's own list is not named, since the call may name no other; the rubric sends a notice that asks the item's agent for something to that agent, lets a mandate decide only a notice about no listed item, and leaves a notice that only reports activity on an item to the rules above) and its page's Current state, started from a login shell with the API variables unset, in the CLI's safe mode so no user hook runs) that returns a JSON ruling per notice (`id`, `ruling`, `target`, `reason`, `for_owner`, and an optional `note`: one line of at most 300 characters for the project page's timeline, kept on the ruling line as `note` for a notice nobody has to act on but that belongs on record). Each notice goes into the call with its full text, from the held stash when there is one, else the routing log's 300-character gist, at most 4000 characters. The owner's principle for the rubric: the fewer messages reach the front agents (the `-main` agent and the other top-level agents he talks to), the better. A front agent gets a notice live only when it asks that agent or the owner for an action or a decision; a notice that is unsure, off-topic or asks nothing is `held` for the project's daily digest to its `-main` agent; background agents get the operational notices as before. A failed or slow call, an answer that does not parse and a notice the answer leaves unruled are tried once more at the next tick (the notices stay unjudged; the tries are kept in `<state>/router/attempts.json`); the second failure, and a notice the answer passes to no agent of the project, go to the project's `-main` agent in the log, for the owner, with the reason. A notice's text is data: the call gets each notice between `<notice>` tags with its angle brackets written as entities, and its rulings are governed in code (`call_line`). Strong signals (the text carries `[for <owner>]` or `needs: decision`, the notice is an anchor notice of an item waiting on a decision, or the call itself marks `for_owner`) turn a drop, merge or hold into a pass to the item's mover (else `-main`) for the owner. Weak signals (the text only names the owner or one of his aliases, or says decision or a word of `[keywords.router] decision`) turn a drop or merge into `held`, "held for the digest: …", never into a pass to a front agent; a call's own `held` stays held. A pass to a front agent stands only with `for_owner` or in a project with no background agent, else it goes to the builder, else it is held for the digest ("held for the digest: …"); a pass to the notice's own sender is merged. The rules in code follow the same line: a message about one item and a quiet-hour notice reach a front agent only on a strong signal, and a weak one is held. Every call is one line in `<state>/router/calls.jsonl` (project, notice count, seconds, status, exit code, token figures and the CLI's own cost figure). `router.py` itself writes no routing log and delivers nothing: `router_live.py` applies its rulings (below).

**The page-debt call** (`page_debt.py`, step 3b). A session that changes a board item's state owes the item's project page an update; one still undone after `[page_rule] grace_minutes` moves to the project's keeper (`_pilot_fallback_tick`). With `[router] page_debt = true` the audit run's pages stage (the last stage of its model calls, under the same `[audit] max_minutes` limit) asks the server for a pass, once per audit run, so twice a day with the timer's two times (there is no thread of its own and no start hour of its own; a button press makes a pass too). The pass therefore needs `[audit] enabled = true` in script mode: with the audit off, or in agent mode (the audit agent, no script), no pass runs and the debts wait. The audit script is a separate process and the debt ledger and the page writes are the server's, so the stage calls `POST /client/page-debt` (`client.page_debt`) and the server makes the pass (`server._page_debt_pass`; the page is marked per run, so one run calls it once, and a pass stops beginning pages at the run's time limit and leaves the rest unmarked for the next run; a page whose call raises is counted and the pass goes on): for each page with moved, still unsettled debts, one fresh `claude -p` call (the router's runner: Sonnet, no tools, safe mode, the API variables unset, a timeout) is given the items (subject, description, next, needs, ask, outcome, the last `said` lines), the page's Current state and its last timeline lines, and answers with JSON: `{"edits": [{"old_text": ..., "new_text": ...}], "reason": ...}` (each `old_text` copied from the Current state, occurring exactly once there) or `{"cannot": ...}`. In **shadow** mode (`page_debt_shadow = true`, the default) it only logs: `<state>/router/page_debt.jsonl` gets the proposal and whether its edits would apply to the page as the pages checkout holds it, and nothing else changes; the 30-minute relay stays as it is, so the log can be set beside what the keeper wrote. In **live** mode (`page_debt_shadow = false`) the page is read with the gbrain CLI (`gbrain call get_page`, as `rename_page.py` does), the edits are applied to its Current state only and written with `gbrain put <key> --expected-revision <revision>`, read back, and the debts settled. A put that times out, or fails in a way that leaves it open whether the page was written, is not taken as a refusal: the page is read back first, and one that shows the edits counts as written and the debts are settled (`GbrainIO.put` returns `ok`, `refused` for an explicit error such as a stale revision, or `unknown` for a timeout). A `cannot`, an answer that does not parse, a call that fails or times out, an `old_text` that does not match exactly once, a refused put (the page changed since it was read), a read-back that does not show the edit and an unknown put whose read-back shows no edit all give the same result: the debts go in one message in the words of today's relay to the agent that keeps the page (the page's frontmatter `agent:`, when that session is live), else the project's `-main` agent, else its hub's or the scope's general `-main` agent (`anchors.fallback_main`), and are theirs from then on; nothing is dropped. The `[for <owner>]` mark opens the message only when the recipient is a front agent (the `-main` agent or another top-level `agents:` entry); a background agent gets the plain text. The notice is ruled `passed` to a page's agent or the `-main` agent by `stackshot (auto)`, so nobody is reminded of it. In live mode the 30-minute relay to the keeper is no longer sent: the debt is marked as moved and waits for the next audit run's call. Each call is also a line in `calls.jsonl` (`"kind": "page_debt"`; `router.py compare` leaves those out).

**The router acts on every project** (`router_live.py`). There is no switch: `[router] live`, `live_projects`, `shadow`, `tick_minutes` and `[pilot] projects` are ignored if a config still has them, and the rollback is a code revert. Every page of the registry is a project the router rules (`anchors.pilot_root`: its top-level page key). A notice is not delivered to a session by the place that receives it: that place (anchor-hub's `send`, the server's message to a project, the board relay `_pilot_relay` / `_pilot_relay_many`, the wake pass) asks `router_live.holds(project, recipient)`, and when it says yes the notice goes into the routing log as usual with `"live": true` and no recipient, its full text is kept in `<state>/router/held/<id>.json`, and nothing is pushed. A thread of the server (`router_live.loop`, every `[router] live_tick_seconds`, 60 by default) takes the unruled held notices and judges them with the router's code (`code_rule`, then one thin call per project, `call_project`), then acts: **passed** is pushed to the target (push.py, the pane inbox), with `[for <owner>]` on the first line whenever the target is a front agent, whatever `for_owner` says (a front pane takes a peer's message only with the mark; a delivery to a background agent is unmarked), and ruled with `routing.rule(id, "passed", to=target, by="stackshot (router)")`; **dropped**, **merged** and **held** are ruled and nothing is delivered (a held notice is the page pass's). After a ruling the server does what `route` does: a hold that waited on the notice's anchor is released on a passed or merged ruling (not on a dropped or held one), and the board is republished. A notice whose call failed or was left out stays held, undelivered, for one more try at the next tick (`attempts.json`); the second failure, or an exception, passes it to the project's `-main` agent with the mark and the reason, never dropped. **Which agents a page has.** A page lists its own front and background agents. A page that lists none is served as follows (`anchors.fallback_main`, `router_live.stand_in`): first its hub's agents (its `hub:` line; `router_live.agent_root`), then the `-main` agent of the scope's general page, configured under `[router]` as `general_<scope>`, one key per scope (none by default; an agent name, or a page key whose `-main` agent is used when the value has a "/"; a general page that lists no front agent but has a `display-name:` line is a one-session page, and its agent is that name-line session), falling back to the default scope's when that page does not exist. For such a page the call is given that one front agent and nobody else. A pass with no agent anywhere is `held` with the reason "no agent can take it", never dropped and never sent to a pane that is gone. A delivery that fails is tried again at each tick, three times, then goes to the `-main` agent. Every action is a line in `<state>/router/live.jsonl`. **Where a page's name-line session used to be written to, delivery goes to the stand-in** (`router_live.stand_in`, `server._stand_in_keeper`): the project's builder (a background agent whose mandate says build, maintain, merge or engineer), else its `-main` agent, else the hub's or the scope's general one. `_page_keeper`, `_keeper` (the item notices) and `_front_keeper` (a front agent mover's notices) answer the stand-in, and `_completion_anchor_prompt` tells it. The pages' `display-name:` lines name the project for the agent prefix and for `anchors.notice_name`, which falls back to the stand-in chain for a page without one; every name-line session of a page that lists user-facing agents is `retired` in `session_roles`. There is no unruled-notice reminder and no daily digest of automatic rulings (they chased a pane that is gone); the board's "waiting" flag and `python3 router_live.py status` show a notice the router has not ruled. The page-debt relay is held and ruled by the router too (the rubric sends a page debt to the agent that keeps the page, else the `-main` agent); with `page_debt` live the audit's page call takes it as before. **The daily digest of held notices.** A notice the router rules `held` (the page pass, a page's quiet hours, what the call is unsure of, an image it could not read) reaches no agent at once, and owes nothing: what is for the owner lives in stackshot's For you list, not in a front agent's turn. Once a day per project, from `[router] digest_hour` (default 9, the instance timezone; a server that was down then does it when it is back the same day), `digest_tick` (the live thread) writes one entry to `live.jsonl` (`kind: digest_logged`: project, group, count, ids and the digest's text, one line per notice with id, label, sender, time, gist and the router's reason) and counts the notices as digested (none is listed twice); an empty list writes nothing. The notices held by the code rule `page-pass` (the page sheets of the jobs `[router] page_senders` names) are their own group under the head "held for the page pass", logged the same way. A notice the router could not rule (see the cap below) is stackshot's own problem, so a digest with one is delivered: to the project's builder, unmarked, listing every row of the group; a project with no builder takes it to its `-main` agent, opening with `[for <owner>]`, since someone must see it. The held quiet-hour notices of `router.QUIET` and the `[[router.quiet]]` rows (a page such as `projects/example-site`, with its agent) go in the same pass to that agent instead, unmarked while it is a background agent. Each group has its own day mark and tries. `<state>/router/digest.json` records the day each group was done, the notices already digested (none is sent twice) and each project's last delivered digest; the routing log's `held` ruling is not touched. Each digest delivered is a line of `live.jsonl` (`kind: digest`, project, count, target, outcome). A push that fails is tried again at the next ticks, three times a day, and its notices wait for the next day. **A timeline note.** A ruling line from the call may carry `note` (one line, at most 300 characters). After the ruling is written the note goes to the project page's timeline, source `stackshot (router)`, dated today in the instance timezone, with the gbrain CLI the way `page_debt.GbrainIO` runs it (`gbrain call add_timeline_entry '{"slug", "date", "summary", "source", "detail", "request_id"}'`), once per notice (`noted: true` on the line). A gbrain failure is kept on the line (`note_error`) and never blocks the ruling. `[router] notes = false` ignores notes. **A cap on the re-judge loop.** A ruling that cannot be written to the routing log (`routing.rule` raised) is tried again at the next ticks, three times in all; the notice is then logged with status `stuck`, never judged again, and listed in the next digest as one that could not be ruled. `python3 router_live.py status` prints a line per project: the notices acted on in the last hour, the notices held since the last digest, the time of the last digest, the last error.
**The Machines panel.** The board shows one card per machine, its name as the card's bold first label, with a row per
package in `[machines_panel]`: the installed version and `latest ✓`, `→ <latest version>` (in the text colour, on the row's own line), `offline` (from the puller's check-ins) or
`latest unknown`, against the latest from npm or GitHub releases (`versions.py`). A failed setup step or update is a card of its
own under the machine's. The cards are the fleet's machines (sala's letters) and then the `[machines_panel] ssh_hosts`, machines with
no puller (spare-box): **one card per machine**, keyed by the full hostname: a host that is also a fleet machine (spare-box, while the config still lists it in `ssh_hosts`) is the fleet machine's card, with the ssh-probed version where the host has the package and answers, and the fleet's row for any package only the fleet knows, or for every package while the probe fails (`fleet.machines_order` gives the one list of names: the publisher, then the fleet by letter, then the remaining hosts in config order). An `ssh_hosts` name merges only when it equals the fleet name exactly (the full hostname). The publisher runs one `ssh -o BatchMode=yes` call per host on each forced refresh (the timer publish,
every `[publish] every_minutes`, and `bump`), 10 s at most, with each package's version command (`ssh_cmd`, else the `cmd:` command, else
`self_cmd`); a package the host lacks gets no row, an unreachable host shows `offline` with the versions it last had. A machine card, fleet or ssh host,
carries no hover text at all. The read is cached in `<state>/site/versions.ssh.json`.

**The GitHub mirror** (`ghsync.py`; slimmed by an earlier change). With `[github] sync` on, the store stays the working copy and every item has an
issue in `[github] issues_repo`, which gives it its number. The server's save calls the hook; a queue on disk (`<state>/github/queue/`)
and a worker thread carry the writes, paced under GitHub's limits, and an outage only delays them. **An issue carries the item's subject
(the title), whether it is open or closed (with the close reason) and its description (the body: a marker line that maps issue to item, the
description, and one footer line linking the item's page), and nothing else** (the store's files are the backup, and
issues are a mirror; people read the board instead). There are no history comments, no labels (`on hold` included), no typed
issue fields (owner, needs, project, priority, due, wake), no blocked-by links, and no Next, Ask, Options, Draft or Outcome in the body. The
store's files are the backup (the publisher pushes them to a repository you choose), and the item page's History is the store's git, with the mirror on
or off. A change to a field the issue does not carry makes no job and so no GitHub call at all (`ghsync.mirrored`, `on_change`); what older
versions wrote to GitHub (comments, labels, field values) stays as it is, nothing is cleaned up. With the mirror on, an `update`, `confirm` or `complete` that names no
`sender` is refused instead of being credited to the item's mover (`client.py recycle-due` names its script). `ghmigrate.py` moves a store's open items onto issues #1 onward (old ids
stay as aliases: `/items/<old id>` redirects); `[github] issue_ids` makes a new item's id its issue number, written
`board#<n>`. A create while GitHub is down, or when the hour's write budget leaves no room within a few seconds (`ghsync.CREATE_WAIT`, so a create never hangs behind a backlog of updates), is not refused: the item gets a temporary id under `[github] local_letter`
(`l1`, `l2`, …, never reused), its create waits in the queue, ahead of every other job, and when GitHub answers or the budget has room the worker creates the issue,
re-keys the item to its number (the old id an alias, `after` lists and anchor scopes following, as in the migration) and
tells the mover the new id. A temporary id is written `board-tmp-<machine>#<n>` (`<machine>` is `[instance] machine`,
e.g. `board#N`) and its page is `/items/board-tmp-<machine>-<n>`, which redirects to the issue-numbered item once
it is re-keyed; the tools read either form, and `l<n>`, as the item. Both switches are off by default.
The merge window: the updates of an issue that already exists wait `[github] merge_seconds` (300) in the queue, counted from the
oldest one, and go out together as one PATCH of the issue (title, body and state). A create, and a close or reopening (with whatever waits beside it), go at
once. The window is read off the jobs' timestamps on disk, so a restart in the middle keeps every pending update. One ledger,
