`<state>/github/budget.json` (a file locked with `flock`, shared by the server and anchor-hub), counts every call of the host's one token
in the last hour: writes under `hourly_budget`, reads under `read_budget`, a `304` never. The server's reads (runners page, side
panel, merged-PR checks, the comment reader) and anchor-hub's `gh api` calls are in it. Each call also carries a caller tag (the optional `by` key of the
file, beside the unchanged `w` and `r` lists): `done_check`, `step_proof` and `event_wait` for the three merged-PR readers, `ghsync` for the mirror,
`comments` for the comment reader, `receivers` for anchor-hub, `release` (reserved for the release script's path), and `other` for a call with no tag, an unknown tag, or one a
process from before the tags made. `ghbudget.status()` and `ghbudget.by_caller()` give the hour's counts per caller (`by_caller` in the status, next to the
unchanged `reads`, `writes` and caps; the tags of each kind add up to its total). A file without `by` still reads (every call is `other`).
anchor-hub's GitHub reads and a restart (a restart made every conditional read a full, counted 200, one pass over every anchor and a start-up rescan, which could spend the hour's cap and leave the `github` row at "error"):
anchor-hub keeps its read position in `<state>/anchor-hub/cursors.json` (written aside and replaced, saved each minute and at a clean stop), and
for `github` that holds, besides each anchor's state and CI state, the ETag of each conditional read (per anchor: the issue, the pull request and
the check runs of its newest commit), the repository IDs it has learnt, and the saved resolution of each anchor's board label with the ETag it was
taken at. After a restart every read of an unchanged anchor is a `304`, which costs nothing and needs no body, since what it would have said is
in the saved state; a change during the downtime is a `200`, read against that state and reported. An anchor's ETags are saved only when the pass
has finished with it, so a save in the middle of a read (a clean stop) or after a read that failed keeps the older ETag and the change is read
again after the restart, never answered `304`. ETags saved by another `gh` login, or a section of the file that does not read as expected, are
not used; nor are an anchor's ETags when the saved state has no entry for the anchor (or, for an issue or pull request, no CI state): its first look is a
full read that seeds it. The start-up rescan names an anchor from its saved resolution (a conditional read of its issue, a `304`), and does not ask one that is
closed or merged at all, so the board's label of a finished anchor (its title, its last-activity age) can stay as it was until the next restart after the
anchor changes; the poll drops that saved label when it sees the anchor change. A finished anchor, a merged pull request or a closed issue (its CI, issue and
comments), is read once in 10 passes (10 minutes), staggered by anchor so they do not fall on one pass, instead of every pass: a reopening or a late comment is
seen within 10 passes. A closed pull request that is not merged is not finished (it can be reopened and merged, which must be reported): it is read every pass,
as a `304`; a merge that follows a reopening between two looks is reported as a merge. A restart with the ETags saved costs one counted read (GET /user) and
the reads of what changed; the first start with this code over a file from before it (state but no ETags, and no record of which anchors are merged pull
requests) runs the rescan in full once, reads the open anchors in full, and reads each closed one once as an issue only (no CI), which learns whether it is finished.
The inbound half (phase 3): with the mirror on, a reader thread polls the repo's comments every `[github] poll_seconds` (60 by
default, one GET each; no webhook, since the server sits behind an access proxy), and a comment by `[github] owner_login`
(by default the login the mirror's token belongs to) with no `[author: …]` header (the mirror's older comments carry one) is the owner's own: it is recorded on the
item under `metadata.answers` as `{ts, kind: "comment", text, via: "github", url, comment_id}`, shown in the item page's
history as written on GitHub, and told in one line to the item's mover and to every session named in its needs (a front
agent mover hears it marked for the owner). It clears no needs, changes no status and owes no page; the server's save of it
is not echoed back to the issue. The cursor and the ids delivered live in `<state>/github/comments.json`: a restart delivers
nothing twice, an edit of a delivered comment is not a new one, and the first run starts at "now", never at the history
before the deploy.
The body form (`BODY_FORM`) says which body an issue was last written with. The existing issues are not rewritten in bulk
(that would cost hours of the hourly write cap for no reader's benefit): an issue takes the slim body the next time its subject,
open or closed, or description changes. `[github] backfill_bodies` (off) lets `resync_bodies` sweep the stale ones once, paced, on the background
lane, behind the live writes. The map's `repo` note follows `[github] issues_repo` on its own when the board repo moves: never a hand edit in
the store. With `[github] issues_repo_id` set, every call for the repo addresses it by its numeric id (`/repositories/<id>/...`), so the writes
go on through a move too (GitHub follows a 301 on a read, not on a write); the name still makes the links. Until an earlier change an issue also carried per-change comments (bundled within 10 minutes, the item page's History drawn from them), typed
issue fields and blocked-by links, and the labels `needs:<x>`, `project:<x>` and `on hold`; those writers, their sweeps and the local copy of the
comments (`<state>/github/comments/`, `last.json`, `fields.json`) are gone, and the files they left in the state directory are unread.

**A front agent does not watch handed-off work** (stackshot refuses a sender's wake or check on a handed-off item, and its text stops teaching it; the line in his rulebook: a front agent does not watch handed-off work itself, no wake, poll or background check it schedules). `update` refuses a **front agent** sender's reminder `wake` when, once the call's own `mover`, `needs`, state and hand-off lines are applied, the item is **alive** and moved by another session (its mover; a session named by a `<session>: <action>` line of the call's `next` counts too). The refusal names the rule: the result lands through stackshot when the mover closes the item or hands it back to the front agent (`_tell_owner_close`, `_tell_handback`). Not gated: a `hold=true` park with its wake (a waiting item's wake is the park's own timer, fired by the server), a done `check` (the server-run push the rule itself names; an engineer's choice against the issue's first wording, which said "wake or check"), `ask_by` (the server watches that date), the mover's own wake, a wake the item already carries (re-sending it sets nothing), and any wake by a background agent, the owner or a sender no project page lists (the test is the message tool's front-agent test, `anchors.session_roles()`). `watch_reason=<why no push reaches you>` (at least 15 characters) lets a real exception through: it is stored as `metadata.watch_reason` and shows as a `watch exception` line in the item's History. It belongs to the wake it was given for: it is dropped when a later call changes the wake without a reason, clears it or sets none, when the wake pass fires or drops the wake, when a done check closes or fails the item, and at the close. A wake already stored survives a later hand-off (deliberate: it fires once as a reminder to the keeper). The hand-off gate below does not ask a front agent for a wake or check.

**The done check**. An item whose mover handed its work to another session
used to stay open after the work was done: nothing triggered the mover's check-and-close step. Now an `update` that creates such
a hand-off (a line of its `next` in the form `<session>: <action>`, a list marker such as `- ` or `1. ` before the name at most,
naming a session other than the mover and the writer that a page or the fleet knows and that its `needs` does not already name;
a name anywhere else, inside a page slug, a `#s=<name>` link or a sentence that mentions the session, hands nothing over) must
leave the item with a `wake` (a reminder: the sessions its notices go to (the sessions in its `needs`, else its mover) are told once at the date, and the item stays active in its lane; a wake parks an item only with an explicit `hold=true`; a wake that carries a `wait_basis` names what the item waits for, and the board lists the item as waiting until that wake) or a done check,
`metadata.check`, one of a fixed list the server reads itself every minute, never a command: `pr_merged <owner>/<repo>#<n>`
(the pull request is merged: one GET through the mirror's GitHub client, at most one per PR every `[check] github_minutes`,
shared by the items that name it, backing off up to an hour while GitHub is down), `item_closed <id>` (that item is
completed), `lane <session> <lane>` (the fleet tool's lane record, `[client] lanes_file`, has the pane on that lane: its row,
else the default lane, as the tool reads them; refused while `lanes_file` is unset; `sala_lane`, its former name, is still taken and read) and `unit_active <unit>` (the systemd user unit is loaded and active on the server
box, one `systemctl --user show`). When a check passes the server closes the item itself, outcome `done` and the reason
`done check passed: <the check>`, as its own act: no page owed, no notice to the mover, the sessions in its needs or the
observers, so no session turn is spent; with the mirror on the issue gets the server's comment, and the items that waited on it
(`after`) are released and told as any close releases them. A check the server cannot read (GitHub down or refusing, a unit
systemd does not know, no lane data) never closes: it is logged once per error, shown by `get`, and tried again each pass. A
check alone is a filing edit (no page owed); `check="none"` clears it; a close clears it. A write that leaves an existing
hand-off as it was is not asked again, and an item in that state before this rule is never retro-refused. A write that only
names a session in `needs` is not gated: that session is asked about the item at each of its turn ends and told to close it or
hand it back. The pass keeps its own state in `<state>/checks.json`.

**A draft** (`draft.py`). When the step the owner takes is to post, send or paste a text, the text itself is
on the item, not in a session's chat. `create` and `update` take `draft`: the text exactly as it is to be pasted, many lines, any language, stored as written in `metadata.draft` (only its line breaks are
made `\n` and the blank lines around it dropped; at most 20,000 characters, refused above). `update(clear_draft=true)` or `draft="none"` (`-`, `clear`) removes it; `draft=""` is the default and means
"not passed". A draft is a filing edit (no clock moves, no page is owed). `get` shows it as a `draft:` block, the brief only says it is there; the item page draws it inside the Your step block, right under the step's text and before the note box and the buttons, under a "Draft" label with a Copy button just beside it (the one the
command blocks use; where the browser refuses to copy, the text is selected for him); the text is in the page's own body font with its line breaks kept and escaped, not monospace (command blocks stay monospace). On an item that is
not in that block it is drawn in a block of its own. The home page's Your move row draws it the same way under the step line (under the question on an ask row), collapsed behind the label "draft", with the same Copy
beside the label, which copies without opening it. The GitHub mirror carries it in the issue body under `### Draft` (a fenced
code block) and says `draft set`, `draft edited` or `draft cleared` in the History. A close drops it, and so does his Send on the step (an empty box or with a note).
**The write guard** (`draft.problem`, `server._gate_draft`): an `update` that leaves an item in state `your-step` with the owner as the mover (no stored mover, or his name), whose `step` names a text to post, send or
paste, is refused unless the item has a draft (stored, or in this write) or its step or description holds an `http(s)` link to a document that can carry the text (a doc, Drive, Notion or Slack link; a
GitHub issue, pull request or commit link, and a link to the board itself ([instance] `board_url`), do not count). A step names such a text when, outside backticks and links, it holds a word of `[keywords.draft]` (`stems` and `stem_patterns` anywhere in the step, `standalone_stems` as a whole word with at most one of the `particles` after it, and a phrase of `ignore_phrases`, such as a screen share, cut out first), or the whole word
post, send, paste, reply, draft, message, email, share, forward, submit (with their endings: posting, sent, replied, e-mail; "post-mortem", "postgres", an "error", "commit", "log" or "warning" message, "send me",
"send back", "reply here", "message me" (the answer to the session that asked), "share your screen", "going forward" and "submit the form" or "a PR" do not count). **The guard is a heuristic, not a complete
list**: a step that asks for a post in other words passes it, and a step that uses one of these words for something else is refused until it has a draft or a link. The refusal is one line that names `draft` and the link
alternative. The guard reads only a write that sets the state, the step, the mover or the draft, so an unrelated write to an old item is never held up; `[model] guards = false` skips it; a `create` cannot set a
state, so it only stores a draft (a text drafted before the step exists).

**The step is a one-line summary; the commands are in Next** (`step_commands.py`).
The `step` of a your-step item is one line (at most 300 characters) that says what he does; what he runs is in the lines of
`next` that open with his name (`<owner>: In PowerShell run `ssh -t <host> 'uptime'` and read the output`), each command a backticked span with a space in it or a fenced block. The item page's Your step block shows the summary, then those lines in full as a numbered list, each command as a code block with a Copy
button under its line (the draft, if any, after the summary), and Next stays whole below. A step stored before this rule, with numbered lines or a fenced block, is drawn as it always was (an ordered list, each command in a code block with a Copy button). **The step guards** (`step_commands.problem`,
`server._gate_step`) read an item a write leaves in your-step with the owner as the mover, in this order, and refuse in one line that names the field: (1) the description holds a shell command (a fenced block tagged sh, bash, shell, powershell, pwsh or console with one in it, or a backticked span that starts with a known command word or a path and has arguments: `COMMAND_WORDS`; words that are also English, such as make, go, find, sort, top, free, head and watch, count only with a flag, a path or another argument-shaped token after them, so a button label in backticks passes; a description write is judged only on the commands it adds or changes, a write that leaves the description alone against the stored one): the refusal says to put the command in a "<owner>:" line of next; (2) the step points elsewhere ("see the description", "see below", "refer to the description",
or a phrase of `[keywords.step_commands] pointer_patterns`; naming his Next line is no pointer); (3) a write that SETS the step and gives it a command (a backticked span that looks like a shell command, a `! ` line, a fenced block) is refused, and the refusal says to put the command in a "<owner>:" line of next and let the step say what he does. A step stored with a command before that rule keeps the older judgement on a write that leaves it alone (`step_commands.command_problem`): a command is bash for the host (`LINUX_ONLY`: sala, systemctl, sudo, python3 …, a `./` or `~/` path, `$(…)`, an `ls -la`) and not the ssh form `ssh -t <host> '<command>'` (a `~/` path alone is valid PowerShell; a cmdlet and the
cross-platform tools such as git, gh, uv, curl and tailscale pass), or it has no word that says where it runs: PowerShell, Windows, WSL or a name from `[keywords.step_commands] windows_words` (a Windows user's machine, the default for every shell command of his); "a terminal on the host" is no place.
A `! ` line (a command typed into a session's pane, also inline after a colon or a run cue such as "type ! cmd") is refused outright, in a step and in a "<owner>:" line of next, on create and update, whether or not the line asks him to run it: the fix is `In PowerShell:` with `ssh -t <host> '<command>'` (for another host `ssh <host> '<command>'`); "done!" and lines for other agents are not judged. That older check is the one the check on "<owner>:" lines of next reuses (my-engineer's build) and goes from the step when no your-step item holds a stored command. With `[steps] owner_shell = "sala-shell"` the owner runs his commands in sala's Shell (a terminal in the sala web UI that runs bash on `bash_host`; `shell_name` names its session in the text), and the place rules above change: a command is bash as typed there (`step_commands.command_problem`), or `ssh <host> '<command>'` for another machine, no line saying where it runs is asked for ("In sala's Shell:" is a place phrase like "In PowerShell:", never required), and a PowerShell or Windows-only command outside ssh (a cmdlet, `winget`, `ipconfig`, a drive path: `step_commands.windows_only`) is refused; a `! ` line is refused as before, with the Shell form in the refusal. The create and update texts and `client.py`'s usage say so, with the paste hint (Shift+Insert, not Ctrl+V). With `"powershell"` nothing changes. Only a write that sets the state, step, mover, description or draft is judged; `[model] guards = false` skips it.

**The board's own sign-in** (`[auth] ui_open = false`; `ui_login`, `ui_callback`, `_ui_guard`, `_ui_renewed` in `server.py`) lasts `[auth] ui_session_days` from the last visit. A visit is a GET that a browser opens as a page (`Sec-Fetch-Mode: navigate`, or no such header, as a tool sends): the signed session cookie is written again with the full days from now, once an hour of its days is used, so a machine in use stays signed in and one left alone longer than the days is asked again. A session that has run out, a forged one and one of an address no longer in `allowed_emails` are never renewed; a script's fetch (the open tab's polling) and the `/ui/` routes (sign-out) do not renew. The board signs in through this server's own OAuth as a client it registers for itself, and for that one client and its own callback `/ui/callback` the server goes from `/authorize` straight to Google with no Allow page: any other client (claude.ai, a connector, a client that registers itself with the board's callback) still gets it. The consent binding cookie is still set and still checked at the Google callback, and the code goes only to `/ui/callback`, which takes it from the browser that holds the signed flow cookie of a sign-in started at `/ui/login` (its `state`, and the PKCE verifier the code is exchanged with). A page's script (a request with `Sec-Fetch-Mode` other than `navigate`) that finds itself signed out gets a plain `401` from every guarded route and from `/ui/login`, never a redirect to the sign-in, so a tab that has lapsed starts no sign-in: the home's polling script stops at the first 401 and shows "Signed out — sign in again" (a link that opens the page, which signs in), the Runners page's script stops too. A person's browser opening a page without a session is redirected to the sign-in as before. A browser older than Safari 16.4 sends no `Sec-Fetch-Mode`, so its polling script is still redirected and follows it as before. A lapsed tab does not recover by itself when the person signs in in another tab: its "Signed out" link, or a reload, does.

**Answering from an item's page**. For a viewer signed in to the board (`[auth] ui_open = false`, the
server's own sign-in), an open item's page carries an answer box under its Ask: while it needs the owner's decision, one
button per stored option (the recommended one first; Approve and Decline only when the owner lists them as options), or with
no options, Yes / No and the note box with its one Send; a stored your-step item gets the note box and one Send instead: Send with an empty box finishes the step, as Done did (recorded as
his answer "Done", the item goes to its then-mover), and Send with a note records `kind: step-reply` and hands the item to the then-mover with the note, which judges it (carry on if the note finishes the step; if it is a question or a request,
answer it or do it and hand the item back to him with a step; if it says he will do the step at a later time, put the item on hold with a wake at that time, `update(hold=true, wake=…, wait_reason=…)`, leave his step open and hand no new step back; the server does not read the note's time, the then-mover's notice carries the instruction). A page loaded before the release may still post `act=done` (as an empty Send) or `act=comment` (a comment only, the step stands). In the other cases the comment box is sent as his answer or as a comment. It is a plain form POST to `/items/<id>/answer`, refused without the sign-in, without the page's
token (an HMAC over his address and the item, keyed by `<secrets>/ui-session.key`) or from another origin. The page sends it
by script (a fetch that follows the 303, then `location.replace`), so the answer replaces the item page in history and Back
goes to where he came from; without script it is the plain POST and 303. Item and project pages are served `no-store` and
reload when restored from the back-forward cache (the board updates itself in place then). An answer is
stored in `metadata.answers`, takes `decision` out of needs (the sessions named stay) and clears the ask; a comment changes
neither. Each is pushed to the owner and to the sessions named in needs, and with the mirror on becomes the issue's comment
headed `[author: <owner> · board]`, a header only the server writes (a tool `sender` that names the owner is refused). A
decision that no session it went to has followed with a write within 30 min goes to the project's keeper once
(`<state>/answers.json`). There is no idle pass: a mover that goes quiet is the stall pass's and the hand-off ladder's (below). A raise to the owner's call stored before the last rung
existed keeps its "Park until <day>" button, which the server still applies itself (the hold and its wake, a YYYY-MM-DD written in the box changes the day; `[idle] park_days` is only its
fallback). `[idle] hours` is still read, by `waiting.py`: it is the expected time of a plain on-agent item.

**The minute set's runner** (`runner.py`, `server._minute_ticks`). The clocks that end a wait and move work along run in one `Runner` (one thread, one lock), always started by `server._start_background`; they used to run in two threads of their own, a wake loop and a 20-second fleet watch, in no order the code stated. The runner runs them in this order, each in its own try so that one that raises is logged and recorded and never stops the next: (1) the events that end a wait: the watchers' pass (`_wake_pass`), the anchor pass, the phase sweep (`_phase_tick`, below), the event waits (`_evwait_tick`), the done check (`_check_tick`), the step proof (`_proof_tick`); (2) the hand-off tick; (3) the answers (`_answer_tick`); (4) the fleet's other clocks, after the wait-enders: the registry sync, the clients' tick, the pilot fallback, the stall pass, the hand-back, and the publish when the fleet files changed; (5) the ten-minute set, after the rest: the filing pass, the dates pass (the For you due dates, and an expected time that has passed) and the wake catch-up. Each tick is a plain server function, called as it is, and keeps its own interval gate (the done check once a minute, the stall pass `[stall] every_seconds`). **The pace is one minute, and `runner.PACE` is the runner's one knob:** the fleet's clocks are *paced*, one round every `runner.PACE` seconds (60), the pause counted from the end of the last paced round, so a clock with its own gate of a minute or more (the done check, the event waits, the step proof) is not slowed; the two wake passes are *event* ticks, run when the wake event `_update` sets, when the wait the runner computes ends (until the earliest wake, at most 60 s), and in every paced round, in front of the paced ticks, so the events that end a wait are settled before the done check, the step proof or the hand-off tick look at the board, whichever of the two clocks started the round; a wake still fires at its minute, except that the lock is held for the whole round: a wake that falls due while a round runs waits for it to end and can slip by the length of one round (a tick over 30 s is logged by name). The thread sleeps until the earlier of the two. The ten-minute set (`runner.TEN_PACE`, 600 s) is a third group: it runs in the first paced round after a start (so a restart never delays it), then in the first paced round after each tenth minute, right after the minute set in that round, on a schedule that does not slip when a round is late and does not run twice after a long gap; it stands still while the board is held, and a wake event alone never runs it. `GET /audit/status` carries `ten_pace_seconds` beside `pace_seconds`. The migration's hold on the board skips the same clocks as before (all but the registry sync and the clients' tick). **The runner is the only path:** there is no switch. A config file that still carries the retired `[runner] enabled` key starts as usual; the key is read by nothing, and `./stackshot doctor`, the server's start and a reload print one note saying it can go. The start logs `stackshot: minute set: runner …`, and `GET /audit/status` carries a `runner` object: `enabled` (always true), `mode` (always `runner`), `running`, `started`, `pace_seconds`, `rounds`, `last_round` and per clock `name`, `group`, `pace`, `runs`, `errors`, `skipped`, `last_run`, `last_ms`, `last_error` and `last_error_at` (epoch seconds; `last_error` is null until the first error and then stays, with its time, until the next error: a clean run does not clear it). **The wake catch-up and the runner's watchdog:** the timer publish (every `[publish] every_minutes`) no longer runs a wake pass of its own. The pass it ran moved into the runner's ten-minute set as the tick `wake_catchup`, which runs the watchers' pass without the due-wake gate of the minute tick (the watchers also end waits that `_next_wake` does not count: an item wait whose `after` items all closed while nobody saw the close, with its ask-by days away), and publishes the board when it released something. The watchdog is a thread of its own (`server._runner_watchdog_loop`, started with the runner, a look a minute, so a stuck publish never silences it): it asks `Runner.stalled()`, and when the last completed round is older than `runner.STALL_AFTER` (3 minutes; a blocking call inside a tick) it writes one stderr line and sends ONE message to the `[audit] builder` per stall, naming the tick the runner is in (`Runner.current`), with no board item and nothing for the owner; the next look that finds a round completed again re-arms it. The runner's times are the wall clock (`time.time`), as its status has always been, so a clock step could show or hide a stall for one look. Not in the runner: the 10-minute publish, the router, the GitHub mirror, the comments reader and the search index keep their own loops (the page-debt call is no loop of the server any more: the audit run's pages stage asks for it).

**The filing pass and the dates pass** (`audit_checks.py`, `audit_run.filing_pass`, `server._filing_tick` and `_dates_tick`; the filing pass also needs `[audit] enabled`). The daily audit's checks that need no judgment live in `audit_checks.py` as functions (`field_findings` and the per-item checks; the daily run in `audit_run.py` calls them and re-exports their names). The ten-minute set runs a subset on the open items: **filing**, where the item says one thing and the board another and the fix is the writer's, nothing about silence or time passing. It checks stuck-elsewhere (a next line that hands a step to a session the item's mover does not own, `audit_checks.check_stuck_elsewhere`), step-handover (a finished step that did not hand the item on, `audit_checks.check_step_handover`: a step that delivers a pull request has another session's step after it and no proof, or the last done step was marked by the mover or the server at least 30 minutes ago and the session the first open step names, a background session, was never handed the item, unless the item is on hold, waits on a decision or has needs that name another session, which the server leaves alone too (`handover.skip_reason`, one rule for both); the finding goes to the mover), the board report's filing kinds (arbitrary-hold and mislabeled-state, the board report's two kinds), ask-options (an open item's ask the page cannot be answered from, `audit_checks.check_ask_options`, read with the write gates' own matchers in `ask_read.py`: a Yes / No (also options the page cannot read) under a Decide part that is an open question or names alternatives, or a Recommend part that plainly does not match the options (it names no option by label, letter, place or a leading word only the recommended label has, or names another; an option after not, avoid, never, skip, except or instead of, or a word of `[keywords.ask_options]` `refusals`, or a label right before a word of `refusals_after`, is turned away; a label followed by a `particles` suffix still counts as named) or answers yes or no to an open Decide; in doubt nothing is found, and an ask that does not split into Decide and Recommend is not read; the fix asks the mover to rewrite the ask with options; no model call, no cap, quiet once the ask is rewritten), a next line that reads as a wait in words only (`wait_words`: the precise hits, such as "once board#N closes", are a finding; the broad words when, once, after and until alone are listed in the pass's flags and sent to no one, and a date-only wait left alive (`audit_checks.date_only_wait`, category `why-not-now`: a doing item whose wake is over the hour away and whose every open next line is its mover's and opens with the wake's own hour or day, so nothing is left to do before then and the board still lists it as doing; no model call and no cap, unlike the daily `why-not-now` on dated steps; the fix text is the park form)). A finding goes to the same recipient the daily run's findings go to, through the same `deliver_findings`, and is sent once while the item is not written to. The pass drops a finding from its own record at the end of a pass in which it no longer holds, and no daily record shields it any more (the daily run does not make these checks), so a finding that ends and comes back with its item unwritten (the role map briefly empty, a pane that changes, an anchor or an after-item that changes) is sent again. The pass's record `<state>/audit/filing.json` (written atomically; it is no run, so `audit.runs`, `last` and `status` do not read it) and the newest daily record are merged (`merged_sent`), which matters only for a send an earlier daily run made; an item written since the send is sent again. The daily run no longer makes these checks (stuck-elsewhere, arbitrary-hold, mislabeled-state): they are the filing pass's alone, so a finding reaches its mover once, and the daily run's summary, its `by_category`, the unmoved digest, the `[audit] threshold` count and the 20% share of flagged items count only the checks the run makes. Idle-on-agent stays with the daily run. The pass does nothing while a daily run is in progress. The filing pass sends its messages from the runner's one thread, so a large first-run backlog holds the other rounds (the wake round included) until it is sent. The **dates pass** does two looks every ten minutes, each in its own try (the first error is raised after both have run, so the runner records it): it opens the For you due-date look's gate, and it looks for **an expected time that has passed with nobody acting** (`audit_checks.dates_findings`, `audit_run.dates_pass`, `server._dates_expected_tick`; it also needs `[audit] enabled`, script mode). An item's expected time is the one `waiting.py` derives from what the item carries: its wake (a dated wait's, a hold's wake included). The rule is the date half of the daily audit's stale check (`check_stale`, which now is only this date rule), with its exemptions kept: an item is found when it is an open item in the state doing whose wake has passed and which nobody has written since the wake; agent work in progress with no wake carries no expected time and is never past due; a wake still ahead, a hold with no wake, an item on the owner or an outside party, behind an open item, or in a state that is itself a wait (parked, outside, blocked, your-call, your-step, verifying) is left alone; one the idle pass poked is no finding, as in the daily run. A finding is the `stale` finding the daily run used to send for this, word for word, goes to the same recipient through the same `deliver_findings` (headed `Dates check <time>`), and is sent once while the item is not written to; the pass's record `<state>/audit/dates.json` (written atomically beside `filing.json`; no run, `dry` sends and writes nothing) and the newest daily record are merged (`merged_sent`), so a send an earlier daily run made still holds while its record is the newest; a finding the pass drops from its own record and that comes back is sent again, as in the filing pass. The pass does nothing while a daily run is in progress (what falls due in that time is sent by the first pass after the run).  No state-check note is stored on an item any more: the next-line words that are not refused (a stored `model_flags` list, read by nothing but the writer's reply) are derived from the stored next when the writer's reply or `get` needs them (`_state_check_notes`, `audit_checks.wait_words`), and a write that sets the next drops a legacy `model_flags` entry. Suites: `tests/filing_scenario.py`, `tests/dates_scenario.py`, `tests/audit_run_scenario.py` (the pin of what the daily run still checks, and that each moved check is made by its ten-minute pass alone), `tests/runner_scenario.py`. **An unanswered finding is not forgotten** (`audit_run.split_sent`, `[audit] remind_hours`, default 6, 0 = once only as before): a finding of the filing or dates pass that its item has not answered (no write since) is sent again that long after its last send, worded as a later try (`second try, no answer`, the ordinal in words: the router drops a message that repeats an earlier one but for numbers and dates), until it has had three sends (a send after a write counts too: each finding record carries `tries`, kept while the finding stands and gone when it ends; a reminder or escalation that is not reached keeps the finding on its old send and tries, `audit_run.carry_unreached`, so the next pass retries that same send); at the next due time after that it goes once to the project's builder as unmoved (the daily run's unmoved flag, `chase the mover (job)`), to no one else, and by the project's page key (where the router rules it) when the builder is the mover or the finding's recipient, never to the session that did not answer. **Level 2:** when the finding still stands unanswered at the next due time after that send and its recipient was a builder (a background agent), it goes once more, to the project's `-main` agent, marked `[for <owner>]`: `<id> · nobody answered after N messages · <category> · <detail> · <mover> writes it, or says what holds it` (`audit_run.deliver_findings`; `split_sent` sets `escalate` to 1 for the builder, 2 for `-main`; an unreached send keeps its tries like any other). A finding whose first recipient was the page key or a front agent (a front agent's finding, a retired or outside mover's) went to the builder at level 1 and ends there, as does one whose builder is `-main` itself; a builder-moved item's finding goes to the page key at level 1 (never to the mover) and then to `-main`. Only a builder-level send made with the level kept (`lvl: 1` on the finding's record, read by `audit_run.levels_before`) is followed by level 2, so a finding already escalated before this level existed stays quiet and a release sends no burst. After level 2 it is quiet until the item is written. The silence pass has its own ladder and sends none of these. Suite: `tests/front_step_move_scenario.py` (8).

**The front-step rule** (`front_step.py`, `audit_run.front_step_pass`, the server's `_front_step_mark` and `_front_step_move`; part of the ten-minute filing pass, so it also needs `[audit] enabled`, script mode). A stuck-elsewhere finding about a front agent's step goes to the project's page key and is sent once (`audit_run.recipient`); the mover only relays it and the ladder gives a front agent a History line, so nothing changed the board and such a step could stay open for as long as the item did. The rule moves it. **Scope:** a stuck-elsewhere finding whose step opens with a front agent or a keeper (role `front` or `keeper` in the role map, `audit_run.FRONT_ROLES`) or a retired session, on an item whose mover is a session that is not retired. Not the owner's steps, not a background agent's (it gets the finding itself and can answer it), and not an item the owner moves or one with no session as mover (there is nobody to move the step to). **Clock:** the first pass that sees the finding for a step stores `metadata.stuck_since = {"step": <hash>, "mover": <the item's mover>, "at": <epoch>}` on the item (written only after the server has read the item again under its lock and found the finding still holding) (the hash is twelve hex digits of the step as the checklist compares it); it is the server's own bookkeeping, a save with no writer, so it moves no activity clock and writes no notice. A write to the item by anyone does not restart it, only a change of that step does: a pass that no longer sees the finding for the step (the step done, dropped or reworded, the opener changed, the item closed) drops the record, and a reworded step starts a clock of its own. A clock kept for another mover reads as none: when the item goes to a new mover while the front agent's step stays first, the clock starts afresh under the new mover, so it is not handed the step minutes after it took the item. **Threshold:** `[audit] front_step_move_hours`, default 3, a decimal reads, 0 = off (a pass with the setting at 0 only drops clocks it finds, so switching it on again starts clean). **Action:** when `now - at` reaches the threshold and the finding still holds, the server reads the item again under its lock, and moves nothing unless the item's mover is still the one the pass saw and the stuck-elsewhere finding still holds on the item as it is now (the same check, so an item handed to the opener, parked, held or put on a wait since the pass read it is left alone), and rewords the step so its first line opens with the mover (the list marker, bold marks, the rest of the line and the lines under it kept; a role in parentheses after the old name is dropped), through the pieces an `update(reword=…)` uses: `nextlines.reword` (the line is the mover's own from then on: `reword` takes a `new_writer`), `steps.apply` for the checklist, and the step's proof moved to the new text. Nothing moves when the item is closed, the first open step is another one, the new text would read like another open step, or the first line has no opener to replace. It writes one History line (`metadata.stall.log`, drawn as `stackshot: …`): `step moved from <opener> to <mover> after N hours open with the stuck-elsewhere finding unanswered (reword it back to undo): "<the step>"`, so the original opener is on record and a reword puts it back. The step's hash goes into `metadata.stuck_moved` (the last five), the clock is dropped, and the step moves **once**: worded back to the front agent it is a finding again, with no clock and no second move. **Messages:** the mover gets one plain message per moved step, `fyi` (it starts no turn for a background agent), from the filing pass; nobody else is told, not the front agent and not the page key, and a mover that is itself a front agent is not messaged at all (the History line is the record: a front agent hears of its item at two moments only). **The finding** of a step still waiting says so: its fix line reads `take the step, or reword it to <mover>; if it is still open then, it moves to <mover> at <time>` (the finding is not tool text; the time is the clock's start plus the threshold, in the instance zone; a finding whose clock could not be written, or whose move was refused, promises no time and keeps its plain fix line). With no roles at all (the role map is briefly empty now and then) the rule does nothing and drops no clock; without the item writers (`Env.step_mark`, `Env.step_move`: the command line) it does nothing; a dry run starts and moves nothing. The pass's record, `filing.json`, carries `front_steps` (`hours`, `started`, `moved`, `cleared`, `errors`, `messages`) when the rule did something. **After the move** the server leaves `metadata.stuck_landed = {step, mover, at}` (the reworded step's hash, the mover, the time; `_REPARK_QUIET`, no writer) and the silence pass chases the mover from it: its 45 minutes (`silence.LIMIT`) count from the move (`silence.clock`), and a wake no longer silences the step (`front_step.overdue`: a step that was moved and not acted on since (the mover writing the item ends it, the step unchanged; so does turning the rule off), or whose clock is running, and that names no date of its own; a step dated for the wake's day or later stays the wake's business), so a mover who does not act is told, once per silence, and put on the one ladder with no person asking; the finding's evidence reads `no write for N minutes and a wake that does not cover the step in hand`, followed by the ways out (write the item, hold it, store a wait, date the step or reword it). A reword of the step, the mover's own activity or a new mover ends it. Decisions: a front mover is moved to but not messaged; the owner's own steps are left to the existing finding; the clock lives on the item rather than in a file of the pass, so it survives a restart and shows with the item. Suite: `tests/front_step_move_scenario.py`.

**The front-step write check** (`front_step.held`, `server._gate_front_step`, called from `_update_unlocked` and `_create_item`; one of the model's write guards, so `[model] guards = false` switches it off). The front-step rule repairs a step a front agent already holds; this check stops a new one at the write. A session's `create` or `update` is refused when it adds or rewords an open step (a line of `next`, a `reword`) whose opener is a front agent (role `front`, not retired) and the item's mover is another session: a front agent gets no work notice and its own reminder wakes are refused, so such a step can never fire. Judged: only a step not already open before the write (compared as the checklist compares steps, so a re-sent line, a rebullet or an unrelated write passes), and only a session's tool write (`_SESSION_NEXT`), never the server's own writes, a script's sender or a done mark. Allowed: the front agent is the item's own mover, the opener is a background agent, the owner or an outside party, the item has no session as mover, or the mover is retired. A failing roles read lets the write through with one stderr line (`front-step gate: ... not judged`). The refusal reads `Refused: the step "<first line>" opens with <agent>, a user-facing agent of <project>: it gets no work notice and its own wakes are refused, so nothing would wake it for the step, and <mover> moves <item>.` followed by `Give the step to <mover> (open it with "<mover>:") or send <mover> a message asking for it.`, or, when the mover itself wrote the step, `Keep the step with <mover> and message <agent> for what only it can give.` **The monthly count:** `audit_run.front_step_count`, the first run of each calendar month (instance zone), puts `front_step_count` (`month`, `days`, `closed`, `by_other`, `open`, `ids`) on the run record and one line in the run's summary to the stackshot project: `front-held steps, last 30 days: X of Y closed by someone else · Z open step(s) the write check would refuse now (ids)`. `closed` is the steps opening with a front agent that were closed (done or dropped) in the last 30 days, `by_other` those closed by a session other than that agent (a server mark or an unsigned close is no session), `open` the open steps on open items the check would refuse now (`front_step.month_count`). Suites: `tests/front_step_refuse_scenario.py`, `tests/front_step_move_scenario.py` (level 2).

**The silence pass** (`silence.py`, `server._silence_tick`, a ten-minute entry after the dates pass; it needs `[audit] enabled`, script mode). It replaces the daily run's two silence rules, the stale check's 12 hours without a write on an item with no date of its own and the board report's idle-on-agent (6 hours), with one clock on the item: `activity.at`, the time of its last real activity (a step done or added, a state change, a new next step or description, the owner's answer, a message written onto the item, an event on one of its anchors). An audit's own write and a bare confirm do not move it, so the pass's message cannot reset the clock it reads. An open item in the state doing, on a session, with no activity for 45 minutes (`silence.LIMIT`) is found; an item on hold, behind an open item, with a stored wait (a release, a CI run, any event wait, a relay, an outside party), with a wake of its own (a wake ahead is its expected time; one that passed is the dates pass's; but a wake never silences a first open step that is already overdue and names no date of its own, a step the front-step rule moved or found stuck: `front_step.overdue`) or with a hand-off nobody took yet (the hand-off audit's alone) is left alone. The count starts at the later of that activity and the moment the item left a hold, a wait or an after link (a wake, a lifted hold and a released after link are saved by the server with no writer, so the activity clock does not move for them): an item parked for a day is not found the round it wakes. Silence is judged per item, never per pane: the pass does not read the mover's pane, so a busy pane (a builder directing subagents is nearly always busy), a background agent's `shell` pane or a pane this box does not show hides no silent item. Before it sends, the pass asks one view (`server._silence_skip`): an item the stall ladder is already on (`_stall_active`) is left alone, and a view that cannot be built sends nothing (fail open). A subagent's work that writes nothing to the item is not activity, so a builder keeps a long job visible by writing it onto the item; one such write is read beyond the clock: an item whose mover is its project's builder and which carries a `work` line in progress (changed within 90 minutes, `silence.subagent_running`; a job that runs longer changes the line's words or writes to the item, since a line sent again unchanged keeps its old time) has a build subagent running on it and counts as taken, while a builder's item with no such line and no event in 45 minutes is silent whatever its pane shows. A finding is the daily run's `stale` finding in its wording ("no write for N minutes and no date of its own", the same fix; a span past four hours reads in hours and minutes), so the two share one `finding_key`; it goes to the mover (else the project's -main agent) through `deliver_findings`, once per silence episode: an episode ends when the item has activity again, and the send stamp is compared with the item's activity, not with its last write. The pass's record is `<state>/audit/silence.json` (`started`, `findings` with their send stamps, `carried`: the stamps of findings that went away with no activity on their item, so a wait filed and lifted does not restart the episode); like the filing and dates passes it does nothing while a daily run is in progress, and the stall pass stays (it watches the pane: a session with no turn running, the ladder to the cold call and the builder; with `[ladder] silence` on it takes the rungs of the items this pass brings instead of starting its own). By default it sends the ask rung only, as one message per silence episode; with `[ladder] enabled` and `[ladder] silence` both true that message is the ask of the one ladder and the item goes on it (below). Suites: `tests/ladder_silence_scenario.py`, `tests/silence_scenario.py`, `tests/runner_scenario.py`, `tests/runner_order_scenario.py`.

**The stall pass** (`stall.py`, `server._stall_tick`). Every `[stall] every_seconds` (20; the stall pass runs on the runner's 60-second pace, so a smaller value still runs once per round) it looks at every open, live item whose mover is a session, front agents included. **The signal** is the CLI's registry (`~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json`, read by `server._handoff_status`, the same read the hand-off clock uses): the mover's live process has `status: idle` (or `shell`, which the CLI writes for a pane whose turn is over but which has a `run_in_background` Bash task running; a background subagent keeps the pane `busy`, which is left alone) and its `statusUpdatedAt` is `[stall] minutes` (10) old or more. A pane that starts a turn goes busy and, back to idle, has a later `statusUpdatedAt`, so a turn starting restarts the count; an item that only just reached its mover counts from then, not from the pane's age. A pane the registry does not show on the server's box (another machine's, a dead process) says nothing, and its items are left alone. **The ladder**, each step `minutes` after the one before it, counted from the later of the pane going idle, the item reaching its mover and the previous step: (1) the notice goes to the mover once more (one message per mover, items batched; **a front agent is sent none**, because no item notice wakes a front agent: its step 1 is the History line alone, and steps 2 and 3 cover its item; at most `[stall] max_per_tick` (5) first steps a tick, the rest on the next ticks (the cap is per tick, so with one tick a minute a burst of 30 idle panes after a deploy takes about 6 minutes); **no clock starts before the pass first ran**, recorded under `_pass` in `<state>/stall.json`, so a deploy or a restart does not step every long-idle pane at once); (2) the hand-off's cold call (`server._handoff_cold`, through the same writer, [model] `cold_call`) names who takes it, at most once per item in 24 hours (**a stall cold call never asks the owner**: the record's `stall` marker gives it `cold_call.STALL_RUBRIC`, which offers `hand_to` and `close` only and answers `none` when unsure; an `ask_owner` answer, a `none`, an answer it could not give or a write the item refused is not written, so no `needs`, ask or mover changes, a `stall:` journal line and a History line say "the cold call named no taker: to the builder", and the item goes on to step 3 at once; the ordinary hand-off cold call keeps its three answers); (3) the project's builder (`server._handoff_builder`, through `_handoff_exec`, which reads the builder as section 5 does); (4) when none of them can take it, the last rung: a History line ("stackshot: …") and a journal line, and ONE message to the fleet's builder (`[fleet] operator_session`, `server._recovery_tell`), first line `[stackshot] fleet recovery: <item> …`, naming the item, its mover, why and what was tried, and asking it to take the fix through its usual path. The hand-off record carries it (`recovery_at`), so the pass sends it once, and the mark is written before the send on purpose (a failed send is not retried every pass: `metadata.undelivered` records it and the audit's unmoved digest is the backstop); the History line is written after the send and says what happened (asked, or why nothing was sent); a write by the item's mover, or the item moving on, ends the record and a later episode may send another. The item stays with its mover and does not go to the owner; no list of his carries it. A stall record (`metadata.handoff.stall`) runs no clock of the hand-off ladder (`no_clock`) and never returns the item to a sender. **Not looked at:** an item on hold, on a wait or moved by the owner or an outsider (the model derives no session mover for them), a hand-off in flight to a session that is not a front agent (the hand-off ladder, `[model] handoff_minutes`, owns it), a cold call running, **an item whose work is not due now, whatever its mover's role** (`stall.not_due`, read from the item model's own fields: a `wake` (check-back) still ahead, an `expected_by` still ahead (an idle pane is not a stalled one: a builder that ended its turn while a background task runs shows `shell`, which counts as idle once the item is due, and one with a background subagent running shows `busy`; an item past its expected-by shows as past due in get and on the board), a hold or a park, an `after` link to an item still open, a wait on the owner (`needs` decision, his step) or on someone outside (`needs` external). Such an item gets no step and **no record is kept for it**, so a leftover ladder position does not carry over: when it falls due (the wake or expected-by passes, the hold lifts, the answer comes) its clock starts then, the mover's idle time counted from the later of the pane going idle and that moment), an item already raised in the last 24 hours, and an item whose pane status is unknown. Every step is written on the item (`metadata.stall.log`, one History line each, "stackshot: …"; the last three show in `get` as `stall:` lines) and a `stall:` line in the journal; the episode's progress is in `<state>/stall.json` (an entry restarts when anyone writes the item or its mover changes). It needs `[model] handoff` on and not paused. **A pause of the pass always ends on its own** (a pause that is forgotten would otherwise leave idle builders asleep). Two keys pause it, both read live on a config reload: `[stall] paused_until` (an ISO-8601 time with an offset, such as `"2099-03-14T21:00+00:00"`) holds the pass until then and it runs again with no edit; `[stall] enabled = false` holds it for at most `[stall] max_pause_hours` (default 4) counted from when the server first saw the pause (the reload or start that read it; the start time is kept in memory and in `<state>/stall_pause.json`, so a restart does not reset the clock). The cap covers a `paused_until` too: one further out than `max_pause_hours` from when it was first seen is cut to the cap, and a changed setting is a new pause seen at that moment. After the end the pass runs although the file still says `enabled = false`; edit the file to start another pause. The journal has one line when a pause starts, `stall: paused until Fri Mar 14th 2098 21:00 (paused_until)` (the reason is `paused_until`, `enabled=false, capped at 4 h` or `paused_until, capped at 4 h`), and one when it ends, `stall: pause ended, the pass runs again`, which is also a line on the routing log (ruled `dropped` by `stackshot (auto)`, under the page of the fleet operator's project, `[fleet] operator_session`). A reload logs `config reload: warning: the stall pass is paused and takes no step until …`, and `./stackshot doctor` and the server's start print a `[stall] the stall pass is paused …` note with the end time. **An agent never switches the pass off**. The server honors `enabled = false` or a `paused_until` still ahead only when `[stall] pause_item` names a board item that carries the owner's own answer approving a pause (`metadata.answers`, written only by his board login or his GitHub comment, never by a session's `update`; the chosen option or the comment says "pause", and an unrelated answer of his does not count) dated no earlier than 4 hours before the server first saw the setting (an answer that comes only after the pause's cap has passed starts it then, with a new clock and one `stall: paused until … (started late …)` line); the cap on any pause is at most 24 hours (`max_pause_hours` above 24 is read as 24). Any other setting is refused: the pass keeps running, the journal has one line, `stall: pause refused, the pass keeps running: <why>` (once per config load, not per tick; a reload or a restart logs it again once), and `./stackshot doctor`, the server's start and a reload print the same reason. **When the pass takes a wrong step on an item**, an agent (1) undoes the step on the item with `update`: a hand-off (`cold`, `builder`) is undone by setting `mover` and `next` back, a close by `status=open` with a new `next`, a recovery request needs nothing undone (the message to the fleet's builder cannot be unsent; the item stayed with its mover); the `stall:` line in the item's History stays as the record; a notice already sent (`resend`) cannot be unsent and needs nothing; (2) fixes the cause through a normal PR to stackshot (`not_due`, `action` or the registry signal read the item wrong, which is code), handed to my-engineer; (3) if wrong steps reach the owner before the fix lands, asks him for a pause that ends by itself: he answers on a board item, and `pause_item` names it. The same text is in `stall.py`'s docstring and in the `get` tool (the `update` tool has a short clause pointing to it).

**The one ladder** (`ladder.py`, `server._ladder_*`, `[ladder] enabled`, off by default). The hand-off ladder (a reminder for a background agent, the project's builder, the cold call, the sender, the fleet's builder asked) and the stall ladder (the notice again, the cold call, the builder, the fleet's builder asked) were two orders on two records, and an item could climb both. With `[ladder] enabled = true` an item whose mover went quiet has one record, its entry in `<state>/stall.json` (its `via` says which audit brought it: `handoff` for a hand-off nobody took, `stall` for a pane with no turn running), and one order: (1) **ask**: the mover is sent the notice again (a hand-off's own notice for an untaken hand-off, the stall notice for a silent pane; a front agent gets the History line alone); (2) **look**: `[ladder] look_minutes` (30) after the ask, and only then, the pane is looked at: with no turn running the notice goes again, with a turn running nothing is sent and the look comes when the turn ends; (3) **cold**: `[stall] minutes` (10) later the cold call names who takes it (it never asks the owner); (4) **builder**: `[stall] minutes` later, or at once when the cold call named no taker, the project's builder takes the item; with none able, ONE message asks the fleet's builder to recover the session, the item stays with its mover and is not put to the owner. The two audits that bring an item stay apart: the hand-off tick (`_handoff_tick`) brings an untaken hand-off (not taken in `[model] handoff_minutes`, a turn that ended with no write, a gone pane, two failed pushes: those last two start at the cold call, there being nothing to ask), the stall tick (`_stall_tick`) a silent pane, and both take the rungs after the first here. An untaken hand-off to a session is the hand-off audit's alone (the stall tick does not start it) and the hand-off tick leaves an item already on the ladder, so one item never climbs two ladders at once. The sender rung is gone (an untaken hand-off is not sent back to the session that handed it over), and so is the reminder as a step of its own: a background agent is asked like any mover, so one whose turn ended with no write is now asked after the idle grace period rather than after the `handoff_minutes` clock, and the reminder's extra wait is gone. A write to the item by anyone but the mover, or the mover's own confirm (at most `stall.RESTARTS` times), restarts the episode but keeps the item on the ladder: the ask is not sent a second time, the look comes `look_minutes` after the last rung taken, and the hand-off tick does not bring the item again; the mover starting work on the item takes the hand-off and ends the ladder, as does a new mover, and the entry is for the hand-off it was made for (`ho_at`: a later hand-off of the item to the same mover starts a ladder of its own). The rungs after the ask are taken by the stall tick, so while the stall pass is paused (`[stall]` paused, or `[model] handoff` off) the ladder waits where it is and goes on when the pass runs again; the cold call is made at most once per item in 24 hours. With the flag off both ladders run as they did; turning it off in the middle of a ladder hands the item back to them, the stall ladder reading the ladder's steps in its own shorter table (`ladder.unwind`: a builder rung still to come stays reachable). The idle pass leaves an item the ladder has a step on for the whole ladder (`_stall_active`). **The silence switch** (`[ladder] silence`, off by default, and it does nothing unless `[ladder] enabled` is true as well; only a TOML true turns it on, read at use): the ladder has a third way in, and the pane with no turn running stops being one. With it on, the ten-minute silence pass (an open item on an agent with no activity for 45 minutes) and the dates pass (an item past its own expected time with no write since) send their message to the mover as before, and for each fresh finding that reached someone (`silence_pass(entered=)`, `audit_run.dates_pass(entered=)`, `server._ladder_enter_quiet`) the item goes on the ladder with `via` = `silence`: that message is the ask, so the entry starts on the look, with its clock started when the message went and a History line ("asked for news"). The look comes `look_minutes` (30) later, and only if the mover's pane has no turn running (a turn running waits; a front agent gets the History line alone); the mover is told once more in the silence wording ("nothing has been written to it for N minutes and your pane has no turn running"; `server._ladder_quiet_look`), then the cold call `[stall] minutes` after the look, then the project's builder, then ONE message to the fleet's builder, the item staying with its mover and off the owner's list, as above. The entry is for this silence alone: it ends when the item has activity (its `activity` clock moves past the entry's `act`) or its mover changes, so a bare confirm by the mover and a write that is no activity restart nothing (the stall pass's `RESTARTS` do not apply to it), and a leftover entry of the cold call's 24-hour bound is kept. A pane with no turn running no longer starts a ladder (`_stall_tick` only takes the rungs of an entry the silence audit or the hand-off audit made; an untaken hand-off is still the hand-off audit's). A finding sent before the switch was turned on is not entered (no flood: such an item climbs from its next silence). Turning `silence` off (with `enabled` on) lets an entry already made finish its rungs and brings the pane trigger back; turning `enabled` off reads the entry in the old stall table (the look maps to the cold call). Suite: `tests/ladder_silence_scenario.py`.

**The hand-off record follows the mover** (`handoff.follow`, `server._handoff_follow`, called by `_save` on every save). An item has one open hand-off record (`metadata.handoff`), and it never names someone but the item's effective mover: a session's write hands over through `handoff.apply` as before; any other save ends a record that names a mover the item left (it goes to `handoff_history` as "handed on, not taken", or "the item no longer has a mover"), and a save of the server's own that changes the mover to a session (a wake that makes the post-wake mover the mover, an event wake, the stall pass, a release of `after`) records the hand-off to the new mover, from the server, marked `no_clock`: **the 10-minute take clock still starts only on a session's hand-off**, never on a server-made record, and the timing of a woken mover stays with the stall pass, which leaves a busy pane alone. A taken record to the same name does not count as already naming the mover: a wake that hands the item back to a session that took it before makes a new record (the taken one goes to the history as "handed on"). The record is marked delivered only when a notice actually reaches the mover: a record to a front agent, one whose wake notice is held, and one whose notice goes to a session in needs other than the mover stay undelivered. The owner's answer, a script's save and a hand-back out of the owner's state end a stale record and make none; a record that carries a recovery request (the ladder's last rung) of an open item is left, unless a save of the server's own moves the item on to another session: that ends it as "handed on" and records the new mover (so the stall pass is not silent for it); the open record of a closed item is ended. Delivery is recorded on every notice path: the message tool, the router's push, the keeper's page-owed batch and the anchors prompt mark the open record of an item the text carries the notice link of to that session, and the message tool one the text cites (`server._handoff_told_text`; a batch that only cites another item in a subject marks nothing for it), so a lost notice can be told from a fast take. Records the older code left are repaired by a pass in the server's start (`server._handoff_repair_pass`, after the mover migrations and before the wake pass and any thread; idempotent, one log line with the count): it ends each open record that names someone but the mover and makes nothing new. `tools/handoff_repair.py --store <copy of the store>` is the rehearsal (a dry run unless `--apply`); `--live` is a read-only dry run, and `--live --apply` is refused while the user unit `stackshot.service` is active.

**A hand-off is taken only by work started** (`handoff.started`, `handoff.touch`, called by `_save` through `handoff.apply`). The mover's write takes its open hand-off only when the write shows work begun: a step marked done (the checklist's own mark), a work line (`update work={…}`) new or moved to `in progress` or `done`, or the item closed. An anchor scoped to the item added since the hand-off counts through `plan` as before. A note, a confirm, a reword of `next` or the description, a queue or wait line, a hold or wake, a dropped or new step and a timeline line do not, so they leave the 10-minute clock and its reminders running; the sentence of a note is never read, only the marks. The hand-off notices tell the mover so.

**A background agent that does not take a hand-off is reminded first** (with `[ladder] enabled` off only: on the one ladder there is no separate reminder, see above) (`handoff.plan`, `handoff.remind`, `server._handoff_exec`). When a hand-off to a background agent is due and not taken (delivered and `[model] handoff_minutes` old; for such an agent a turn that ended with no write does not hurry it, the clock alone does), the first step is one reminder to that agent's own session, through the normal push: the item was handed over, no work on it has started, start its subagent alongside the current work. The recipient is checked fail closed (`server._handoff_reminder_target`): the name must resolve to a live session that the role map lists with a background role and not retired, so a front agent, a page's name-line session, an unknown name, a keeper entry, or an unreadable role map gets no reminder and the ladder is what it was. The record gets `reminded_at`, its delivery is forgotten and the clock restarts from the reminder's own push result (delivered, or queued plus the inbox grace); a reminder whose push fails twice goes down the ladder. After a reminder only the clock counts (not the turn-end rule): when the item is still not taken after another `handoff_minutes`, section 5 goes on as before (the project's builder, the cold call, the sender, then the recovery request to the fleet's builder). Several reminders due in one pass for one agent go in one message. A new hand-off of the same item is a new record and is reminded again. An anchor scoped to the item and added at or after the hand-off counts as taken, for every mover (a pull request in flight is a build started; an older anchor says nothing about this hand-off): such an item is neither reminded nor moved on; the anchors are read once per pass and only for an item that would act. A `no_clock` record (one the server made) and a front agent stay without a clock.

**Doing is work in hand, not a wait, and carries no expected time.** An item in the stored state `doing` shows how long it has run (the item page's "since 2h ago", the home row's age) and nothing else: no expected time is set, kept or shown, so it is never "past due", and a far time cannot make it read as Waiting on the home page. The board keeps a date only for a wait on an outside party, a date or a person: a wake, a hold, `after`, a relay wait with its `ask_by` day. Work in hand takes `next` and an agent session in `needs`; `expected_by` and `expected_reason`, which older calls pass, are accepted and not stored, and the reply carries one note saying so and naming the park calls. When an `update` leaves an item in the stored state `doing` (not on hold) and a line of `next` it writes reads as a wait on a time or an event ("overnight", "in the morning", "nightly", "the next run", "once the run finishes", "until 09:00", "waiting on the 09:00 timer", `model_guards.reads_as_wait`), the reply carries one note, never a refusal, saying to park it that turn with `update(hold=true, wake=…, wait_basis=…)` (a valid reason to wait on an item does not warrant marking it as on agent and letting it sit there for hours: it should be marked waiting). "Until" and "waiting on" count only with a time or a day after them: a builder waiting on its own CI or review, or running tests until green, is work in hand and gets no note. A create takes no state, so it carries none. The wait's kind for a doing item is `doing` (no date, never overdue; the items API carries `expected_by: null` for it), and an item `after` a doing item is expected by 12 hours after that item's last write (`waiting.AFTER_DOING_SECONDS`; the later of its `updated` and `state_updated`, so any write by its mover moves the clock), not by a cap counted from the waiter's own last move. A quiet builder is nudged at once: the stall pass no longer waits for an expected time to pass. A doing item stored before this keeps its old `expected_by` on disk, which nothing reads; the one-time pass that parked those whose stored `expected_by` was far ahead (`tools/move_waiting_doing.py`) went with the state inputs, and such an item is parked by hand with `update(hold=true, wake=…, wait_basis=…)`. A bare-date wake is the start of the next day, 00:00 in the instance zone. **Derived waits on an agent are hours too** (a due the server worked out was arbitrary and too distant): when the wait is on an agent session (the mover is a session) and the item is not stored as doing, the date is `agent_hours` (3) from when the wait was set, never `default_days` or `cap_days`: a needs wait on a session reads "needs set + the agent hours", a check "check set + the agent hours", and both are out of the idle pass too. A wait on the owner or an outside party, a due date, a wake and a hold keep the day-based rule. A derived agent wait counts its hours from the item's state clock, or from `metadata.agent_from` when that is later (the rank cleanup at the server's start stamps it, so an old wait does not read as overdue at once). A check counts as an agent wait when `needs` names any session (no special case for the item's creator, who is no one's mover); with empty needs it keeps the 7-day cap. A derived agent wait is also out of the idle pass (so there is no second notice); items with no session in needs stay the idle pass's.

**A doing item that only waits for a later day reads as waiting** (`dated_wait.py`, shown by `model.view`). A `doing` item (stored, or read as doing from its fields when none is stored, as a mover and a wake do; mover a session, not on hold) whose check-back `wake` is more than `[waiting] dated_wait_min_hours` (1) ahead, and whose every open step of `next` names a date (`states.named_dates`: `Mon Mar 2nd`, `Mar 2`, `2099-03-02`) that the step says it waits for (a waiting word stands directly before it, or with one filler word `the`, `a` or `on` between, in the same clause: `after Mar 3`, `until the 2099-03-03`; `after`, `once`, `until`, `till`, `when`, `following`, `wait`; a bracket ends the clause and a waiting word further back never counts, so `merge once CI is green (Mar 3)` and `finish the notes after lunch for the 2099-03-03 release` are no wait; or it opens the step, at the line start or right after the `<name>:` opener, with a waiting word right after it: `Mon Mar 2nd after the run`; never one right after `by`, `due`, `before`, `closed`, `since`, `on`, `eta` or `target`, one followed by `deadline` or `due`, or one behind a comma, colon or dash after other text, as in `deadline: Mar 3` or `Mar 3 - draft the notes`, which are deadlines or references) and that is a later calendar day than today, not before the day of that wake and at most 60 days after it (a yearless date that `named_dates` rolled a year ahead is no wait), is listed as waiting on a time until the wake, a wait on a time: no write, the stored state stays doing, `derive` is unchanged for the guards and the router, and the brief counts these items on a line, the silence pass skips them (a wake of its own), and `get` and the brief carry the tail `· the board lists it as waiting until <time> (every open step is dated for later (<date>))` on the `model:` line. One open step with no date means the agent can act now: the item stays doing. So does a wake less than the hour away, an item with no open step, and a step dated today or in the past; a step with several dates counts by its earliest; a time of day in a step is not read, the day decides. The daily audit (`audit_run.py`) treats such an item as a suspect of the check `why-not-now` (ranked with obsolete-wait alone, after the other checks; at most five a run, inside the `[audit] max_calls` cap; its signature is the dated lines plus the wake) and asks its mover whether something concrete keeps the step from running before that time (an external event, someone else's run, a scheduled job) or the agent could do it now; a finding goes to the mover like the others. A wait has to justify itself. A stored `doing` item that is on hold with a wake more than the hour ahead is listed as waiting on that wake as well (`dated_wait.held_wait`, basis `on hold until its wake`; its steps are not read); a hold with no wake, or a wake already past or within the hour, leaves it as it was. **A wake that carries a `wait_basis` is a wait, hold or not** (an item on hold keeps the reason `on hold until its wake`, with or without a basis) (`dated_wait.basis_wait`): a `doing` item (stored, or read as doing; mover a session; no `wait_kind`, no `needs` of `decision` or `external`, no `after` link; not on hold, which `held_wait` reads) whose wake is still ahead and has a `wait_basis` written for that very wake (`waiting.basis_of`) is listed as waiting on a time until the wake, the basis text as the reason (the item's row and `get` show it, and who set it; this reading is for the item that is not on hold), with no wake floor of an hour and none of its steps read. The basis names what the item waits for ("the contract renews on 2099-01-20"), which a bare wake does not: a wake with no `wait_basis` is a reminder and the item stays active. Once the wake has passed the item reads as it is stored again (the wake pass tells its sessions and drops the wake and its basis). It is the same model-layer reading (`model.view`) as the two above, so the home page's lists, the brief, `get`, the wire `list` and the Waiting sort agree; `derive`, the guards and the router still read it as stored, and the daily audit, which asks about dated-step suspects only, does not ask about it. A write that sets such a wake gets none of the notes that a wake alone draws (the window note, the timed-step note), and the brief's advice about doing items with dated steps does not count it.

**An item opens with its goal** (`server._gate_goal`, `bullet_text.first_bullet` and `goal_of`, the audit check `goal-unclear` in `audit_run.py`, the report `tools/goal_pass.py`; the gate is off with `[description] require_bullets = false`). The subject is the outcome wanted ("Make X work when Y"), never the symptom ("X fails when Y"), and the description's first `- ` bullet opens `Goal:` and says the same outcome in a sentence; a symptom, cause or trigger goes in a later bullet that opens `Problem:`. A session's `create`, and an `update` that replaces the description with text other than what the item already holds, is refused when its first `- ` bullet (a quote line or a fenced block before it is passed over) does not start `Goal:` (case-sensitive, right after the dash, with text after it); a description with no bullet is refused the same way, after the bullet-shape gate has spoken. The refusal names the first six words it saw, says the subject and the first bullet state the outcome, and gives the "Fix X fails when Y" to "Make X work when Y" example. Never judged: an `update` that leaves the description out, a close or `complete`, a subject set alone, scripts (a sender starting `a script on `, the client API's question route, any other script label) and the server's own writes (watchers, post-wake asks, the cold call, the re-bullet route), and the items already on the board. **The audit's `goal-unclear` check** (one model call per suspect, `audit_checks.goal_view`/`goal_sig`, `audit_run.goal_evidence`) asks about an open item that has a `Goal:` first bullet and whose (subject, Goal sentence) pair is not the one the record's `goal_judged` holds for it: does each name an outcome wanted, and do the two agree? Items written for another reason are not asked again; a reworded subject or Goal sentence is; an item with no Goal bullet is not a suspect; at most `GOAL_MAX` (10) a run, ranked with `obsolete-wait` and `why-not-now`, and the cap of calls holds. A yes goes to the item's mover like the other model checks, with the fixed fix text (reword the subject and the first bullet, put the symptom under `Problem:`). **The one-time pass** `tools/goal_pass.py [--dry-run] [--max-calls N] [--out FILE] [--store DIR]` asks the same judge about EVERY open item once, the ones with no Goal bullet included, prints id, verdict, reason, subject and first bullet to stdout and to `<paths.state>/audit/goal-pass-<stamp>.txt`, and is report only: it files nothing, sends no message and writes no audit record.

**No clock renewal without progress** (`server._gate_clock_renewal`, in the update tool's write path; a session's write only, off with `[model] guards = false`). A write that moves a `wake` or an `ask_by` to a LATER time than the item holds, while the item's reason (the description or the `wait_basis` text; a `wait_reason` does not count, it is not stored) and its open steps (the open lines of `next`) are as they were, is refused: re-dating an item is how work sits unseen. The refusal names the ways out.
