Change a step in the same write (`done=[…]`, `dropped=[…]`, a new line), state the new reason, or send the owner's own words in `said` (an Acked-by entry checked against his transcript, recent, and a message the item does not hold yet: an unchecked or Reported-by entry, or a message already stored whatever its quote, does not count), or change what the wait stands on to a shape the item never held (`wait_kind`, `wait_event`, `wait_anchor`, `after`; the last few shapes are kept in `metadata.clock_struct`, so a flip back to an earlier one, or removing `after` or `wait_event`, does not count; a `recycle` or `fleet_op` `wait_event` is told by its verb, not its machine argument, and a `pr_*` one by its full target; such a change of shape alone lets a clock push through twice in a row and the third is refused (`metadata.clock_struct_n` counts them; a changed step, the reason, a new owner message or a hand-off resets it); `post_wake_mover` is not part of it: who takes the item after is a hand-off, judged by the holder rules); or, if the item now waits, park it (`hold=true` with `wake` and `wait_basis`) or use the event-wait form (`wait_kind="event"`, `wait_event`, `ask_by`, `post_wake_mover`). The text fits the item: a parked item is told to give a new `wait_basis`, an item on a decision is not told to hold (it cannot be held). Setting a clock for the first time, pulling one in, re-sending the value the item holds, clearing a clock and a real hand-off are not renewals. A hand-off is a write after which the effective holder (the stored `mover`, else the first session in `needs`), resolved through the registry, is another session than before and is not the sender: setting the holder again, flipping it back, or writing the same session as a name and as an id is no hand-off. The third-re-park check on a parked item's wake (`_gate_repark`) stays: it counts the pushes a changed step lets through.

**On agent is work moving in real time; a window the work needs is a park** (work moving in real time is 'on agent', however long it takes, and a wait-and-see window the work itself needs is 'waiting' (parked); an item can sit on agent through a long log collection because its mover had only set a wake). A build, tests, a review, a release or a run in progress stays on agent however long it takes. A session parks its own item when the work itself needs the wait: a soak, a log or data collection window, a trial week, a timer it started. The form is `update(hold=true, wake="<when the window ends>", wait_basis="<what the window collects> ends <YYYY-MM-DD>")`: the hold with a wake and a `wait_basis` is a park (`states.derive`, `waiting` in the board), it passes `_gate_wait_basis` at any length up to a year, and the item shows as waiting until the wake, then returns to its mover. A session does not park to defer work it could do now: an item not on hold is one its mover can move now. A `wake` alone stays a reminder, never a park: use it for a real check-back on work the session keeps moving. An item the model already reads as a time wait without a hold flag (a verifying item, whose end date is its wait, or any item the model reads as a time wait), or a write that puts the item into the verifying state in the same call, takes `wait_basis` with a closer wake in one call, at any length, as a held item does (`_reads_time_wait`): the `snooze` guard asks that wait for its reason, and the basis is bound to the new wake and satisfies it; `tests/cap_words_scenario.py` section C. When a write sets a wake without hold and the item's next, as it now stands (the lines this call wrote and the stored ones), names that wake's day (`2099-03-02`, `Mar 2nd`, `2 Mar` or `3/2`, each as a whole word; a weekday only right after the waiting word, "wait until Monday") close behind a waiting word (after, once, wait, until, till, collect, soak, "then review": at most three words between, no comma, semicolon or bracket, and none of due, by, before or deadline; after "for N days" the day may follow a comma; `model_guards.reads_as_window`), the reply carries one note, never a refusal, saying the item stays on agent with a wake alone and giving the park form for a window the work itself needs. **A wake that is the whole of a mover's wait is parked by the write** (`server._date_only_wait`, `dated_wait.timed_wait_lines`, `model_guards.reads_as_timed_step(dated_only=True)`): when the sender is the item's mover and every open step of `next` opens with the wake's own clock or day ("at 08:00", "on <that day>", "on Fri Mar 6th", "on Fri" for the wake's weekday within the week ahead, "Mar 6: …", "tomorrow at 9"; matched at the line's start only, never after a "then", so "build it now, then at 08:00 verify" keeps the note; a day-only step on today itself can run now and is no wait, a clock time on today still counts), with the wake more than `[waiting] dated_wait_min_hours` ahead, no hold or wait_basis in the call and the item open, the write sets `hold` and a `wait_basis` itself (the call's `wait_reason` plus the wake day, else the step's own words) and the usual gates run on that park; the reply says so and names the way out (write the step as the action and pass `clear_hold=true`). A step that starts on an outside event keeps the note, and an action to take now, another party's step, a wake within the hour and a call that holds or gives its own basis are left as they were. "Merge the PR once CI is green; demo on Monday" and "ship after review, due 2099-03-02" draw none. The note is skipped for an item that is not on agent: one on hold, one on the owner's decision, one waiting after an open item, one with `needs=external`, and one stored as outside, verifying or blocked. A new `next` line that reads as a wait ("collect logs until 2099-03-02") is still refused by the `next_wait` guard, whose refusal names the hold, wake and reason, and for a window the work needs the `wait_basis` form above. `tools/board_report.py` reads a hold whose stored `wait_basis` carries a real YYYY-MM-DD date as naming its event, so a window park raises no arbitrary-hold finding.

**The For you list** (`foryou.py`). A notice that is for the owner himself is a row in a list, never a turn
of a front agent. Rows live in `<state>/for-you.json` (a small JSON file, written whole and swapped in with `os.replace`), newest first, at most 200;
a row marked seen leaves, and its key is remembered for 30 days so the same event does not come back. What makes a row: a watched item's close, reopen,
move to `needs: decision`, failed check (`wait_failed`) or a due date within 24 h (an urgent row). A failed release makes no row: the message `release.sh`
sends to `[release] report_to` (default `projects/example`) goes to the builder only, and the owner hears of it through the item the release serves, once past its expected-by. A row of a kind that is no longer listed (the old `release_failed`) is dropped when the file is read. A row is made when the state change is saved (`server._save` → `_foryou_on_save`), so a session's
write, the server's own pass and the done check all count; his own answer on the board makes no row, and takes the item's decision row off the list.
**Watched by default:** an item with a `said` entry, a quoted attribution or a board answer of his on it, and an item that waits on his decision.
**Watch / Mute** is per item, set on the item's page (a button each, behind the board's sign-in, the page's token and the board's own origin) and stored
in the same file; Mute stops rows for that item, Watch adds an unwatched one, Default removes the flag. The router's automatic `[for <owner>]` passes of
a close are gone with this (the `close-words` rule now only rules an old notice merged); a message a session sends on purpose with `[for <owner>]` is
not touched. **Slack:** stackshot has no Slack posting path of its own (anchor-hub only receives Slack), so an urgent row calls
`server._for_you_slack_post`, which runs `[for_you] slack_command` (an argv, the row's text on its standard input; empty = no Slack, the row only);
a row is posted once (`slacked`). The post runs on a worker thread of its own, in a session of its own, and a command that outlives `_FORYOU_SLACK_TIMEOUT` (30 s) is killed with its whole process group. A failed post is logged and tried again by the due-date pass, at most 3 tries in all (`slack_tries` on the row) and only while the row is under 24 h old. A `for-you.json` that does not parse is moved aside to `for-you.json.bad-<epoch>` and the list starts empty (ids go on from the highest one still readable, else from the clock). If a close with his words cannot be written as a row, the old `[for <owner>]` notice goes to the front owner and the writer's reply says so. The board page carries a "For you" section (item link, what happened, when in the instance timezone, a "seen" button, "mark all seen");
`client.py get for-you` prints `{"generated", "rows": [{id, kind, item, project, what, url, urgent, ts, when, slacked}], "flags": {item: "watch" | "mute"}}` as JSON.
**A notice's ruling**: a message to a project is answered `held for the router — the router rules notice <id> …`, ending `get(item_id="<id>") shows its ruling`; when the sender is the mover of the one open item the text names, the router merges it (nothing to deliver), so the reply says `NOT PASSED ON: you move <item> yourself …` and a hand-on of a step is made by writing the item (`router.own_relay`, the same routing the router applies). No word for a builder or worker handing back on an item another session moves (routine), for a `For <agent>:` message or the audit agent's batch (other rules rule those first), or for a sender that is no session. The `get` tool takes that notice id as well as an item id (`client.py get notice <id>` is the same read for a script) and shows who sent it and when (in the instance timezone), the project, a one-line excerpt (120 characters at most), the router's ruling (passed to whom, merged, held or dropped) with its reason, rule (a rule in code, or the router's call) and the item it went onto, who ruled it and when; a repeat dropped by the rule in code names the notice it repeats; a notice not yet ruled says it is still held and when the router expects to rule it; an unknown id answers `no notice <id>`. The ruling is the routing log's (`routing.lookup`: each project's file searched as bytes, only the lines that carry the id parsed, the live log's `key` tried first, about 1 to 3 ms against 45 ms for reading every file); the rule and item come from `router/live.jsonl` (`router_live.trace`).
**For the fleet tool's web server**, which holds the machine token and has no board cookie or page token, the same two writes are machine-token routes:
`POST /client/for-you/seen` (mark a row, or all, seen) and `POST /client/items/{key}/watch` (Watch / Mute / Default; `client.py post for-you-seen`
and `client.py post watch`). They are the New session form's caller only (`_form_call`: `sender` is a label in `[project_agent] form_senders` and
the token's machine is in `form_machines`; defaults: no sender label, so the form is off until `form_senders` names one, and no machine, `form_machines` = `[]`). Any other caller gets 403 with the reason: a session
that holds the machine token must not be able to mute his items or clear his list. Each change is logged to stderr with the sender and publishes the board.
(The label is the caller's own word, as for the form's other calls: the machine check is what the token proves.)

**A notice that asks nothing of a background agent is held for its next turn** (measured, such notices were a few percent of builder cost and up to half of them ended with nothing done). A background agent is an entry under `background:` in a page's agents map (`anchors.session_roles`, role `back`). A notice to one that asks nothing of it does not start a turn: it is held (`held.py`) and handed over with the agent's next turn, whatever causes that turn. The kinds (`held.KINDS`): `closed` (an item the agent moves or observes was completed with the outcome done by another session, `_tell_owner`, `_tell_observers`, `_tell_owner_change`; a background observer's close is held on purpose, like the owner's, since it asks nothing of the observer; a dropped or superseded item tells a builder to stop, so that close is delivered), `changed` (another session changed next, needs, check or after of an item the agent neither moves nor is named on), `queue` (a wake that leaves an item still after an open item, `_wake_tell`), `page-owed` (a page-update notice that owes the keeper nothing, such as the anchors notice of `_pilot_relay`, and the routing-log form the router passes; a notice that hands the debt to the keeper, "so it is yours as its keeper" or "the page update is yours as its keeper", is an ask and is delivered, and so is the page-debt fallback), `fyi` (a message marked `fyi=true`). Everything else wakes the agent as before: the audit's batch finding for an item's mover (see **The audit's findings** below), a notice that names it in needs, hands it an item (mover, hand-off), ends its wait, carries the owner's words (`[for <owner>]`, `needs: decision`, a `said` entry or a quote of his on the item, a `words` or `handback` mark), or asks something; so do a reopen, a reminder, a close that is not done, the page-debt fallback and any debt handed to the keeper, and any kind the code does not know. **Rollout gate:** a notice is held only for a session whose hook has fetched held lines (`held.mark_client`, recorded when the hook calls `/client/held`; `held.can_fetch`, valid 30 days). A machine on an old checkout, a cloud session and a session with no turn since the update get everything as before, so no line is stranded. **One decision point:** `held.decide(kind, is_background, text, owner_words, asks)`; the two places that ask it are `server._hold_notice` (the notices the server pushes itself: the senders call `_tell_or_hold(gid, owner, note, kind)`, or `_hold_notice` directly) and `router_live._hold_kind` (a pass of the router to a background agent: a notice recorded with `hold` (`routing.record(..., hold=…)`); an audit finding is never held there). A front agent is never affected. **The log:** a hold made by the server is a routing-log line ruled `held` by `stackshot (auto)`, reason `held: asks nothing (<kind>)`; a hold made by the router is the notice's own ruling `held`, by `stackshot (router)`, with the same reason (and `held_for`, `hold` on its `live.jsonl` line). **Where they live:** `<state>/held/<key>.jsonl`, one file per session (its permanent id when the registry resolves it, else its transcript id or name), one line each `{id, ts, kind, text, from, gid, nid}`: server-side state, so a pane's conversation cleared or recycled loses nothing and a restart of the server reloads it from the files. At most 100 lines a session (the oldest go) and none older than 7 days; every line dropped unread is a stderr line (`held: …`), and a file nothing has touched for 7 days (a retired session) is removed when anything is next held. **How they reach the turn:** `pages.py nag`, the `UserPromptSubmit` hook every `./stackshot client install` wires, asks the server (`POST /client/held`, the machine's token, `{uuid}`; the session must be one the server holds for that machine, as for `drain`, and its name is the server's record, never the request's; `client.py held_for`), prints the answer, which is the turn's context, and then acknowledges the ids it printed (a second `POST /client/held` with `{uuid, ack: [ids]}`, `client.py held_ack`). **At least once:** the server leases the lines it returns for 60 seconds and removes them only on the acknowledgement (`held.lease`, `held.ack`), so a line whose answer never arrived or whose hook died before printing is offered again; a duplicate is possible, a loss is not. Oldest first, one line each (time in the instance timezone, kind, text), at most 8 at a turn start and a last line `+N more, handed over at your next turn`. A session with nothing held gets an empty answer; the hook fails open. `[router] hold_notices = false` delivers everything as before. **The message tool's `fyi`:** `fyi=true` marks a message that asks nothing. To a background agent it is held (the reply begins `held for <agent>'s next turn`) and names the agent in no item's needs; to a project it is recorded with `hold: fyi`, and the router holds it when it passes it to a background agent; to a front agent it changes nothing (the front gate, the report check, the refusals); with `[for <owner>]` or `needs: decision` in it, or from the owner himself, it is an ordinary message. `client.py message … --fyi` and the `fyi` field of `POST /client/message` do the same for a script.

**The audit's findings reach their mover as a task** (decided on the item by the project's -main). The audit's batch to a project (router rule `audit-owners`, `router.audit_rule`) is split by item mover, one message per agent (`router_live._act_split`), each carrying the batch's opening lines, the agent's own findings whole (`router.audit_text`, never cut to one line) and the relay's closing paragraph. A finding asks its mover to act, so it is never held: a background mover (a builder) is woken at once with a normal notice, like a front one (marked for the owner as a pass to a front agent is), whatever `hold` the message carried, and it is never shown under "they asked nothing of you". An item with no mover, a mover who is no agent of the project, a closed item and the lines that are no finding go to the -main agent. The ruling's `to` (the "passed to" line) lists every recipient; an agent whose lines an earlier try held (a `live.jsonl` entry of `sent` ending `: held`, written before an earlier change) is named "(held)" there, so it does not look dropped. Before this, the lines for a builder were held as "asked nothing", and as one line made of the batch's opening and its findings cut to 260 characters, so the findings were lost.

**A front agent mover hears of its item at two moments only** (so a front agent need not set a far wake and wait on a builder): when the item is **back with it alone**, and when it is **closed** by another session. A builder's step is never pushed to it: a session that takes itself out of `needs` while another session stays in it, or marks its work lines done, sends the owner nothing; that step is recorded where the server already records it: one line in the item's History (a comment on the GitHub mirror) and, for a state-changing write, the page debt against the writer (the project page's Current state or a timeline line, `page_debt.py`). Nothing new is sent in its place.

**No automatic return or fallback goes to a front agent, and an item notice reaches one only while the owner is there**. Two rules; both leave what a session or the owner writes on purpose as written (an explicit mover, a `then`, the `message` tool, a `[for <owner>]` notice, his board answer relayed to the session it is for, a hand-off, a migration or recycle notice, a notice that names the recipient in `needs`).
**(1) Returns and fallbacks.** Every place stackshot hands an item back or picks a fallback mover by itself now asks the same question: is the target a front agent (`anchors.session_roles()`, role `front`: the test the message tool uses)? The places: the hand-off ladder's last step, the return to the sender or origin (`handoff.next_step`, `Env.is_front`); the cold call's pick (`server._handoff_agents` offers only `background:` agents, so the call cannot name a front agent); the mover a stepped answer of his lands on when his `then` names none; the mover an ended wait falls back to (`mod.wait.post_wake_mover`, the answer-watch fallbacks). A front agent there is replaced by the project's builder (`server._auto_mover`, which reads `_handoff_builder`). **A project with no builder** keeps the item with its current mover; in the hand-off ladder that is the recovery request to the fleet's builder (a front origin is no longer a place to return to), and in the two places that have no other holder for the item (his answer, an ended wait) the existing fallback stays, because refusing his answer would lose the item. The derived default mover of the item model (`_fallback_mover`) is read, not changed. **The hand-back line** (`server._tell_handback`: a builder or another session leaves the item's needs naming its front agent mover alone) is no exception: stackshot does not hand the item to that front agent. The project's builder is told instead, reworded ("is back with <front agent>, a front agent, alone ... told to you, not to <front agent>"), after the usual `handback_hold_seconds` window (a close in it drops the line); when the builder wrote the hand-back itself, or the project lists none, nobody is told: the item's needs and History show it, and the routing log has a `held` line. The write's reply says which of these happened.
**(2) What a front agent hears about an item.** An item notice for a front agent (`server._tell_woken` → `_front_gate`; kinds `progress`, `close`, `stuck`, from the call site or the notice's tag) is delivered only when **the owner is there for that pane**: `<state>/prompts/<session id>` holds epoch seconds within `[presence] minutes` (30). Another repo's hook writes those files and stackshot only reads them; the session id comes from the CLI's registry (`~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json`, the pane `server._handoff_pane_row` finds); a pane this box does not run, a missing or unreadable file, a stamp from the future or an old one is "not there". **And only when the agent touched the item in its current conversation**: a revision of the item since the registry's `startedAt` for the pane names the session as its writer (a pane whose registry row has no `startedAt` counts as not touched: the gate holds, like a missing presence stamp) (`written_by` or `written_by_id`, the same git revisions History shows; a relaunch, a resume or a recycle moves `startedAt`, so an earlier conversation's touch does not count). Then an ordinary progress notice is filed as an `fyi` line (`held.py`, it rides his next turn and starts none), and the close of the item or an item marked stuck is a normal notice. Every other case sends nothing and writes a routing-log line ruled `held` by `stackshot (auto)`, reason `held: front agent, <why> (<kind>)`. A pane whose hook has not fetched held lines (`held.can_fetch`) gets no fyi line either (logged as held): a new turn for a progress notice is not worth it. Notices that ask the mover to act are never gated (the tags `wake`, `watch`, `idle`, `wait`, `comment`, `answer`, `needs`, `stall`, hand-off, migration, recycle): held, the item would sit unmoved. **The board always reaches him; a front-agent turn is only ever extra.** Whenever one of these reaches a front pane, or is held, the same news is written to the board in the same pass: a progress notice leaves one History line (`metadata.front_quiet.log`, `quiet.py`; written by the server, no writer, no clock moved, nothing posted to the issue; "<agent> hears of it on his next turn" or "<agent> was not told (why)"; deduplicated, the newest 8 kept, open items only); a close is the item's own close revision in History; a stuck item is the `wait_failed` mark a failed wait writes, written before the notice. The sender hints the kind through `server._tell_kind` (a thread-local read by the gate), so `_tell_woken` keeps its signature; the gate's verdict (sent, filed as an fyi line, or held) is read back through `server._gate_result()`, so a writer's reply says a front agent "was told" only when the gate let the notice through, and otherwise says it was not told and that the board carries it. The owner-change notice's last resort (the router record could not be written) is a progress notice, gated like the rest. A failure of the gate itself sends the notice as before.
- *Back with you alone*: a session other than the mover leaves the item's `needs` naming the mover alone (`decision` still in `needs` is his, not a hand-back; `external` is ignored), when before the write `needs` named someone else beside it. One line, "is back with you alone", with the first line of `next`. **It is held for `[board] handback_hold_seconds` (default 180) before it is sent** (a hand-back followed by a close would wake the keeper twice): a close of the item inside the window (any close, saved through `_save`) drops it, so the keeper hears only the close line; a second hand-back of the same item inside the window replaces the held line and keeps the first due time; when the window ends the line is sent if the item is still open and its `needs` still name the keeper alone (an item that moved on is dropped, one that waits on his decision too). The held lines are the file `<state>/handback-held.json`, so a restart loses none; the fleet tick (every 20 s, every 60 s under the runner) sends what is due. The tick sends first and removes the line after (at least once: a crash between the two sends it again, never zero), and when the held line cannot be written the hand-back is sent at once. `0` sends at once, as before. When it is sent, on a project the router is live for it is recorded for the router and passed by its `hand-back` rule; if the record cannot be written, it is delivered at once with the mark. **For a front agent mover** the item is not handed back by stackshot: the line is reworded ("is back with <mover>, a front agent, alone ... told to you, not to <mover>") and goes to the project's builder instead, through `_tell_or_hold` (kind `changed`: it asks the builder nothing, so where the builder's hook fetches held lines it rides the builder's next turn and wakes it for nothing, else it is pushed as a message). Nobody is told, and the board and the routing log carry it, when the project lists no builder, when the builder wrote the hand-back itself, or when a held record from before this release has no writer id (`wid`): then the builder may have written it, so it is not told about its own write.
- *Closed*: one marked message (`[for <owner>]` first line, delivered with the server's own owner delivery, router live or not) when a session other than the mover closes the item (any close, with his words or not; the For you row above stays as it is). The server's own close on a done check (a merged pull request) tells a front agent mover the same way, "closed by stackshot's done check".
Neither is sent when the mover wrote it, when the mover is not a session, when `[board] tell_owner` is off, or when the item is muted on the list; a close is also not sent when the mover already heard the write. A mover that is not a front agent hears a close and a `needs` change through its older notices.

**The Landed list** (`landed.py`; kept until he clears it). An item an agent finishes and closes reached him only when his words
were on it (the For you list, hidden). Every close that he did not make himself, to the outcome done or dropped, is now one record in `<state>/landed.json` (a small JSON file written
whole, one record per item: its latest close, with the close's clock, the outcome, who closed it as the writer was stored, the project and when he cleared it, 0 = not). The record is made where a
write is saved (`server._landed_on_save`, beside `_foryou_on_save`): a session's close, and the server's own close on a done check (no closer, shown as "stackshot"). **His own close makes none**:
his answer on the board (`author` is he, e.g. "drop it" on a watcher's ask), or a write whose sender is he. A close to any other outcome (superseded, unrecorded) lands nothing. A reopen takes
the item's record off; its next close is a new record, uncleared, whatever the first one's state. A record is kept 60 days (a file that cannot be read is never written over: the writers raise and leave it, the board reads an empty list; a bad hand-edited record is skipped). It is a store of its own because a For you row exists only for
a watched item, carries neither outcome nor closer as fields, and leaves the list when seen. **Like a phone's notifications, an entry stays until he clears it**: there is no seen state, and opening the page clears nothing. **The board's header** carries `Landed N` as the fourth of its five count cards (Your move, Agents at work, Waiting, Landed, Completed; `Board.home_counts`), a link to
`/landed`; and at 0 the card is still drawn, its number muted. **The count and the page read one function**, `Board.landed_rows` (every uncleared close of the 60 days whose item is still closed with an outcome that lands); the count is its length and the page lists its rows, so the two cannot differ. **The page** (`GET /landed`, `Board.landed_html`, behind the board's sign-in, rendered on
request) lists them grouped by scope (for example team, personal and infra, as sala's sidebar groups them: the project's scope from `projects.py`, set on purpose or derived from its page and its agents' names, in `[project_scopes]` order) in one foldable subsection per scope, the same markup and the same code as home's lists (`Board.scope_sections`, `ui/groups.py`): the scope's name and its entry count in the summary, open by default, the entries of a scope newest first, no row naming its project (home's rows do not; opening the item shows it; clearing a line never moves a subsection); the Last 14 days section below draws its rows the same way (the rows of the page shown); each entry reads as a notification: a Clear button first, then the subject as the headline with the close's age beside it ("12 min ago", "3 h ago", "yesterday"; the exact time in the board's zone on hover; the page script redraws it every 30 s), the close reason under it (as large and dark as the subject, wrapped, never cut) and a quiet line with the item id (a link) and done or dropped; who closed it is on the item's page, not here. **Clear all** sits at the top, above the list. **Clearing** is per close
(`landed.key`: item and close clock): a line's Clear and Clear all post the keys they carry to `POST /landed/clear` (the For you seen route's gates: signed in, from the board's
own origin), which republishes the board so the header count drops; a close that lands after the page was drawn is not cleared by it. **Undo**: the page's script sends Clear, Clear all and Undo with `fetch` (`Accept: application/json`, answered `{"ok": true, "changed": n}`; the form post, 303 back to the page, is the fallback without JavaScript) and leaves a cleared line where it is, dimmed, its Clear replaced by an Undo that stays until the page is reloaded (Clear all leaves a per-line Undo on each line it cleared); an Undo posts to `POST /landed/unclear` (same gates, same 64 KB cap), which restores only a record that is cleared, still the close the key names and inside the 60 days (`landed.unclear`); an unknown, uncleared or old key does nothing. The home Landed count is the server's: each change republishes the board, so the header follows (an open home page picks it up with its in-place update). The old `POST /landed/seen` is kept as the same route for a page still open. A cleared record stays in the file until it is 60 days old, so the same close cannot come back. A record the first release marked `seen` (opening the page did that) is uncleared: he never cleared it. Nothing from before the release is
back-filled. No tool changes: a session does not read this list.

**The CI runners page** (`runners.py`, `GET /runners`, `GET /runners.json`, behind the board's sign-in). It opens with five count cards (Runners, Running, Idle, Disabled, Offline; the Offline card turns red above 0), then one row a machine in the Machines panel's order: its full name, its state (`on · 2 of 3 busy`, `disabling · 1 job finishing`, `disabled since 14:35`, `not reachable`) and a Disable or Enable button, beside the machine's runners (state, and the job a running one is on). The words are fixed: a runner turned off on purpose is **disabled**; **offline** is a runner GitHub lists as offline that nobody disabled, for 5 minutes or more (a fault, or a machine that cannot be reached); a runner offline for less than that (`runners.OFFLINE_GRACE_S`) is one restarting between two jobs, as a runner does between two jobs: it reads `restarting` (with the idle dot), is not red and counts as idle, in the side panel too. The time it has been offline is `off_since` on its row in the snapshot file `runners-snapshot.json` only (not in `/runners.json`), cleared when the runner is online again; it is carried only from a snapshot read at most 3 times the slowest rebuild interval ago (the largest of `[runners] cache_s`, `[runners] panel_refresh_s` and `[publish] every_minutes`, which sets the pace when nobody views the page: 30 minutes with the defaults, never less than the 5-minute grace), so a runner offline for good turns to a fault after 5 minutes whether or not the page is open, while a snapshot older than that (a page nobody opened for hours, a long restart) starts the clock again. The table is swapped every 30 s from `/runners.json`; the pages never wait on GitHub or ssh: they draw the last snapshot at once, with how old it is and whether a rebuild is running, and a viewer finding it older than `[runners] cache_s` starts the one background rebuild every viewer shares (the last snapshot is kept in `<paths.state>/runners-snapshot.json`, so a restart draws the table at once). A busy runner whose job GitHub has not listed yet, or no longer lists, reads `starting job…` or `finishing job…`; `job not found` is left for a mismatch that lasts over 3 minutes. **The button** (`POST /runners/switch`, form fields `box`, `act` = `off` or `on`, `token`) runs `bash <[runners] switch_script> off|on <box>` on the server (the switch script, which ssh's to the other machines), never through a shell string and under `switch_timeout_s`, one press at a time per machine. Disable lets a running job finish and then the machine takes no new job (it is not a hard stop); the answer, ok or the error in plain words, is shown above the table. The gates are the answer form's: refused with 503 on a read-only copy, 403 unless signed in to the board with a real address (an open board is refused too, as for /answer and /watch, and draws no button), from the board's own origin, with the page's token (an HMAC over the viewer and the machine); 413 above 4096 bytes; 400 for an action other than off or on, or a machine that is not one of the fleet's names or a `[runners] boxes` entry named in `[runners.ssh_hosts]` (spare-box, outside the sala fleet), before any command runs. No script configured = no buttons. The side panel's Runners card reads `disabled` for such a machine.

**Runners are optional** (`runners.enabled()`, `runners.unused()`). A board with no `[runners]` table, or one whose `repos` is empty, does not use the feature: nothing reads GitHub or ssh for it, no rebuild thread starts and no snapshot file is read back. `/runners` and `/runners.json` still answer, with one plain line ("This board does not use self-hosted CI runners.") in place of the cards and the table, and the side panel's Runners section reads "not used on this board". When `repos` is set and GitHub lists no self-hosted runner for them (the runners are retired), the page says so in one plain line ("GitHub lists no self-hosted CI runners for the repositories this board follows.") and the section reads "no self-hosted runners"; no red card, no empty table. A failed read is still shown as an error. With runners listed, the page is as described above.

**A close keeps the description** (`server._gate_close`, `_update_unlocked`, `close_repair.py`, `tools/restore_close_descriptions.py`). A close used to tell the closing session to rewrite the description to the end state, and nearly every close did, so the owner could not tell what a closed item had been about. Now the description stays exactly as it was, and how the item ended is its own field, `metadata.outcome_text`: `complete` (and `update` with `status=completed`) takes it as `outcome_text`, required, in the bullet form and length of a description (`[description]` limits; the forbidden tokens such as `Resolved:` apply to live descriptions only, since an end state may say what was resolved). `reason` stays the one-line, notice-facing text (at most `[close] reason_max`); `outcome` stays the kind. A description passed with a close by a caller that predates the field is accepted and stored as `outcome_text`, never over the description; both together are refused. An update that edits the description of an already closed item works as before; a reopen drops `outcome_text` with the other close fields. `get` shows an `outcome text:` line after `reason:`, search indexes it, and the item page of a closed item with an outcome text shows an Outcome block first in the main column, under the state line and above Background (an open item, or a closed one with no outcome text, shows none). A description passed with a close that equals the stored one (after strip) is ignored. Whose words are on a close (`_owner_words`, the For you row) are read from the outcome text and the reason, not from the description the close keeps (an item with no outcome text, or an open one, is still read from its description). The server's own closes (done check, terminal prompt, job gap, cold call) write their summary as `outcome_text` too; the cold call's answer key is `outcome_text` (the old `description` key is still read). On GitHub a close still closes the issue (completed or not planned) at once, in one PATCH and with no comment, and the issue body is the description the item keeps followed by an `## Outcome` section holding the outcome text (stored with ids already rewritten from `g<n>` to `board#<n>`, written as stored like the description); a reopen removes the section, and a close with no outcome text (stored before the field) has none. The body form number is not raised and nothing is backfilled: earlier closes already show their end state in the body, since it was their description. The repair's restored description and outcome text reach the issue as an ordinary paced change. **The repair** `tools/restore_close_descriptions.py` puts back the descriptions that closes replaced in the last 14 days (`--days`): from the store's history it takes the description held just before the close, restores it, and moves the close-time text into `outcome_text`. It leaves an item closed before the window, one whose description is already the earlier one, one edited after the close, one with no earlier revision and one that already has an outcome text (counted as "has outcome text" in the dry run; its close's text would otherwise be lost); clocks and every other field stay. Dry run by default (`--store <copy>` for a copy of the board repository, `--live` for the live store, read only); `--live --apply` is refused unless `systemctl --user show -p ActiveState --value stackshot.service` says `inactive` or `failed` (so also while the unit is activating or deactivating, or when systemd cannot say), and `--store <copy> --apply` rehearses it. It is a tool and not a pass at server start because it runs once and the owner should see the dry run first; running it twice is safe.

### A wait a session sets on its own item names its blocker

An agent that moves an item can park its own work with a wake, or by dating its own step ("apply the step at Monday's scheduled run"), although nothing stops the step now, and the ready work then sits idle until the date (`self_wait.py`). A **self-set wait** is a write by the item's mover (or the creator of an item it moves with `mover=<itself>`) that sets a wake that differs from the stored one (a wake re-sent in another spelling of the same moment, `2099-03-04 10:00` for `2099-03-04T10:00`, sets nothing), or writes a line of its own `next` (a line that opens with its own name) whose step is dated later than today: a date as `states.named_dates` reads it (`2099-03-03`, `Mar 3rd`), `next week`, `next month` or `next weekend` (after a plan cue such as `ship it next week`, after until or till, or opening a clause; `next week's planning doc` is a noun, and the same end-of-phrase rule holds: `what next week looks like` is no wait), and the relative words below, read conservatively because a false refusal stops ordinary work while a missed wait only leaves things as they were: `until Monday` or `next Monday` (a weekday after until, till or next); `on`, `at`, `for`, `after` or `this` with a weekday (`apply it on Monday`) only when the day is the adjunct of a plan cue (apply, ship, retry, re-run, check, will, should, then and the like, at most two words between and no determiner among them: `apply it on Monday`, `retry on Friday`; `check why the release on Monday failed` and `schedule the review on Friday` are no wait; a plain noun after the day makes it a modifier, so `run tests on Monday data` and `look at the Monday failures` are no wait: the phrase must end at the day, at the end of the line, a punctuation mark or a word such as then, and, to, at, so, if, when, before, after, with, for, once, while, as, during, via, per, in, because, since, until; `by` is left out on purpose, since `on Friday by noon` is a deadline) and no reporting or past verb stands near it (`said on Monday`, `found on Tuesday`, `failed on Monday`, `the check on Monday` are no wait); a weekday at the start of a line followed by a doing verb (`Monday: apply the step`); a possessive weekday only after a preposition and before a run-like noun (`at Monday's scheduled run`; `Monday's update`, `Monday's build` and `Monday's log` are no wait); `tomorrow` or `tonight` as a word that ends the clause or is followed by then, and, at, morning and the like (not `tomorrow.sh`, not `the tomorrow parser`, not `tomorrow's agenda`); `at`, `on`, `for`, `until`, `after` or `with` before `the next scheduled`, `nightly`, `daily` or `weekly run` (`document how the next nightly run works` is no wait); `after the weekend` after a plan cue or opening a clause, and `until the weekend`; `in 3 days` or `in two weeks` opening a clause (`in 3 days check the build`) or after a plan cue and at the end of the clause (`re-check in 3 days`; `the build finished in 2 days` and `the retry in 3 days case` are no wait). A date is a **deadline** and never a wait when it follows by, due, before, deadline, target, ETA, "no later than", "prior to", "ahead of" or "aim for" (a colon, a dash, a comma or up to eight words may sit between: `ship it, due: Mar 9`, `by the end of the day on Mar 9th`), or is followed by deadline, due, "or earlier" or "at the latest" (`Mar 9 at the latest`); a word that is a wait itself (until, till, after) ends that gap. A wake in no valid form, or not in the future, is left to the write's own wake check: it gets that refusal and no model is asked. Such a write carries `wait_reason=<what the step waits on that is not available now>` (at least `15` characters; the server does not store it): without it the write is refused, and the refusal says what to add. With a reason, one model call (`router.run_call`: the `[router]` command and lane, no tools, safe mode; `[wait_guard] model` picks the model, `timeout_seconds` the time) is asked whether the step could be done now, given the reason; an answer of yes refuses the write and quotes the model's reason, saying to do it now or name the real blocker. The call fails open: no model, a timeout, an error or an unreadable answer lets the write through, and so does a call past `[wait_guard] max_calls` (30 in any rolling hour); each is one line on stderr (`wait guard: …`). The check runs before the item lock is taken (it reads the item, writes nothing), so a slow call never holds the board. **Not judged**, because the wait already names its blocker: a write that leaves the item waiting on another item (`after`), on the owner or an outside party (`needs` decision or external), on a stored `wait_kind`, a hold (a park carries its `wait_basis`), an item that is not alive (one that already waits by its own state: parked, outside, blocked, verifying, or on the owner's call), an item whose mover is another session or the owner, a `next` that hands the work to another session (`<session>: <action>`), a step the item already carried, and a close. `[wait_guard] enabled = false` turns the whole check off (default on). The reasons, the model's answer and the cap are in `self_wait.py`'s docstring; `tests/self_wait_scenario.py` replays the case with a stubbed model.

## How a write flows

1. A session calls `create`, `update` or `complete` through the connector, signed in with Google.
2. The server checks the gates: subject prefix, description shape (short `- ` bullets), next lines, an existing project page, the enums, the date, an owner.
3. It allocates the id, writes the item file and, for a state-changing write, records a page debt against the writer.
4. About five seconds later the publisher renders `board.md` and the site, commits the store and pushes the data repository.
5. The board at `/` and the next `brief` show the change.
6. In the writer's pane, the PostToolUse hook (`pages.py touch`) remembers which page the write belongs to.
7. At the next prompt `pages.py nag` reminds the session if the page has not changed; at the turn's end `pages.py check` nags or blocks once.
8. The debt settles by itself when the page is newer than the write; for a page's keeper only a change to its current state counts.
9. After `page_rule.grace_minutes` the server refuses that session's next state-changing write until the page is updated.
10. Another session's write on a project page is also charged to the page's keeper (the router holds the notice and passes the page update to the agent that keeps the page). With `[pilot] writer_owes_page = true` the writer owes that page update itself (its Current state; any page write for a next-only change or a hold) and is never refused for it; still missing after `[page_rule] grace_minutes`, it moves to the keeper with the board notice. Debts that move to one keeper in the same tick go in one message, each with its own notice (the turn rule's fourth build); a debt is never held back for that, so it still moves at the first tick past the grace. The same goes for wakes: the items one wake pass releases for a session reach it in one message.
11. The server names a background agent in an item's needs when a message hands the item to it or when it updates or closes the item. `[pilot] never_named` lists the background agents it never names this way, for mandates that never work items (e.g. `["my-audit"]`: the daily board audit); they can still be named by hand with `needs=`.
12. Every save also settles the item's `activity` clock (`activity.py`, stamped in `server._save`): the time something real last happened on the work. A session's or the owner's write (create, update, a step, an answer or comment; the owner's GitHub comment counts at its own time, never after now and never before the clock already read), a message written onto the item and an event on one of its anchors (a step its proof ticked, a done check met) move it; a bare `confirm` (which moves `confirmed` only), a write by the audit (its agent, its push label, the instance's own name, the router's ruling name, the migration's label) and the server's own bookkeeping (a wake, a nudge of the stall or hand-off ladder, a repair) do not, so a nudge never resets the clock that decides whether to nudge. An item from before the field has none and reads as its last state change or update; its first save that does not count seeds the field from that value, and no pass rewrites the store. Nothing reads the clock yet and no page or tool text shows it.

## Scope

There are four ways one could imagine running stackshot:

1. One person, one server, one box: the person's own machine, physical or virtual.
2. One server per person on a box that several people share, each person running their own instance under their own account.
3. Several people served by one server process.
4. Several such multi-person servers on one box.

The first is what stackshot is built for and supported for.

The second comes with it by construction. Each person clones the code and runs `./stackshot setup` as themselves, with
`--port` set to a port of their own when the box already has an instance on the default one. Everything an instance owns
then lives under that account: the config in `~/.config/stackshot`, the state in `~/.local/state/stackshot`, the secrets
and machine tokens, the data repository, the Claude Code hooks in `~/.claude/settings.json`, and the systemd user units,
which run in that person's own user manager on their loopback port, behind their own HTTPS hostname. No instance knows
that another exists, and none needs root beyond the one-time `loginctl enable-linger` where the account may not switch
lingering on for itself. Setup refuses a port another account already listens on, and `doctor` says when the port its
config names belongs to someone else, rather than taking that person's server for its own. Every unit is a user unit
(the system scope under `/etc/systemd/system`, of which a box has one, was removed in an earlier change), so nothing an instance
installs is shared with another account. The same goes for anchor-hub's `[anchor_hub] hook_port`, which each person who
enables anchor-hub on a shared box also sets to a port of their own.

This is proven once rather than assumed. `tests/two-users.sh` runs as root in a fresh Ubuntu 24.04 container, creates two
ordinary users without sudo, installs an instance for each as that user, and checks that both servers answer on their own
ports as their own Linux users, that each user's puller and client talk only to that user's server (a token from one is
refused by the other), that `doctor` passes for each, that each user's hooks landed in their own settings, and that
nothing was written outside the two homes. After a restart of the container with nobody logged in, it checks that both
instances came back on their own. The top of the script has the exact commands; it is not part of CI, because it needs
systemd, a container and the network.

The third and fourth are out of scope. Everything in stackshot is keyed to one person: the sign-in allow-list, the items
owned by that person's sessions, their page store, anchor-hub holding their Slack, Notion, Drive, Gmail and GitHub
credentials, the publisher committing as them, and the Machines panel listing their machines. Serving several people from
one process would mean tenancy in the data model, credentials per person for every receiver, an authorization check on
every tool call and an admin surface to manage it all. That is a different product, not a setting of this one. And for
software that holds each person's mail and chat tokens, one isolated instance per person (the second setup, in containers
when there are many people) is the safer way to serve many people anyway.

## Install

[INSTALL.md](INSTALL.md) walks a second person from an empty Linux box to the first item, with three commands as the steps:
`./stackshot setup` writes the config, the data repository, the secrets and the units; `./stackshot doctor` checks the instance and starts the server once the Google OAuth client is filled in; `./stackshot client install` adds the Claude Code hooks (on another machine it also stores the machine's token and starts its puller). `doctor` counts a page hook as present when it is in `~/.claude/settings.json` or in the `hooks/hooks.json` of the enabled stackshot Claude Code plugin (`stackshot@<marketplace>` in `enabledPlugins`, found through `~/.claude/plugins/installed_plugins.json`); a plugin that is installed but disabled does not count.
Between them come the Google OAuth client, the HTTPS front and the claude.ai connector; the Windows client is described in [docs/windows.md](docs/windows.md).

## Configuration

An instance is one TOML file outside this repository: `$STACKSHOT_CONFIG`, else `~/.config/stackshot/stackshot.toml`,
else a `stackshot.toml` next to the code, else the shipped `stackshot.example.toml` (see `config.py`). Only one file is
read; nothing is merged. The table lists every key of `stackshot.example.toml` with its example value. "Yes" in the last
column marks what a second person must set; "if used" marks what matters only with that optional piece.

| Key | Example | Meaning | Set |
|---|---|---|---|
| `instance.name` | `"stackshot"` | The product name in tool texts; the connector's display name is set on claude.ai, not here. | |
| `instance.title` | `"stackshot"` | The board page's title and heading. | |
| `instance.owner` | `"the owner"` | The person every text a session or the board shows speaks of ("&lt;owner&gt;'s task board", the tool texts, the refusals, the page text), never a fixed name. Set it: the start, a reload and `doctor` warn when it is missing. A line of `next` that starts "&lt;owner&gt;:" is the owner's step. | yes |
| `instance.owner_aliases` | `[]` | The other names a session may open a line of `next` with for the owner's step (e.g. `["AE", "boss"]`); `you` always is one and is not listed. | yes |
| `instance.timezone` | `"UTC"` | The timezone of every time the board, the hub or a notice prints, and the one every date and clock check reads (a text says "the instance timezone", or the zone's own abbreviation such as `+04`). UTC when unset; nothing in the code assumes another zone. | yes |
| `instance.board_url` | `"https://stackshot.example.com"` | The board's public address, named in tool texts. | yes |
| `instance.machine` | `""` | The server box's name (e.g. `"main-box"`), written into a temporary id: `board-tmp-<machine>#<n>`. Lowercase letters, digits and hyphens; empty reads `local`. Restart-only. | |
| `instance.font_css` | a Pretendard stylesheet URL | The web font stylesheet the board loads. | |
| `instance.font_stack` | `'Pretendard,-apple-system,…'` | The board's CSS font stack. | |
| `paths.repo` | `"~/stackshot-data"` | The git checkout the publisher commits into; the store and the rendered board live there. | yes |
| `paths.store` | `"~/stackshot-data/board-data"` | Items, `anchors.json`, `projects.json`, `project-record.json` (each page's permanent project id, `prj_<8 hex>`, minted once; the agents registered before a project had an entry whose page line is still owed (`pending_agents`: the migration folds them into the entries and clears them, and `client.py post project-agent` now writes the project's entry instead); a project's scope set on purpose and its "listed on purpose" mark; and, once `tools/migrate_project_record.py --apply` has run, one entry per project in the section `projects`, read before the page's own lines: `project_registry.py`), `next-id`; the server is the only writer. | yes |
| `paths.markdown` | `"~/stackshot-data/board.md"` | The rendered board kept in the repository. | |
| `paths.rules` | `"~/stackshot-data/RULES.md"` | The rulebook the `rules` tool serves. | yes |
| `paths.brain` | `"~/brain"` | The pages checkout (for example gbrain's managed mirror): the page registry and page freshness, on the server box only. | yes |
| `paths.fleet_names` | `"~/stackshot-data/session-names.tsv"` | OS hostname to fleet name to letter, for a machine with no fleet state yet. | |
| `paths.state` | `"~/.local/state/stackshot"` | Everything the instance writes for itself: site, ledger, messages, inbox, clients, anchor-hub state. | |
| `paths.secrets` | `"~/.config/stackshot"` | `oauth.env`, portal client files, anchor-hub credentials, `client.token`, `client.url`. | |
| `paths.claude_credentials` | `"~/.claude/.credentials.json"` | The Claude Code login, read by `cloud.py` to look up cloud session titles. | |
| `paths.project_touch` | `"~/.claude/project-touch"` | The pane gate's per-session state, on every machine. | |
| `pages.dirs` | `["projects"]` | The sub-directories of `paths.brain` that hold pages, in order; a bare slug names a page in the first (e.g. `["projects", "infra"]`). Keep `registries.projects.match` in step with this. | |
| `pages.flavor` | `"plain"` | How a session is told to update its page: plain (edit `<paths.brain>/<key>.md`, commit and push) or gbrain (through gbrain's tools). With plain, the server itself writes pages as markdown files in `paths.brain` (a new project's page, the router's timeline notes, the page-debt pass) and runs no git: committing that checkout is the instance's job; `rename_page.py` and `page_keeper.py` are gbrain tools and refuse under plain with a message that says so. | |
| `pages.template_page` | `""` | The page whose Part 2 holds the project template a new project's page is made from (a key under the dirs, e.g. `"projects/template"`); empty = the short built-in page. | |
| `pages.rules_line` | `""` | The bullet a new project's page carries to link the rules, written as given; empty = `- Not written yet.` | |
| `pages.timeline_source` | `"stackshot"` | The source word on the timeline line a page move writes (`rename_page.py`; gbrain flavor). | |
| `ids.letter` | `"s"` | The letter of every new id (`s<n>`): one global sequence, allocated only by the server. | |
| `fleet.publisher_letter` | `"a"` | The letter of the box that runs the server: first column of the Machines panel, always live. | |
| `fleet.operator_label` | `"fleet operator"` | What the `sessions` tool calls the session that operates the others. | |
| `fleet.operator_session` | `""` | The session of this name is the operator (the one the `sessions` tool names for fleet work). Contract: none; it names a session, nothing is exchanged. | if used |
| `fleet.tool_name` | `""` | The fleet tool as the prose names it: the board's "Pane recycle pending for … (`<name>` runs it)", a session link's "open this session in `<name>`", the update tool's recycle text. `""`: "the fleet tool". Contract: none; it only changes prose. | if used |
| `fleet.recycle_when_idle` | `""` | The command, as words, that asks the fleet tool for a pane recycle once the box is idle, the reason appended (e.g. `"sala recycle --when-idle"`). Sessions are told to run it when no item covers a change; `release.sh` runs it on a recycle (picked from the PR's files, or `--recycle`) when no item was flagged. `""`: nothing is run or suggested. Contract: [13, stackshot's release asks for a recycle](https://github.com/howardpark-ai/salamander/blob/main/docs/stackshot-contracts.md#13-stackshots-release-asks-for-a-recycle). | if used |
| `fleet.notice_cmd` | `""` | The command, as words like `recycle_when_idle`, that tells the running sessions about a change they should know but need not reload (a rule, a tool's wording). `release.sh` runs it after a green deploy whose session pick is a notice, with one argument appended, "<title> — <summary>" (a newline or tab turned into a space, cut to 400 characters and ending in "…" when cut), so a command like `sala notice add --from release` takes it as its one text argument ([docs/session-reload.md](docs/session-reload.md)). A command that fails or is not on `PATH` makes the report go to the keeper instead of green. `""`: the release log says no notice command is set, and the release goes on. Read at each use. | if used |
| `release.report_to` | `"projects/example"` | The page key (or session name) `release.sh` sends the report of each release to, and names in the line it prints when a release starts. The default is the stand-in page of the example config; set a page of your own. A value that is not a page key or session name reads as the default (`config.py` `notes()` and doctor say so). | |
| `subject.prefixes` | `["[work] ", "[home] "]` | Every subject starts with one of these. | yes |
| `subject.fallback_prefix` | `""` | The prefix of an item the server files itself (a pane's terminal prompt, a job gap) when the project's scope has no prefix of its own; it must be one of `subject.prefixes`. `""`, or one that is not in the list: the first prefix. | |
| `subject.max_length` | `80` | the longest subject a create or an update that sets one takes, the scope prefix counted; longer is refused with the limit, the length and the fix. A stored longer subject is left as it is: a write that does not set the subject is never refused for it. Before this the code default was 200 and the example file's 160. | |
| `close.reason_max` | `120` | the longest `reason` a close takes (`complete`, or `update` with `status=completed`); longer is refused. `0` = no limit. The server's own closes (done check, watcher drop, cold call, pane prompt) keep to it by cutting their text. | |
| `description.max_lines` | `8` | The hard ceiling on description lines (non-blank), refused above it. | |
| `description.line_max` | `120` | every description line must be a `- ` bullet of at most this many characters, else the write is refused. A link counts by its text (its destination does not, in any form: `(url)`, `(url "title")`, `(<url with spaces>)`, one level of parentheses in the url; nor does a naked URL). A dash followed by spaces or a tab is a bullet. A session's create, and an update that replaces the description, must also open with a `- Goal: …` first bullet (see "An item opens with its goal"). Only the description written in the call is checked, a close's new description included; a stored one is never judged. The client API's question route writes its text as bullets itself and is gated like any create; the cold call's close description is put into bullets before it is written (split on lines only, a leading `-`, `*`, `•` or `1.` marker taken off, each line clipped as it reads; more than `max_lines` lines end in `- …and N more`). | |
| `description.require_bullets` | `true` | `false` switches all of the description strictness off (a way back): the bullet and length checks, the refusal of a long `next` line (the old note returns), the question route's bulleting and clipping, and the cold call's re-bulleting; the line cap and `forbid_tokens` still apply, and the re-bullet pass goes back to its own checks. `[model] description_guard` is inert while this is `true`: a description that is not bullets is refused before that guard sees it, so the guard's note/refuse modes only matter with this set to `false`. The re-bullet pass holds its rewrites to `line_max` too, so the server does not refuse what the pass writes. | |
| `metadata.next.line_max` | `200` | a line of `next` over this many characters is refused, counted after its `<actor>: ` prefix (the text up to and including the colon-space that names the actor, so a long session name takes no room from the action; a line with no such prefix counts whole; the whole line is capped at this plus 80, 280 by default, since a name may be three long words) (`0` = off, and the old "a line over 200 characters" note returns) (links counted as in a description). Only the `next` written in the call is checked; a `<owner>: `command`` line (the whole command in one backtick span; any other line of his is prose and counts) and next's first non-blank line when it is an ask line and the call passes no `ask` are not judged. A hash, a test count or past tense stays a note. | |
| `metadata.next.require_marks` | `true` | `next` is a checklist; a write of `next` that no longer lists an open step, with the step not named in `done` or `dropped`, is refused. `false` is the way back: the step reads as dropped, with nobody and no time (as a line that left `next` by a write that knows nothing of steps does). | |
| `description.forbid_tokens` | `["resolved:", "done:"]` | Refused in a description, which is live state, not a changelog. | |
| `metadata.owner.required` | `true` | An item must name its creator session (stored as `metadata.owner`, shown in History as "created by"; it is not who moves the item). | |
| `metadata.project.required`, `.registry` | `true`, `"projects"` | An item must name a page found in the `projects` registry. | |
| `metadata.needs.enum` | `["decision", "external"]` | The kinds allowed in `needs` beside session names. | |
| `metadata.priority.enum` | `["p1", "p2", "p3"]` | The allowed priorities. | |
| `metadata.due.format` | `"YYYY-MM-DD"` | The format of `due`. | |
| `metadata.next.type` | `"string"` | `next` is free text. | |
| `registries.projects.key` | `"pages"` | The top-level key of `projects.json`, which the publisher writes from `paths.brain`. | |
| `registries.projects.match` | `["projects/{value}", "{value}"]` | How a bare project value is resolved to a page key, tried in order. | |
| `page_rule.mode` | `"refuse"` | nag, or refuse: after the grace period the debtor's next state-changing write is refused. | |
| `page_rule.grace_minutes` | `30` | How long a page debt may stand before refusal. | |
| `page_rule.slack_seconds` | `300` | A page put up to this long before the board write counts as the same turn. | |
| `board_check.mode` | `"off"` | The end-of-turn board check: off, block (a working turn that left the session's open items untouched is asked once to update or confirm them, or to say `Board: no change, <reason>`), or note (the same question said at the next prompt instead of holding the turn). A machine needs a `pages.py` with the check (with note mode, for `note`; an older one reads it as off). | |
| `audit.enabled` | `false` | true: `install.sh` installs and starts the audit's timer, and `POST /audit/now` starts the audit run (or cues the audit agent, in agent mode); false: no timer (one installed earlier is disabled and removed) and a cue is refused. | if used |
| `audit.mode` | `"script"` | `"script"`: the audit is a script (field checks in code, one model call per suspect); `"agent"`: the earlier way, a cue to the audit agent. Switching back to `"agent"` is the rollback. Any other value reads as `"script"`. | |
| `audit.agent` | `"my-audit"` | Mode `"agent"`: the session that runs the audit and writes its run records; every cue is pushed to it. | if used |
| `audit.max_calls` | `40` | Script mode: the most `claude -p` calls one run makes; the suspects past it wait for the next run. `0`: no call. | |
| `audit.max_minutes` | `100` | Script mode: no new `claude -p` call is begun once this many minutes have passed since the run started (the record says `timed_out`), also for the page-debt calls of the pages stage; kept under the unit's 2 h, so a larger value reads as 110. | |
| `audit.threshold` | `5` | Script mode: findings of one check in one run that trip the tightening message to the builder (also: more than a fifth of the open items flagged). | |
| `audit.front_step_move_hours` | `3` | The ten-minute filing pass: hours a step held by a front agent (or a retired session) may stay the first open step of an item another session moves, with the stuck-elsewhere finding up, before the pass rewords it to open with the item's mover (see *The front-step rule*). A decimal reads (`1.5`). `0`: the rule is off (no clock kept, nothing moved, clocks left over are dropped). A negative or non-numeric value reads as `3`. | |
| `audit.remind_hours` | `6` | The ten-minute passes: hours a finding its item has not answered (no write since) waits after its last send before it is sent again, worded as a later try (second, third), up to three sends in all and then one line to the project's builder as unmoved. A decimal reads. `0`: sent once, as before. | |
| `audit.builder` | `"my-engineer"` | Script mode: the session the threshold message and an unmoved flag go to. | |
| `audit.summary_to` | `"projects/example"` | Script mode: the page key (or session name) the summary of each run is sent to. The default is the stand-in page of the example config; set a page of your own. A value that is not a page key or session name reads as the default (`config.py` `notes()` and doctor say so). | |
| `audit.main` | `"my-main"` | Script mode: the `-main` agent that gets findings addressed to the owner (marked `[for <owner>]`) when the project has none of its own. | |
| `audit.model` | `""` | Script mode: the model of the judgment calls; empty is the `[router]` model. | |
| `audit.lane` | `""` | Script mode: a sala lane for the judgment calls (for example `"cloud-lane"`): each runs as `salamander lane exec <lane> -- claude …`, with the lane's environment and no other provider's key. Empty: the `[router]` command's own lane. Only the audit's calls move; every other router call keeps the `[router]` command. The model (`audit.model`, else `[router] model`) must exist on the lane. | |
| `audit.pattern_review` | `"shadow"` | Script mode: the pattern review, the last stage of each model run. `"shadow"`: it reads the findings the audits made since the review before it, groups the repeats by kind, agent and project, and makes one model call (on `audit.lane` and `audit.model`, inside `audit.max_minutes`, keeping one of the `audit.max_calls`) that drafts one guardrail per pattern; the drafts go onto the run record and the summary line only, and nothing is sent or opened. `"off"`: no review, no call, no findings log. Any other value reads as `"shadow"`. | |
| `audit.at` | `["05:00"]` in code; `["05:00", "20:00"]` in the example | The times of day (HH:MM, instance timezone) the timer starts the audit: a list runs it at each time, one `OnCalendar` line apiece, and a single `"HH:MM"` string runs it once a day as before. An entry that is not a time of day is left out; with none left the audit runs at 05:00 (the default when the key is absent). | |
| `wait_guard.enabled` | `true` | A wait a session sets on its own item (a wake, or a dated line of its own next) must carry `wait_reason`, and one model call refuses it when the step could be done now (see the section "A wait a session sets on its own item names its blocker", under The pieces). false: no refusal and no call. Read live. | |
| `wait_guard.max_calls` | `30` | The most model calls in any rolling hour; a write past it goes through unjudged and is logged. `0`: no call (the missing-reason refusal stays). | |
| `wait_guard.timeout_seconds` | `30` | One call, seconds; a write whose call runs longer goes through unjudged. | |
| `wait_guard.model` | `""` | The call's model; empty is the `[router]` model. | |
| `said.injected_marker` | `""` | A message that starts with this marker is text a fleet tool typed into the pane, not the owner's words, and a `said` entry naming it is refused (sala's: `"<!-- sala -->"`). `""`: no marker; the stopgap prefixes and phrases still apply. Contract: [2, Message marker](https://github.com/howardpark-ai/salamander/blob/main/docs/stackshot-contracts.md#2-message-marker). | if used |
| `check.github_minutes` | `5` | How often the `pr_merged` done check reads one pull request at most (one GET, shared by the items naming it; the wait doubles up to an hour while GitHub is down). `0`: every pass. | |
| `ask.refuse_after` | `""` | The date (YYYY-MM-DD) from which a needs=decision item without an ask line is refused; before it, and with the empty default, the write is accepted and the reply nags. | |
| `freshness.call_days`, `.agent_days`, `.external_days` | `1`, `1`, `3` | How long an active open item in each lane (your call, on agent, on others) may go without a state change or a `confirm` before it reads stale. Items on hold never do. | |
| `page_gate.keeper_currency` | `"keepers"` | Whose debt settles only on a current-state edit: keepers (every session that keeps an active page), all, off, or a list of session names. | |
| `publish.git` | `true` | false renders only and never fetches, commits or pushes (a staging instance). | if used |
| `publish.debounce_seconds` | `5` | The delay between a write and its publish. | |
| `publish.every_minutes` | `10` | The server's own publish timer, on the dot. | |
| `github.sync` | `false` | true mirrors every item change to an issue in `github.issues_repo` through a paced queue on disk; false sends nothing. Turn it on only after the migration. | |
| `github.issues_repo` | `""` | owner/name of the board repo (private, issues only, every issue opened through the server). | if used |
| `github.issues_repo_id` | `""` | GitHub's numeric id of the board repo (a positive integer; empty: not set). Set, every REST call for the repo (issues, comments, the id allocator, the queue, a merged-PR check on it) goes to `/repositories/<id>/...`, which survives a transfer to another owner: GitHub follows a 301 on a read but not on a write. `issues_repo` still names the repo on the board, in the map's repo note and in the issue links. | |
| `github.issue_ids` | `false` | true: a new item's id is its issue number, written `board#<n>` and kept as `<letter><n>` in the store; the create then waits for GitHub, and while GitHub is down, or the hour's write budget leaves no room within a few seconds, takes a temporary local id instead. | |
| `github.issues_letter` | `"g"` | The store letter of issue-numbered items (`g/<n>.json`); restart-only. | |
| `github.local_letter` | `"l"` | The store letter of items created while GitHub was down or the hour's writes were spent (`l/<n>.json`, id `l<n>`), re-keyed to their issue number when it has room; restart-only. | |
| `github.min_interval_seconds`, `.hourly_budget` | `1.5`, `250` | The pace of the mirror's writes: at least this long between two, at most this many an hour (GitHub allows about 80 a minute and 500 an hour). The hour is counted in the host's ledger, `<state>/github/budget.json`, so a restart does not reset it. | |
| `github.read_budget` | `1500` | At most this many counted GitHub reads an hour, from the server (runners page, side panel, merged-PR checks, the comment reader) and anchor-hub together, in the same ledger as the writes; a `304` is not counted. Over it a read is refused until the hour's oldest call expires. Live in the server and in anchor-hub (both re-read the config on a reload); `hourly_budget` is read when the server starts. The side panel's Audit section shows them as a "GitHub calls" card (writes and reads against their caps, and the 60-minute window they cover; not `/healthz`, which is public). | |
| `github.merge_seconds` | `300` | The updates of an existing issue (a changed subject, description, or a new close reason) wait this long and go out as one PATCH; a create and a close or reopening go at once. `0`: each update goes at once. | |
| `github.backfill_bodies` | `false` | true (with sync on): when the body form is above the recorded one, every mirrored issue whose body differs from what the item renders now is rewritten once, paced, on the background lane. false: an issue takes the slim body the next time it changes. | |
| `github.api_url`, `.token_file` | `"https://api.github.com"`, `""` | The API base (a test points it at a fake) and a file with the token; an empty token file means the gh CLI's login. | |
| `github.poll_seconds` | `60` | How often the server reads the repo's new comments while the mirror is on (one GET each; at least 15): the owner's own comment on an item's issue reaches the item and its sessions. | |
| `github.owner_login` | `""` | The GitHub login the owner comments from; empty means the login the mirror's token belongs to (`GET /user`). | |
| `github.moved` | none | Repositories transferred to another owner: old `"owner/repo"` = new `"owner/repo"` (lower-case; an entry in another form is ignored, as is a table that is not a table). GitHub redirects the old address but stackshot compares names as text; listed here, an old and a new name are one repository wherever names are compared: an anchored pull request's URL (`recycle-due --pr`, the items held on a PR anchor), an anchor's identity (a PR added under the new name is the anchor already stored under the old one, and the hub keys it by the current name), the hub's notification dedupe, `pilot.own_repos` name entries, `pr_merged` checks, and release.sh's green result against this checkout's origin. Matching only: stored anchors, items and URLs are never rewritten. Read at each use: add the entries on the day of the transfer and reload (SIGHUP), no restart. | |
| `released.repos` | none | Other repositories whose pull requests count as released here: `"owner/repo" = { checkout = "/abs/path" }` (names compared as the other repository checks do: case, `github.moved`). A pull request of such a repository is live when its merge commit is an ancestor of that checkout's HEAD, which the server reads itself (`git merge-base --is-ancestor`, read-only, with a timeout; a client never names a path). Used by `client.py released --pr <URL> --sha <merge>` (the sha is checked against that checkout; with no sha the server finds the merge in the checkout's log), by a step's `pr_released` proof and by a `pr_released <owner/repo#n>` event wait (the clock's catch-up finds a release the call missed). A repository the table does not list is refused, with a message that names the ones it lists. Absent (no defaults are merged) = none: only this repository's pull requests are released, as before. An entry that is not `owner/repo` with an absolute `checkout` is ignored and said at start and on a reload. Read at each use: a reload applies it. | |
| `scopes.order` | `["work", "home", "infra"]` | The board's project groups, in order, derived from a page's org, tags and key. | yes |
| `scopes.default` | `"home"` | The group of a page that matches no rule. | yes |
| `scopes.by_org.<org>` | `work = "work"` | A page's `org` mapped to a group. | |
| `scopes.by_tag.<tag>` | `infra = "infra"` | A page's tag mapped to a group. | |
| `scopes.by_prefix.<prefix>` | `"infra/" = "infra"` | A page key prefix mapped to a group. | |
| `project_scopes.order` | `["general"]` | The scopes a project can have, in the order the picker shows them (`client.py get projects`, the fleet tool's New session form); an agent of a project is called `<scope>-<slug>-main`. A project's scope is the one set on purpose, else derived from the key prefix, the page's `org`, its tags, then the default. | |
| `project_scopes.default` | `"general"` | The scope of a page that matches no rule (also where `[router] general_<scope>` falls back to). | |
| `project_scopes.orgs` | every scope in `order` | The scopes a page may name as its `org:`. | |
| `project_scopes.by_prefix.<prefix>` | none | A page key prefix mapped to a scope, e.g. `"infra/" = "infra"`. | |
| `project_scopes.by_tag.<tag>` | none | A page's tag mapped to a scope (used when its `org` names none), e.g. `infra = "infra"`. | |
| `project_agent.form_senders` | `[]` | The `--sender` labels of the fleet tool's New session form (sala's: `["sala-web"]`; empty = the form is off). A `client.py post project-agent` call is the form's only when its sender is in this list AND the machine its token proves is in `form_machines`; it may then list a new agent on any listed project (a hidden one is a 403: list it again first). The agent goes into the project's agents map in the record, and the page's `agents:` block is written from the record (`page_owed` is always false now); the same agent again changes nothing, and another side or mandate for a listed name is a 409. The same gate admits `client.py post project-agent-rename <project> <OLD> <NEW>`, which renames an agent's entry in the record's agents map in place and writes the page's block from it, and `client.py post project-agent-retire <project> <name>` (sala's retire step, label `sala-retire`), which takes the retired agent off the record's agents map, writes the page's block from it and adds a timeline line to the page saying the agent was retired and its line removed (200 "line removed"); a leftover pending entry is dropped (200 "dropped"); one that is listed nowhere changes nothing (200 "not listed", with a reason).
