A stackshot project is defined by its entry in `project-record.json` (section `projects`, `project_registry.py`), keyed by its permanent id (`prj_<8 hex>`): its name (the prefix of its agents' names), title, scope, status (`active` or `archived`), agents map with each agent's mandate line (front and background), hub, `page` (a pointer), `former_keys`, `former_names`, `former_agents`, and `made` / `retired` with who, when and how. **Every write to what a project is goes record-first** (`project_verbs.py`): one atomic change of the entry under the record's own lock, then a write of the page's mirror, which is three lines of the page's frontmatter and nothing else: `display-name:`, `agents:` and `status:`, computed from the record as it is when the write happens. The pane hooks, the page-maintenance jobs and the keeper lookups keep reading those page lines, which is why the mirror stays. A page edit made by hand to a mirrored line is not read (the entry wins) and the next mirror write puts the line back; a page that lists an agent the record lacks keeps its `agents:` block as it is, and only that block: the `display-name:` and `status:` lines are still written, and the answer says `mirror.state` is `failed`, naming the extra agents (the write would delete their lines for good). Clear it by listing each name with `agent-add`, which puts it in the record first; the next mirror then writes the block. `former_agents` is the other half of that guard: the names a retire or an agent rename has just taken off the entry, which the page may still list until a mirror has written it without them (a failed page write must not turn the name into a stranger the guard refuses); a mirror that leaves the page without a name drops it from `former_agents`, and a name listed again is dropped at once. A mirror that fails does not undo the change: the record is right, the answer says so, and the project's next change (or `mirror`) writes the page. Every answer of a verb carries `mirror` = `{state, key, note}` (`state` is `written`, `unchanged`, `would-write`, `none` for a project with no page of this instance's page store, `no-page`, `failed`, `exists` or `deferred`).

A project needs no page. Its `page` pointer is optional and may be a link to another knowledge base; page reminders, the page-first rule and the mirror apply only to a project whose pointer is a page in this instance's page store. A project with no entry yet (the migration has not reached it) gets one at its first verb, made from its page as the page store has it at that moment (not from the registry, which follows the pages checkout minutes late), with its pending agents folded in. That page is read **before** any lock is taken (`project_verbs.preread`; the server's `post project-agent` does it before `_PROJECT_LOCK`): a gbrain read can take a minute or two, and every record writer waits on the record's lock. Inside the lock a verb reads the record and nothing from the page store. If the pre-read fails, the answer carries a `warning` and the entry is made from the registry's copy of the page's lines; the mirror's guard keeps a line the registry did not know from being deleted.

**Sub-pages are never projects.** A key under another page (`projects/alpha/sub`) or a page with a `hub:` line is a sub-page: it gets no entry (the migration writes entries for top-level pages only), and `create` refuses it (400). Through a sub-page's key, `rename` and the agent verbs act on the top-level project that owns it; `archive`, `reopen` and `move-page` refuse it (400 naming the top-level page), because they act on the whole project; the id of a sub-page is refused by every verb. A project's page is one level (`projects/<slug>`): `create` and `move-page` refuse a key below another page.

`tools/project.py` runs the verbs from a shell (`--dry-run` shows what would be written; `--json` the whole answer; `--by LABEL` names the actor):

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `create NAME --scope S [--page KEY\|LINK] [--make-page] [--title T]` | a new project; with no `--page` it has no page and none is made |
| `rename PROJECT NEW_NAME` | the name changes, the id stays, the old name goes to `former_names`, pending agents under the old name are dropped |
| `move-page PROJECT NEW_KEY [--phase plan\|copy\|stub ...]` | runs `rename_page.py` (copy, then stub), then the record: the pointer is the new key, the old key a former key. A stub that refuses is a 502 saying the page was not stubbed; fix what it names and run `--phase stub`. A stub that was written for every page the plan listed but whose id move failed is not an error: the record is made right and the answer carries a `warning`; a stub that left a page under the old one unstubbed is a 502 too, with the same `--phase stub` retry (it skips the pages already stubbed) |
| `archive PROJECT [--why W]` / `reopen PROJECT` | status `archived` (with `retired` = who, when, how, why) and back; the page's `status:` follows |
| `agent-add PROJECT NAME (--front\|--back) --mandate M` | the agent on the map, then the page's `agents:` block; the same agent again changes nothing, another mandate is a 409 |
| `agent-retire PROJECT NAME` / `agent-rename PROJECT OLD NEW` | the line off the map / renamed in place; the archive rule applies to a retire |
| `mirror PROJECT [--make-page]` | writes the three mirror lines again |
| `show PROJECT` | the entry and what the registry reads of it |

The same verbs answer the client API: `client.py post project --scope S (--key projects/slug | --name N [--page KEY\|LINK] [--make-page true\|false])`, `post project-agent`, `post project-agent-rename` and `post project-agent-retire`. `tools/migrate_project_record.py` (dry run by default) writes the entries of the top-level pages, folds the pending agents of active projects into them and clears the table; `--undo` removes only the entries that pass made.

**Items store the project's id.** An item holds `metadata.project_id`, the permanent id of the top-level project it belongs to, beside `metadata.project` (the page it is filed on: a copy of the project's own page key, or a sub-page's key as the item's plain detail link). `item_project.py` is the one resolver: `key_of(meta)` reads the project's page through the id (a page that was moved or renamed, one of the project's `former_keys`, reads as the page now), `derive(key)` gives the id a key names (a sub-page or a page with a `hub:` line gives its project's), and the server reads an item through it when it loads one, so the board, the brief, the router's counts, the Projects list and the archive sweep follow a moved page with no write to the items. A save sets the id from the key (create, `update(project=)`, a re-file); `move-page`, `rename`, `archive` and `reopen` change the record only and the items follow. A save whose key names no project the record knows (a re-file to a registered page with no record entry) takes out the id the item carried, so the item reads as its new key and never as the project it left; an item that stores no id reads as stored too, so the pass below is what makes a moved page keep its items.

**An item's project is the top-level project; the page it is filed on is its detail link.** `item_project.project_of(meta)` is the page key of the top-level project that owns the page an item is filed on (`key_of` followed up through `derive`: a sub-page reads as the project that owns it, a page with a `hub:` line as the hub's project, a former key as the page now, a key that names no project as stored), with a stored `project_id` or without one, and `top_of(key)` is the same for a page key. It is a display and grouping reader and writes nothing. The readers that show an item's project use it: board.md's `- project:` line (the label is the top-level key, the link stays the mirror of the page the item is filed on), the item page's Project line, the search results' project label, and the `project:` line of `get`, `brief`, `create` and `update` (`item_project.line_of`: the top-level key, and `(filed on <sub-page>)` after it for an item on a sub-page, so the text keeps the detail link the markdown keeps as a link). The readers that need the page keep `key_of`, the page the item is filed on: page debts and the pages-owed nag, anchors scoped to a page, the keeper and agent lookups, the page-update gate, a page's own project page with its sub-page badge, the board row (`row_of` over the page, which already rolls a sub-page up to its project and keeps a `board_row: true` sub-page its own row: the items under that row show the top-level project on their `project:` line and link the sub-page, and the row heading above them names the sub-page; no live page sets it), the router's roots, move-page, rename and archive. The stored `project` and `project_id` are unchanged by it, so no migration and no write; `tools/project_id_report.py` compares what `project_of` reads for every open item before and after the pass.

`tools/project_id_report.py` stores the ids on the items already there (open and closed). Dry run by default: it copies the store to a private folder, runs the pass on the copy and compares what the board's markdown and brief, the server's brief and every open item's project key show before and after (it exits 1 when they differ). `--apply --sender <you>` runs it through the live server (`POST /client/migrate`, op `project_id`, the migration gate of `[model] migration`): it asks for the plan first, writes the undo file `<run-dir>/<pass id>/undo.json` before the first write, and writes only the one key in each file (no clock and no file time moves). `--undo FILE --sender <you>` takes the ids out again (op `unproject_id`; an item a later save changed is `kept`), and running either again is a no-op.

## Second brains

Some projects' pages can live in a second notes brain. The instance reaches it the same two ways as the first: a separate local git copy
(kept in sync by another process; stackshot only reads it) and the gbrain CLI with its own home folder and sign-in. Each `[[brains]]` entry
in `stackshot.toml` names one (`stackshot.example.toml` documents the keys). `brains.py` resolves a page key to its brain: the first entry
whose `slugs` list the key (or a page above it) or whose `prefixes` it starts with; every other key is in the default brain, `paths.brain`.

**Where a new page starts.** `slugs` and `prefixes` name pages that already live in a second brain, so a page created later under a similar
name would start in the default brain. Two optional keys on an entry say where an absent page starts:

- `new_prefixes = ["projects/beta-"]`: plain prefixes of a page key (end each in `-` or `/`). A key that starts with one, matches no `slugs`
  or `prefixes` entry, and exists in no brain's local copy (neither the page nor a page it is a sub-page of) is routed to this entry's brain,
  so the page is created there and every later read and write on the server box finds it there. The first entry whose `new_prefixes` match wins.
  Routing of a new page depends on the local copies being present and current: a page created in the database but not yet in the copy is
  treated as absent, and when the default brain's copy is unusable (missing, no `.git`, no page in it) every page stays with the default brain.
  A client box gets only `slugs` and `prefixes` (it has no copy to tell an existing page from a new one), so it links a page created by
  `new_prefixes` to the default mirror unless its slug is in `slugs`.
- `keep = ["beta-old"]`: fragments; a page key that contains one of them stays in the default brain, even when a `new_prefixes` entry
  matches. `keep` wins over `new_prefixes` and has no effect without it.

A page that already exists is routed exactly as before: `new_prefixes` never moves a page, so a page of the default brain that matches a
prefix stays where it is. A bad value (not a list of strings, an empty or non-string item, a `new_prefixes` entry without a `/` or not
ending in `-` or `/`) is named by `config.notes` at the start and on a reload and is ignored.

- **Reads** use that brain's copy: the page registry (`pages.py registry` reads each brain's copy for the keys that brain holds), create's
  page check, the page hook's freshness and Current-state checks, pages owed, the audit's page call's shadow read, the search index, and the
  page links (the brain's `mirror_url`, else the file in its copy).
- **Writes** run the gbrain CLI with `GBRAIN_HOME=<home>` of the slug's brain (`rename_page.gb` reads the slug from the call), whatever
  `[pages] flavor` says for the default brain: a second brain is written through gbrain only.
- **Fail open.** A second brain whose copy is missing, is no usable copy (no `.git`, or no page in any page folder: an unmounted folder, a
  clone still being checked out) or is not synced for `stale_hours`, or whose CLI call fails, never blocks the board: create's page check and
  the page hook accept the item and log a warning naming the brain; pages owed and the audit's page call skip that brain's pages for the run
  (their debts are kept, not settled or charged); the registry keeps that brain's pages as it last read them (a stale copy is still read,
  with a warning). The default brain keeps its checks exactly as before.
- **One warning per outage, where a process lives long.** The server says each of these once until the brain reads again. The page hook
  starts a new process for every prompt, so it has no memory of the last one and repeats its line (on stderr, and in the prompt's nag line)
  at each prompt while the outage lasts.
- **The audit's page call retries.** When the CLI read of a second-brain page fails, that page gets no mark for the run, so the next audit run's pass tries again; the log keeps one line per outage.
- **A brain with no `home` is not reached through the CLI.** The gbrain CLI would run with the default home: a read would return the default
  brain's page for that slug and a write would put the page into the default brain. So a CLI call for that brain's slugs (a read, a put, a
  timeline line, a rename) is refused with a line saying so, and `config.notes` names the entry. A refused read is a page that could not be
  read: the audit's page call skips it and says so once per outage, naming the brain.
- **A remote brain's client secret: `secret_file`.** A remote brain's CLI also needs its OAuth client secret (it fails with "No client_secret
  available" on `GBRAIN_HOME` alone). An entry may name `secret_file`, a path (`~` allowed) to a JSON file with a `client_secret` key, or a plain
  file whose first line is the secret. The server reads it at each CLI call for that brain and sets `GBRAIN_REMOTE_CLIENT_SECRET` in that
  child's environment only: never in its own environment, a log, a note, an error message (an exact copy of the value in a CLI's output is removed) or the client
  config (`/client` serves the brain's name, mirror and slug rules, never this key). Keep the file mode 600: one that group or others can
  read (mode & 0o077) is not used, and `config.notes` (doctor, the start) says so, naming the path. A secret the server never takes from its own
  environment: the variable is removed from every child first and set only from the brain's file. A file that cannot be read, is empty, has
  no `client_secret`, or holds an unusable one (shorter than 16 characters, or with a control character) refuses the CLI calls for that brain with a line naming the path and the reason, said once per outage; the board carries
  on as with any other outage of that brain.
- **Prefixes end in `-` or `/`.** A prefix such as `projects/beta` also takes `projects/betamax`; `config.notes` warns about one that does not
  end in a separator, and about a repeated brain name.
- **Client boxes.** `stackshot client install --url` copies each brain's name, mirror link and slug rules into the client's config (marked
  `remote = true`: no copy and no home on that box), so its page links go to the right mirror. A stub left by a page rename links to the new
  page's own brain mirror, and says so when that brain has none.
- With no `[[brains]]` nothing changes: every page is read from `paths.brain` and written with the CLI's default home.

## A typical deployment

A typical instance runs on one Linux server box that hosts the server, the publisher, anchor-hub and most Claude Code panes,
with further client machines (Linux or Windows). Its page store can be a gbrain brain whose pages are mirrored to a GitHub
repository; `paths.brain` points at the mirror's checkout and `gbrain.mirror_url` at GitHub. A tunnel or reverse proxy carries
the public name, with an access gate in front of the board. sala, a separate tool, can operate the fleet of sessions (spawning,
relaunching, the CLI updates) and is named as the fleet operator; it plugs in through the keys of [The fleet tool](#the-fleet-tool).
An MCP portal is a second connector path to the same tools, through the portal's machine token.

## Development

How a change is made, tested and released is in [CONTRIBUTING.md](CONTRIBUTING.md): a branch in its own worktree, a pull
request against `main`, and `release.sh` on the server box, which merges and runs `deploy.sh`. The test suites are
`tests/*_scenario.py` on fixture data, run with `tests/run-suite <name>` and on every pull request
(`.github/workflows/tests.yml`). While `release.sh` waits for a PR's checks or for main's run, each cycle first asks sala's CI-run poller (`salamander ci-runs howardpark-ai/stackshot --sha <sha> --max-age 120`, a file read): a fresh file that lists a run of the commit still queued or in progress makes the cycle wait with no GitHub call; a completed run, a commit the file does not list, a missing, stale or unreadable file, or no `salamander` on PATH sends it to the GitHub read it always made, which alone decides pass or fail (`RELEASE_CIRUNS_MAX_AGE`, 0 = never ask the poller). The PR-checks wait looks every `RELEASE_POLL_PR` (60 s) and shares its read between releases for `RELEASE_GH_TTL` (45 s), like main's wait. A PR that is behind main is brought up to date and tested again, unless no test group reads both a file the PR changed and a file main's new commits changed (`tools/retest_skip.py` reads the suite map that main's measured run publishes as the artifact `suitemap`): then `release.sh` merges the PR with the checks that already passed, deploys at the merge, and says so in its log and report, naming the two file sets; main's full run after the deploy still checks everything and rolls back as before. The map's read sets are those of its commit, so files changed since the map's commit count as main's side too, and a diff on either side that adds or changes an import, a call that loads, runs or reads a file, or a string that looks like a repository path retests. A missing, stale or unreadable map (or one published by a run of another commit), an unknown file, a file changed by both sides, or a change to the CI workflow, `release.sh`, `deploy.sh`, `tests/run-suite`, `tests/pyproject.toml`, `tests/uv.lock`, `tests/suitemap.py`, any `*.lock` file, the `# /// script` block of a Python file or the tool-text, clean-text and snapshot checks' own files retests as it always did (`RELEASE_SKIP_DISJOINT=0` turns the skip off). A change to what a tool takes, says or returns brings its text with it
(`tests/tool_text.snapshot.json`, refreshed by `python3 tools/tool_text.py update`; the gates, among them the reviewer's read before the merge, for which `tools/text_check.py pack` gathers the text and the diff by hand (no CI job calls a model), are in CONTRIBUTING.md, *The tool-text gates*).
**What the running sessions need after a release** is picked by `release.sh` from the files the merged PR changed, as `deploy.sh` picks the restarts:
**nothing** (server-only code, docs, tests, a script run fresh at each call such as a per-call hook or `client.py`), a **notice** (a rule or wording sessions
should know about but need not reload: the tools' text, the write rules the server enforces, the audit's rubric; `release.sh` runs
`[fleet] notice_cmd` with one argument appended ("<title> — <summary>"), and with none set says so in the log and goes on), or a **recycle** (a file a session
loads once, at its start, such as the SessionStart hook: the open items anchored to the PR wait for a recycle, else `[fleet] recycle_when_idle` is asked).
The highest need wins, except that a recycle with a notice among the files does both ("recycle + notice"). `--recycle` forces a recycle, `--no-recycle` forces nothing. The log and the one report say what was picked and from which files.
The map is `session_need` in `release.sh`; the table, with the code that shows how a session reads each file, is [docs/session-reload.md](docs/session-reload.md).
`release.sh --pick <pr>` and `release.sh --pick-files <file>...` print the pick and do nothing else.
Several PRs are released through the release queue (`release.sh queue N [N...]`: each release starts the next at its deploy), and main's measured run is split into six shards: both are in CONTRIBUTING.md (*Several PRs go in the release queue*, *Main's measured run is split across the pool*).
salamander's merge queue (`salamander mq`) is switched per host by `~/.config/mergequeue/<owner>__<repo>.toml`, `mode = "off" | "shadow" | "on"`, rules in `.github/mergequeue.toml`: no file or `off` leaves `release.sh` as it is, `shadow` also queues each released PR next to the old path (the queue merges nothing) and logs in the release log where the two disagree, `on` (which also needs `deploy_lock = "<state>/releases/deploy.lock"` in the host file) releases a lone queued PR by the old path and two or more (at most 4, release.sh's own number; the rules file runs the queue as a chain, each PR tested stacked on the ones ahead, all at once) together through the queue, deployed once, with whatever merged deployed even when the rest of the group fails (CONTRIBUTING.md, *The merge queue, shadow*).
For a local server without sign-in:
`STACKSHOT_AUTH=off uv run --script server.py`, then `curl localhost:8020/healthz`.

The code font is D2Coding (Naver, SIL Open Font License 1.1), vendored in `fonts/` and served by the server itself at `/fonts/D2Coding-Regular.woff2`
(public, versioned by `?v=`, kept a year); `fonts/README.md` has its source, the conversion and how to replace it.
